Question bank · English · v02

346 Interview Questions for a Senior React Developer

The questions that come up most often in senior React interviews, grouped by block, each with a plain-English explanation and a ready-to-say answer. Every question is tagged with how often it actually shows up.

Before you start

How to use this bank

Three hundred and forty-six questions is too much to cram in one sitting, and you don't need to. Filter by frequency with the buttons on the left: the near-certain ones are the core of any senior loop and cover most of what you'll actually be asked. If you have one evening, that's where the return is; with two, add the very common ones and you're past the majority of the bank. The extended topics — Next.js internals, animation, i18n, styling architecture, mobile crossover, AI-assisted development, design collaboration, and compliance — cover ground that comes up less often per interview but is a real differentiator once the core is solid. Common ecosystem libraries are woven into the block where they're most relevant rather than isolated in their own section, the closing blocks move past pure technical knowledge into what it actually takes to run something in production, one block covers the identity protocols behind authentication, and the final two cover browser platform capabilities — WebSockets, service workers, and the full range of client-side storage — that most apps touch only occasionally but need to be understood correctly when they do.

LevelWhat it meansHow to treat it
●●●●Near-certainShows up in almost every senior React processAutomatic answer, no hesitation. Stumbling here costs you the offer.
●●●○Very commonFrequent, especially in the deep-dive technical roundYou need to explain it and back it with your own example.
●●○○CommonComes up often, depending on the interviewerEnough to recognize it and not freeze.
●○○○OccasionalSpecific to the company, domain, or interviewerBonus. Answering it well sets you apart.
Three rules for using this well

Cover the answer first. Read the question, answer it out loud, and only then compare. Reading answers back to back creates the illusion of knowing; recalling from memory is what actually sticks.

The "say it like this" line isn't filler, it's a script. It's written the way you'd actually say it live: the answer first, the nuance after. Rehearse it out loud until it comes out without reading.

Anchor every answer to something real. The gap between mid-level and senior isn't the definition, it's "I hit this in production and here's how I fixed it." Add your own example to the twenty most frequent questions for your stack.

Follow the link when something feels thin. Many answers end with a Doc line pointing to the official reference — usually the React docs or MDN. What's here is enough to answer the question; the link is for when the interviewer keeps digging.

On frequency: this is an estimate based on the pattern of senior React interviews across product companies and remote roles, not a measurement. Use it to prioritize your review, not as a guarantee that something won't come up.

Block 1

JavaScript & TypeScript essentials

The block that opens almost every React interview, even before React comes up. You don't fail this by not knowing the definitions — you fail it by not being able to explain why the behavior happens. Fourteen questions.

001●●●●Near-certainClosures

What is a closure, and where does it bite people in React specifically?

A closure is a function bundled with the lexical scope it was created in — it keeps access to variables from its enclosing scope even after that scope has finished executing. Every callback, event handler, and effect function in React is a closure over the render it was created in.

In React this shows up as the stale closure problem: a callback captured in one render "remembers" the props and state values from that render, not the latest ones, unless you explicitly give it a way to see fresh values (a ref, a functional state update, or a correctly specified dependency array).

function useStaleCounter() {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);
  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setInterval(() => {
      // closes over count from the render that registered the effect
      console.log(count);
    }, 1000);
    return () => clearInterval(id);
  }, []); // empty deps: this closure is frozen forever
}
Say it like this

"A closure captures the scope where a function was defined, not where it's called. In React that turns into the stale closure bug — a callback from render N keeps seeing render N's state. I fix it either by adding the right dependency, using the functional setState form, or reaching for a ref when I deliberately want the latest value without re-subscribing."

DocClosures — MDN·Separating events from Effects — react.dev

002●●●●Near-certainEquality

Why does {} === {} return false, and why does that matter for React?

Objects, arrays, and functions are compared by reference in JavaScript, not by structural content. Two object literals with identical contents are still two different allocations, so === returns false.

React's whole rendering model leans on this: hooks like useEffect, useMemo, and React.memo compare dependencies and props with Object.is (essentially ===). Passing a freshly created object or inline function as a prop or dependency means it's "different" on every render even if nothing meaningful changed, which defeats memoization and can retrigger effects.

Say it like this

"Reference equality is why React's optimizations are opt-in rather than automatic — memo and useMemo can only skip work if the reference is stable. So I'm careful not to create new objects, arrays, or arrow functions inline where they'll be compared on every render; I hoist them out, memoize them, or restructure the dependency instead."

DocEquality comparisons — MDN

003●●●●Near-certainEvent loop

Explain the event loop: why does a setTimeout(fn, 0) run after a synchronous console.log?

JavaScript runs on a single call stack. Synchronous code always finishes emptying the stack first. Callbacks from timers, I/O, and DOM events are queued and only pulled onto the stack once it's empty — setTimeout(fn, 0) still has to wait its turn in the macrotask queue.

Promises use a separate microtask queue that's drained completely after each synchronous block, before the next macrotask runs. That's why a resolved Promise.then always fires before a setTimeout(0), even though both were scheduled at the same instant.

Say it like this

"Synchronous code always runs to completion first. Then the microtask queue — promises — drains fully, and only after that does the engine pull one task off the macrotask queue, which is where timers and events live. That ordering is why a resolved promise beats a setTimeout(0) every time, and it's something I keep in mind when I'm reasoning about update batching in React too."

DocThe event loop — MDN

004●●●○Very commonPromises

What's the difference between Promise.all, Promise.allSettled, and Promise.race?

Promise.all resolves when every promise resolves, but rejects as soon as any one rejects, discarding the results of the others. Promise.allSettled always resolves once every promise has finished, returning an array of {status, value|reason} objects — nothing short-circuits. Promise.race settles with whichever promise finishes first, success or failure.

The practical rule: use all when every request must succeed for the result to make sense; use allSettled when partial failure is fine and you want to report on each independently, like loading several independent dashboard widgets.

Say it like this

"Promise.all is all-or-nothing — one rejection kills the whole batch. allSettled always gives you a result per promise, success or failure, which is what I reach for when the UI has independent widgets that shouldn't take each other down. race is mostly useful for timeouts, racing a fetch against a timer promise."

DocPromise.allSettled — MDN

005●●●○Very commonThis binding

How does this get resolved in a regular function versus an arrow function?

A regular function's this is determined by how it's called — as a method, it's the object before the dot; standalone, it's undefined in strict mode. An arrow function has no this of its own: it captures this lexically from the enclosing scope at the moment it's defined, like a closure would.

This is exactly why class components historically needed this.method = this.method.bind(this) in the constructor, or arrow-function class fields — passing a regular method as a callback loses its binding to the instance.

Say it like this

"Regular functions get this from the call site, arrow functions inherit it lexically from where they were defined. That's the whole reason class components needed manual binding for event handlers — an arrow function class field sidesteps it entirely, which is one more reason hooks with plain closures feel simpler than classes did."

Docthis — MDN

006●●●○Very commonTypeScript

In TypeScript, when do you use an interface versus a type alias for component props?

Functionally the two overlap heavily for object shapes: both can describe props. The practical differences are that interface supports declaration merging (re-opening the same interface adds to it) and reads slightly more naturally with extends, while type is required for unions, tuples, and mapped/conditional types — things an interface can't express.

For component props specifically, most teams default to type because props often need unions (e.g. a variant: 'primary' | 'secondary'), and consistency matters more than the marginal difference.

Say it like this

"They're close enough that the choice is mostly a style-guide decision. I lean on type for props because I frequently need a union in there — a variant or size prop — and type handles that directly, whereas interface can't express a union at the top level. Interface earns its keep when I want declaration merging or a clean extends chain."

DocType aliases vs interfaces — TypeScript Handbook

007●●●○Very commonTypeScript

What's the difference between unknown and any, and why prefer unknown?

any disables type checking entirely for that value — you can call anything on it, assign it anywhere, and TypeScript stays silent. unknown is type-safe: you can hold anything in it, but you must narrow it (a type guard, an instanceof check, a schema parse) before you're allowed to use it as anything specific.

In practice, any is how bugs sneak past the compiler; unknown forces you to prove the shape before touching the value, which is exactly the discipline you want at boundaries like API responses or catch blocks.

Say it like this

"any opts a value out of type checking completely, unknown keeps it type-safe but forces me to narrow before I use it. I use unknown at the edges of the system — API responses, catch blocks, JSON.parse results — and narrow with a type guard or a runtime validator like zod before the data flows further into the app."

Docunknown type — TypeScript Handbook

008●●○○CommonTypeScript

What does TypeScript's structural typing mean, and how does it differ from nominal typing?

TypeScript compares types by shape, not by name: a value satisfies a type if it has the required members, regardless of whether it was ever declared as that type. Two unrelated interfaces with identical members are interchangeable. Nominal typing (like in C# or Java classes) instead cares about the declared type name/identity, not just the shape.

The practical consequence: a function typed to take { id: string } will happily accept any object literal — or a User, a Product, anything — as long as it has an id string. This makes TypeScript flexible with plain objects but means "type" and "runtime guarantee" aren't the same thing.

Say it like this

"TypeScript checks shape, not name — if it has the right members it satisfies the type, even if it was never declared as that type. It's great for working with plain objects and props, but it also means a type annotation is a compile-time promise, not a runtime guarantee, which is why I still validate data coming from outside the app."

DocType Compatibility — TypeScript Handbook

009●●●○Very commonIteration

Why is mutating an array with push or splice a problem in React state, and what do you do instead?

React decides whether to re-render by comparing the reference of the new state to the old one. array.push(x) mutates the array in place and returns the same reference, so React sees "no change" and skips the re-render even though the data changed underneath it.

The fix is to always produce a new array or object: spread syntax ([...arr, x]), array methods that return new arrays (map, filter, concat), or an immutability helper. The same rule applies to nested objects — a shallow spread doesn't help if you mutate something two levels deep.

// wrong — same reference, no re-render, and it corrupts the previous state snapshot
items.push(newItem);
setItems(items);

// right — new reference, React detects the change
setItems(prev => [...prev, newItem]);
Say it like this

"React's state updates rely on reference comparison, so mutating in place produces the same reference and no re-render — and it also corrupts whatever the previous render's closure was holding. I always treat state as immutable: spread, map, filter, whatever produces a new reference, and for deep updates I either spread each level or reach for something like Immer if the nesting gets ugly."

DocUpdating arrays in state — react.dev

010●●○○CommonModules

What's the difference between a default export and a named export, and when do you pick one over the other?

A default export is a single, unnamed value per module — the importer chooses whatever local name they want. A named export exposes specific bindings by name, and the importer must (mostly) use that name, though multiple named exports can live in one file.

Named exports are generally preferred in larger codebases: they're easier to refactor (rename in one place, tooling updates every import), support better auto-import and tree-shaking analysis, and prevent the "same component, five different local names across the codebase" problem. Default exports still make sense for a file whose entire purpose is one thing, like a page component.

Say it like this

"Named exports are my default because renames and auto-imports are more reliable, and it avoids a component getting a different local name in every file that imports it. I'll use a default export for something like a Next.js page file, where the file's whole job is exporting exactly one thing and the framework expects that convention."

DocJavaScript modules — MDN

011●●●○Very commonDebounce/throttle

What's the difference between debouncing and throttling, and give a UI example of each.

Debouncing waits for a pause in activity: it delays the call until no new event has fired for a set window, and resets the timer on every new event. If events keep coming, the function never runs until they stop. Throttling guarantees the function runs at most once per fixed interval, regardless of how many events fire in between — it doesn't wait for silence.

Debounce fits a search-as-you-type input: you only want to fire the request once the user pauses typing. Throttle fits a scroll handler updating a "reading progress" bar: you want steady updates while scrolling, not one huge delayed update after the user stops.

Say it like this

"Debounce collapses a burst of events into one call after things go quiet — that's my search box, so I don't fire a request on every keystroke. Throttle caps the rate but still fires regularly during continuous activity — that's my scroll or resize handler, where I want updates the whole time, just not on every single pixel."

Docscroll event — MDN

012●●○○CommonPrototype

Briefly, how does prototypal inheritance work in JavaScript?

Every object has an internal link ([[Prototype]], exposed as __proto__ or via Object.getPrototypeOf) to another object. When you access a property, the engine looks on the object itself first, and if it's not there, walks up the prototype chain until it finds it or hits null.

class syntax is sugar over this same mechanism — methods defined in a class body live on the prototype, not on each instance, so they're shared rather than duplicated per object.

Say it like this

"Objects link to a prototype, and property lookup walks that chain until it finds a match. Classes are syntax over the same mechanism — instance methods live once on the prototype and get shared, not copied per instance. It's not something I reach for daily with hooks and functional components, but it explains why methods on a class instance are all identical references."

DocInheritance and the prototype chain — MDN

013●●○○CommonGenerics

Write a generic useLocalStorage hook signature and explain what the generic buys you.

A generic lets the hook stay reusable for any stored shape while still giving the caller full type safety, instead of returning any or forcing a cast at every call site.

function useLocalStorage<T>(key: string, initialValue: T): [T, (value: T) => void] {
  // implementation reads/writes JSON.stringify(value) under `key`
}

// caller gets full inference — no `any`, no manual cast
const [user, setUser] = useLocalStorage<User>("user", defaultUser);
Say it like this

"The generic parameter T lets one hook implementation serve any stored type while the caller still gets full autocomplete and type checking on what comes back — no any, no casting at the call site. It's the same reason useState itself is generic under the hood."

DocGenerics — TypeScript Handbook

014●○○○OccasionalSymbols

What is a Symbol used for, and where might you have seen one without realizing it?

A Symbol is a primitive that's guaranteed unique — no two symbols are ever equal, even with the same description. That uniqueness makes them useful as object keys that can't accidentally collide with a string key someone else adds later.

You've almost certainly used one already: Symbol.iterator is what makes an object usable in a for...of loop or spread syntax — arrays, strings, Map, and Set all implement it, and it's how you'd make a custom class iterable too.

Say it like this

"A Symbol is a guaranteed-unique primitive, mostly useful as a collision-proof object key. The one I actually interact with is Symbol.iterator — it's the protocol behind for...of and spread, and implementing it on a custom class is how you'd make your own data structure work with those same constructs."

DocSymbol — MDN

TypeScript deep-dive

A shop that's serious about TypeScript will push past "you know what an interface is" — these check whether you can actually use the type system to prevent bugs, not just annotate code after the fact.

015●●○○CommonConditional types

What does a conditional type like T extends U ? X : Y actually let you express that a simple union or interface can't?

A conditional type lets a type's shape depend on another type, evaluated at the type level rather than at runtime — similar in spirit to a ternary, but operating on types instead of values. This lets you express relationships like "the return type of this function depends on what type was passed in," which no static interface or union alone can capture, since those describe a fixed shape rather than a shape that varies based on an input type.

A common practical use is narrowing a generic utility's return type based on its input — for example, a function that returns a Promise<T> if given an async function type and just T otherwise, expressed entirely in the type system so callers get the correctly narrowed type without any runtime type-check needed.

type UnwrapPromise<T> = T extends Promise<infer U> ? U : T;

type A = UnwrapPromise<Promise<string>>; // string
type B = UnwrapPromise<number>;          // number — falls through to the else branch
Say it like this

"It lets a type's shape depend on another type, evaluated at the type level — a ternary for types. I reach for it when a generic utility's return type genuinely needs to vary based on what's passed in, like unwrapping a Promise type if one was given and passing the type through unchanged otherwise, all resolved statically so callers get the correct narrowed type with zero runtime check needed."

DocConditional Types — TypeScript Handbook

016●●○○Commoninfer keyword

What does the infer keyword actually do inside a conditional type?

infer introduces a new type variable that TypeScript solves for by pattern-matching against the structure being checked — inside a conditional type's extends clause, it lets you say "if this type matches this general shape, capture whatever type is in this specific position and give me a name for it," rather than only being able to check a fixed, fully-specified shape.

Without infer, a conditional type could only ask a yes/no question ("does T extend this exact type") — infer is what turns it into genuine destructuring at the type level, letting you extract a function's return type, an array's element type, or a Promise's resolved type without knowing that type in advance.

type ReturnTypeOf<T> = T extends (...args: any[]) => infer R ? R : never;

type R = ReturnTypeOf<() => string>; // string, extracted via infer, not hardcoded
Say it like this

"infer introduces a type variable that gets solved by pattern-matching against the shape you're checking — it's what turns a conditional type from a yes/no check into genuine destructuring at the type level. It's how you extract a function's return type or a Promise's resolved type without knowing that type ahead of time, by matching the general shape and capturing whatever's in the specific position you care about."

DocInferring within conditional types — TypeScript Handbook

017●●○○CommonMapped types

Write a DeepPartial<T> type that makes every property optional recursively, not just at the top level like the built-in Partial<T>.

The built-in Partial<T> is a mapped type that only makes the top-level keys optional — a nested object's properties are still all required. A DeepPartial needs to recursively apply the same optional-ification to any property that's itself an object, which means combining a mapped type with a conditional check on whether each property's type is an object, recursing into it if so.

type DeepPartial<T> = T extends object
  ? { [K in keyof T]?: DeepPartial<T[K]> }
  : T;

interface User { name: string; address: { city: string; zip: string }; }
type PartialUser = DeepPartial<User>;
// { name?: string; address?: { city?: string; zip?: string } } — nested, not just top-level
Say it like this

"Partial only optionalizes the top level, so a nested object's properties are still all required. DeepPartial combines a mapped type with a conditional check — if a property is itself an object, recurse into it with DeepPartial again instead of just marking it optional as a whole; if it's not an object, leave it as the base case. It's a good example of composing mapped and conditional types together to get real recursive behavior out of the type system."

DocMapped Types — TypeScript Handbook

018●●○○Commonsatisfies

What does the satisfies operator do that a plain type annotation doesn't?

A plain type annotation (const config: Config = {"{"}...{"}"}) widens the variable's inferred type to exactly the annotation — you lose access to the more specific literal types TypeScript would otherwise have inferred from the actual object literal, which matters if you need that specific inference downstream (autocomplete on exact string literal values, for instance). satisfies instead checks that the value is compatible with a type without changing what gets inferred — the variable keeps its most specific inferred type, while still getting a compile error if it doesn't actually satisfy the constraint.

const config = {
  env: "production",
} satisfies Record<string, string>;

// config.env is inferred as the literal type "production", not widened to string
// but TypeScript still checked that the object satisfies Record<string, string>
Say it like this

"A plain annotation widens the variable to exactly that type, so you lose the more specific literal inference TypeScript would otherwise give you. satisfies checks compatibility with a type without changing what actually gets inferred — the variable keeps its most specific type, so I still get precise autocomplete and literal types downstream, while still catching a compile error if the value doesn't actually match the constraint."

DocThe satisfies Operator — TypeScript 4.9 release notes

019●○○○OccasionalModule augmentation

A third-party library's type definitions are missing a field that actually exists at runtime. How do you fix this without forking the library or scattering as any casts everywhere?

Declaration merging lets you augment an existing module's exported types by re-declaring the same interface name inside a declare module block pointing at that library's module path — TypeScript merges your additional properties into the existing interface rather than replacing it, so the rest of the library's real type definitions stay intact and you only add what was actually missing.

// types/some-library.d.ts
import 'some-library';

declare module 'some-library' {
  interface Config {
    experimentalFeatureFlag?: boolean; // field the library's real types are missing
  }
}
Say it like this

"I use declaration merging — a declare module block re-declaring the same interface name from the library's module path, which TypeScript merges into the existing interface rather than replacing it. That fixes the actual type definition in one place, correctly, instead of scattering as-any casts everywhere a component happens to touch that missing field, and it doesn't require forking or patching the library itself."

DocDeclaration Merging — TypeScript Handbook

020●○○○OccasionalTemplate literal types

What can a template literal type express that a plain string union can't?

A plain string union ('red' | 'green' | 'blue') has to enumerate every valid value explicitly. A template literal type builds string types compositionally, similar to a JS template literal but operating on types — it can express a whole pattern of valid strings without listing each one, especially useful when combined with other unions to produce a combinatorial set of valid strings automatically.

type Size = 'sm' | 'md' | 'lg';
type Variant = 'primary' | 'secondary';

type ClassName = `btn-${Variant}-${Size}`;
// automatically expands to all 6 combinations: 'btn-primary-sm' | 'btn-primary-md' | ... 
// without hand-listing every one
Say it like this

"A plain string union has to enumerate every valid value by hand. A template literal type builds string types compositionally, so combining two unions inside a template literal automatically expands to every combination without me hand-listing each one — useful for something like generating all valid CSS class name combinations from a variant and a size union, and it stays correct automatically if either union grows later."

DocTemplate Literal Types — TypeScript Handbook

021●○○○OccasionalType-only imports

What's the point of import type {"{"} Foo {"}"} from './foo' instead of a regular import, given that TypeScript already erases types at compile time?

A regular import of something that's only ever used as a type still leaves an import statement in the compiled JS output in some build configurations, unless the bundler's own dead-code elimination is smart enough to detect and strip it — which isn't always guaranteed, especially with certain transpiler setups that process files in isolation without full type information (like using Babel for the JS transform instead of the TypeScript compiler itself). import type makes it explicit and guaranteed that the import is types-only and should be fully erased at compile time, with zero runtime import remaining, regardless of what the bundler's own analysis could or couldn't infer.

This also has a real, if narrow, correctness benefit: it prevents circular-import or module-side-effect issues that can occur when an import that was only ever meant for typing purposes accidentally triggers a real runtime import of a module with side effects.

Say it like this

"A regular import of something only used as a type can still leave a real import in the compiled output, depending on the build setup — especially with transpilers that process files in isolation without full type information. import type makes it explicit and guaranteed that it's erased entirely at compile time, with zero runtime import, rather than relying on the bundler's own analysis to figure that out. It also avoids some circular-import and module-side-effect edge cases that a type-only import accidentally triggering a real import can cause."

DocType-Only Imports and Export — TypeScript 3.8 release notes

022●●○○CommonYup vs Zod

Given both Yup and Zod solve runtime schema validation, what actually differs enough to justify picking one over the other on a TypeScript project?

Both validate data at runtime and can validate the same kinds of shapes, but they differ in how they relate to TypeScript's type system. Zod is TypeScript-first: you define a schema once and infer the static type directly from it (z.infer<typeof schema>), so the schema is the single source of truth and the TypeScript type can never silently drift out of sync with the validation logic. Yup predates widespread TypeScript-first design in this space and historically required maintaining a separate TypeScript interface alongside the schema, with type inference support added later but generally considered less complete and more prone to edge cases than Zod's inference.

The practical decision usually comes down to how central TypeScript is to the project: a TypeScript-heavy codebase benefits more from Zod's schema-as-single-source-of-truth model, while a codebase with less TypeScript investment, or one already using Yup with React Hook Form/Formik's established integration patterns, may not see enough benefit to justify a migration. Neither is objectively wrong — this is a case where existing project context matters more than an abstract "better" answer.

Say it like this

"The core difference is how tightly each integrates with TypeScript's type system — Zod lets you infer the static type directly from the schema, so there's one source of truth that can't drift, while Yup historically needed a separately maintained interface alongside the schema, with inference support added later but generally less complete. For a TypeScript-heavy project I'd lean Zod for that single-source-of-truth property; for a project already invested in Yup with established form-library integration, I wouldn't necessarily migrate just for that reason alone."

DocZod — Documentation

023●○○○OccasionalLodash

Modern JS natively supports a lot of what Lodash used to be essential for (spread, Array.flat, optional chaining). When is reaching for Lodash still actually justified?

A meaningful chunk of Lodash's original value proposition has genuinely been absorbed into the language itself — deep cloning simple structures, flattening arrays, checking for empty values, and basic object manipulation are mostly covered natively now, and reaching for Lodash for those specific cases just adds a dependency for something native JS already does.

What still justifies it: functions solving genuinely non-trivial problems the language doesn't provide a native equivalent for — a proper deep-equality comparison (isEqual, which correctly handles edge cases a naive JSON.stringify comparison gets wrong), debounce/throttle implementations (the language has no native equivalent, and hand-rolling a genuinely correct one has real edge cases), and safe deep property access on a shape that isn't guaranteed (get, though optional chaining covers much of this now for statically-known paths). The practical decision point is checking whether a specific utility is genuinely still filling a real gap versus being pulled in out of habit for something a native method already does cleanly — and if only one or two functions are actually used, importing them individually (lodash/debounce) rather than the whole library avoids the bundle cost of the rest.

Say it like this

"A lot of what Lodash used to be essential for is native now — deep clone, flatten, basic object checks — so reaching for it there just adds a dependency for something the language already does. What still earns its place is genuinely non-trivial logic with no native equivalent — a real deep-equality check that handles edge cases correctly, debounce and throttle, or safe deep access on uncertain shapes. And if I only need one or two functions, I import them individually rather than the whole library, so I'm not paying the bundle cost for everything else."

DocLodash Documentation

024●●●○Very commonCoercion

Why does '5' + 3 produce '53' while '5' - 3 produces 2, and what does that reveal about how JS coercion actually works?

+ is overloaded: if either operand is a string, JS coerces the other to a string and concatenates, because + has to decide between "add" and "concatenate" and string wins when one is present. Every other arithmetic operator (-, *, /) has no such overload — there's no "subtract as strings" meaning, so JS always coerces both operands to numbers first, which is why - gives the numeric result even with a string operand.

The broader lesson is that coercion isn't one uniform rule — it's operator-specific, and + is the one genuine special case people get bitten by, precisely because it's the one arithmetic-looking operator that also has a legitimate non-numeric meaning.

Say it like this

"Plus is special because it's genuinely overloaded — string concatenation and numeric addition — so if either side is a string, it concatenates. Every other arithmetic operator has no non-numeric meaning at all, so they always coerce both sides to numbers first. It's not that coercion is inconsistent across the board — it's that plus specifically has two legitimate meanings and picks based on operand type, which is exactly the operator people get bitten by."

DocType coercion — MDN

025●●●○Very commonLoose equality

Give a concrete example of where == produces a genuinely surprising result, and explain why senior teams generally ban it via lint rule rather than trust developers to use it carefully.

[] == false evaluates to true — the array coerces to a string (''), the empty string coerces to a number (0), and false coerces to 0, so the comparison ultimately becomes 0 === 0. Similarly, '' == 0, null == undefined (but null == 0 is false, an inconsistency even within loose equality's own rules), and ' \t\n' == 0 are all true — a chain of coercion rules that don't map to any intuition a reader would have without memorizing the specific algorithm.

The reason this gets banned by lint rule (eslint-config-airbnb and most strict configs enforce ===) rather than left to developer discipline is that the failure mode is silent — a == comparison that happens to coerce unexpectedly doesn't throw an error, it just quietly evaluates to the wrong boolean, and the bug surfaces as confusing downstream behavior far from the actual comparison, which is exactly the kind of mistake that's cheap to prevent categorically and expensive to debug after the fact.

Say it like this

"Something like [] == false being true is the classic one — the array coerces to an empty string, the empty string coerces to zero, false coerces to zero, so it collapses to zero equals zero, which nobody would guess without knowing the specific coercion chain. The reason it's banned by lint rule rather than left to discipline is that the failure is silent — it doesn't throw, it just quietly evaluates to the wrong boolean, and the bug shows up as confusing behavior far downstream from the actual comparison, which is cheap to prevent categorically and expensive to debug after it ships."

DocEquality (==) — MDN

026●●●○Very commonPromise concurrency

You fire off five independent fetch calls in a loop with await inside each iteration. Why is this usually a bug, and what's the fix?

awaiting inside a loop, one iteration at a time, means each fetch waits for the previous one to fully complete before starting — five genuinely independent requests execute strictly sequentially, taking roughly the sum of all five latencies, when they could have run concurrently and taken roughly the time of the single slowest one. This is a real, common performance bug specifically because the code looks correct and does produce the right result eventually — it's just needlessly slow, which doesn't throw an error or fail a test, so it tends to survive code review unless someone specifically checks for this pattern.

The fix is starting all the promises first (without awaiting each individually), then awaiting them together — Promise.all (or allSettled, per the earlier question, if partial failure should be tolerated) runs every fetch concurrently, cutting total time from the sum of latencies to roughly the maximum.

// sequential — bug: five round trips back to back, sum of all latencies
const results = [];
for (const id of ids) {
  results.push(await fetchUser(id));
}

// concurrent — correct: all five requests start immediately, max of the latencies
const results = await Promise.all(ids.map(id => fetchUser(id)));
Say it like this

"Awaiting inside a loop makes each request wait for the previous one to fully finish, so five independent requests run strictly sequentially and take the sum of all five latencies — and it's an easy bug to miss because the code still produces the correct result, it's just needlessly slow, which doesn't fail a test or throw. The fix is starting every promise first with map, then awaiting them together with Promise.all, which runs them concurrently and cuts total time down to roughly the slowest single request instead of the sum of all of them."

DocPromise.all — MDN

027●●○○CommonRace conditions

Two async functions both read a value, compute something based on it, then write the result back. Why can this produce a wrong final result even though JS is single-threaded, and how does this relate to the fetch race-condition pattern covered earlier?

Single-threaded doesn't mean non-concurrent — JS can still interleave multiple async operations, because each await is a genuine yield point where the engine can go run other pending work before coming back. If function A reads a value, then awaits something (yielding control), and function B runs in that gap, reads the same original value, computes, and writes back, then A resumes with its now-stale read and overwrites B's write — a classic read-modify-write race condition, entirely possible despite there being only one thread, because the interleaving happens at await boundaries, not from true parallel execution.

This is the exact same underlying mechanism as the stale-fetch-response race condition covered earlier — two operations that both take time and can interleave, where the one that "wins" isn't guaranteed to be the more recent one. The general fix pattern is the same too: either ensure operations that must not interleave are properly sequenced (awaiting the full read-modify-write as one atomic-feeling unit rather than letting other code run in between), or use a flag/token to detect and discard a stale operation's result, the same ignore-flag or AbortController approach from the data-fetching section.

Say it like this

"Single-threaded doesn't mean non-concurrent — every await is a real yield point where other pending work can run before control comes back. So a read-modify-write split across two awaited steps can interleave with another async operation doing the same thing in the gap, and whichever one resumes and writes last wins, regardless of which one is actually more recent. It's the exact same underlying mechanism as the stale-fetch-response problem — the fix is the same too, either sequence the operations so they can't interleave, or use a flag to detect and discard a stale result."

DocUsing promises — MDN

028●●○○CommonRest and spread

Rest and spread use the same ... syntax. What actually distinguishes them, and where does each one apply?

They're opposite operations that happen to share syntax, distinguished entirely by context. Spread expands an iterable or object's own enumerable properties out into individual elements — used in a function call's arguments, an array literal, or an object literal, wherever you're constructing something from an existing collection's contents. Rest does the opposite: it collects multiple individual items back into a single array or object — used in a function's parameter list to gather all remaining arguments, or in a destructuring pattern to gather whatever wasn't explicitly picked out.

The rule of thumb for telling them apart in code: spread appears where a value is being constructed (the right side of an assignment, inside array/object literals, as call arguments), rest appears where a value is being destructured or bound (function parameters, destructuring patterns) — spread expands, rest collects, and which one you're looking at depends entirely on whether ... shows up in a construction context or a binding context.

// spread — expands an existing array into individual arguments
Math.max(...numbers);
const merged = { ...defaults, ...overrides }; // expands into a new object literal

// rest — collects remaining items into a single array/object
function log(first, ...rest) { /* rest is an array of everything after `first` */ }
const { id, ...otherFields } = user; // otherFields collects whatever id didn't take
Say it like this

"They're opposite operations sharing the same syntax, told apart entirely by context. Spread expands an existing collection out — into call arguments, an array literal, an object literal — wherever something's being constructed from existing contents. Rest collects individual items back into one array or object — in function parameters or a destructuring pattern, wherever something's being bound or picked apart. Construction context means spread, binding context means rest."

DocSpread syntax — MDN

029●●●○Very commonCurrency handling

Why does 0.1 + 0.2 not equal 0.3 in JavaScript, and what does that mean for how you should actually handle money in the UI?

JavaScript numbers are IEEE 754 double-precision floats, which represent most decimal fractions as an approximation, not an exact value — 0.1 and 0.2 each carry tiny rounding error in binary floating-point representation, and adding them produces 0.30000000000000004, not exactly 0.3. This isn't a JS-specific bug; it's the standard floating-point representation used by nearly every mainstream language, but it becomes a genuinely serious problem specifically for money, where "off by a fraction of a cent, repeated across millions of transactions" is a real, compounding correctness and legal problem, not a rounding curiosity.

The practical fix is never storing or computing money as a float at all: represent currency amounts as integer cents (or the smallest currency unit) rather than a decimal dollar amount, so all arithmetic is exact integer math with no floating-point error possible, and only convert to a displayed decimal string at the final formatting step using Intl.NumberFormat's currency mode (covered earlier for locale formatting) — never do the currency-aware division and rounding by hand. For genuinely complex financial calculations, a dedicated decimal/arbitrary-precision library (like decimal.js) is worth it over hand-rolled integer-cents math once the calculations get non-trivial (percentages, splits, compounding).

Say it like this

"It's IEEE 754 floating-point representation — 0.1 and 0.2 each carry tiny binary rounding error, so the sum isn't exactly 0.3. That's not a JS bug, it's standard float behavior, but for money specifically, tiny per-transaction errors compound into a real correctness problem, not just a rounding curiosity. The fix is never storing money as a float — I represent amounts as integer cents so all the math is exact integer arithmetic, and only format to a decimal string at display time with Intl.NumberFormat's currency mode, never hand-rolled division and rounding."

Doc0.30000000000000004.com

Block 2

React fundamentals & JSX

The core mental model interviewers check before anything else: do you actually understand what React is doing, or did you memorize the hooks API. Sixteen questions.

030●●●●Near-certainVirtual DOM

What is the virtual DOM, and why does it make updates faster?

The virtual DOM is a lightweight in-memory tree of plain JavaScript objects that mirrors the UI structure. On every state change, React builds a new virtual tree and diffs it against the previous one using its reconciliation algorithm, then computes the minimal set of real DOM mutations needed to match the new tree.

The speedup isn't that JS objects are inherently "faster" than the DOM — it's that direct DOM mutation is expensive when done naively (layout thrashing, reading and writing in an interleaved way), and the diff-then-batch-apply approach lets React group and minimize actual DOM writes instead of touching the DOM on every intermediate state change.

Say it like this

"It's an in-memory representation of the UI that React diffs against the previous version to compute the minimal real DOM changes, then applies them in a batch. The win isn't that the virtual DOM itself is fast — it's that it avoids naive, repeated, unbatched DOM writes, which are the actual expensive part."

DocVirtual DOM and Internals — React (legacy docs)

031●●●●Near-certainReconciliation

Why does React need a key prop on list items, and what goes wrong if you use the array index?

React's reconciler needs a stable identity per item to know whether an element in the new render is the same logical item that moved, or a genuinely new one. Without keys — or with the array index as a key — React falls back to matching by position, so if the list is reordered, filtered, or an item is inserted in the middle, React can misattribute state and DOM nodes to the wrong logical item.

Using the index works fine only when the list is static and never reorders or has items inserted/removed from the middle. Otherwise, a form input's local state or an animation can end up attached to the wrong row after a reorder, because React "sees" position 2 as the same element it was before, not the item that used to be there.

// risky if `items` can reorder or items can be removed from the middle
items.map((item, i) => <Row key={i} item={item} />);

// correct — identity survives reordering
items.map(item => <Row key={item.id} item={item} />);
Say it like this

"Keys give React a stable identity to track across renders, independent of position. If I use the index and the list can reorder, React ends up matching by slot instead of by item, and any local state or uncontrolled input tied to a row can bleed into the wrong row after a reorder. I use a stable id from the data whenever the list isn't strictly append-only and static."

DocRendering lists — react.dev

032●●●●Near-certainRendering

What actually triggers a re-render in React, and what does "re-render" mean in terms of what work happens?

A component re-renders when its own state changes, its parent re-renders (by default, children re-render too, regardless of whether their props changed), or a context value it consumes changes. Props changing by itself doesn't trigger anything directly — it's a symptom of the parent having re-rendered.

"Re-render" means React calls the component function again and computes a new element tree, then diffs it against the last committed tree. It does not automatically mean the real DOM gets touched — if the diff finds no meaningful differences, the commit phase does nothing to the actual DOM for that subtree.

Say it like this

"State changes, a parent re-rendering, or a consumed context value changing — those are the three triggers. Re-rendering just means the function runs again and produces a new element tree to diff; it doesn't automatically mean the DOM changes, which is the distinction people usually miss when they assume every re-render is expensive."

DocRender and commit — react.dev

033●●●○Very commonJSX

What does JSX actually compile down to?

JSX is syntactic sugar. <div className="a">{text}</div> compiles to a function call — historically React.createElement('div', {className: 'a'}, text), and with the modern automatic JSX runtime, to jsx('div', {className: 'a', children: text}) imported automatically from react/jsx-runtime. Either way it produces a plain JS object describing the element, not a DOM node.

Knowing this explains a lot of "gotchas": why you need React in scope with the classic runtime, why conditionals and loops need JS expressions instead of JSX control-flow tags, and why a component is really just a function returning one of these description objects.

Say it like this

"JSX compiles to function calls that build a plain object describing the UI — an element, not a DOM node. That's why you can't write if statements directly inside JSX, only expressions, and it's why a component is really just a function that returns one of these objects for React to reconcile."

DocWriting markup with JSX — react.dev

034●●●○Very commonControlled inputs

What's the difference between a controlled and an uncontrolled input in React?

A controlled input's value is driven entirely by React state — value={state} plus an onChange that updates that state — so the DOM value and the React state are always kept in sync, with React as the single source of truth. An uncontrolled input manages its own value internally in the DOM; React reads it on demand via a ref instead of tracking every keystroke.

Controlled is the default for anything you need to validate live, format, or keep in sync with other UI. Uncontrolled is lighter-weight and fine for simple forms where you only care about the value on submit, or for integrating with non-React code that expects to own the DOM node.

Say it like this

"Controlled means React state is the source of truth and the input just reflects it; uncontrolled means the DOM owns the value and I read it with a ref when I need it. I default to controlled when I need live validation or the value drives other UI, and I'll use uncontrolled for a simple form or a field that's just too high-frequency to justify a state update per keystroke."

DocControlling an input — react.dev

035●●●○Very commonFragments

Why can't a component return two sibling elements directly, and what problem do fragments solve?

A component's render output has to be a single tree with one root, because JSX compiles to one function call producing one element (or null). Wrapping two siblings in an actual <div> works but adds a real DOM node purely to satisfy this rule, which can break CSS assumptions (flex/grid children, semantic HTML like a table needing direct <tr> children).

<>...</> (or explicit React.Fragment, needed when you must pass a key) groups children into a single return value without adding any node to the actual DOM.

Say it like this

"JSX needs a single root because it's really one function call. A fragment satisfies that without injecting an extra DOM node, which matters whenever a wrapper div would break layout assumptions — table rows are the classic case. I use the explicit React.Fragment form only when I need to attach a key, like when fragments themselves are items in a list."

DocFragment — react.dev

036●●●○Very commonComposition

React docs say "prefer composition over inheritance." What does that mean in practice?

React components don't really support classical inheritance as a code-reuse mechanism — there's no "extend this component to add behavior" pattern that works well. Instead, React encourages passing components as props, most commonly children, so a generic wrapper (a Modal, a Card) doesn't need to know anything about what it contains.

This is more flexible than inheritance because the specialization happens at the call site, per usage, instead of being baked into a fixed subclass hierarchy — and it composes cleanly with hooks, since behavior reuse in React is achieved by extracting a custom hook, not by subclassing.

function Modal({ children, onClose }) {
  return <div className="backdrop"><div className="panel">{children}</div></div>;
}
// specialization happens by what you pass in, not by subclassing Modal
<Modal onClose={close}><ConfirmDeleteForm /></Modal>
Say it like this

"Instead of extending a base component, I pass content and behavior in as props, usually children — a Modal doesn't need to know what's inside it. For reusing logic rather than markup, the equivalent move is extracting a custom hook, not building a class hierarchy. Both push specialization to the call site instead of a fixed inheritance chain."

DocComposition vs Inheritance — React (legacy docs)

037●●○○CommonPortals

What is a portal, and when do you actually need one?

createPortal renders a subtree into a DOM node that lives outside the parent component's DOM hierarchy, while keeping it inside the normal React tree for context, event bubbling, and lifecycle purposes.

You need it whenever CSS constraints on an ancestor would break the visual result of normal nesting — an overflow: hidden or a fixed z-index stacking context on a parent that would clip or bury a modal, tooltip, or dropdown menu. The component logically belongs where it's written; visually it needs to escape.

Say it like this

"A portal renders into a different part of the DOM tree while staying in the same React tree for context and events. I reach for it whenever an ancestor's overflow or stacking context would clip something that visually needs to sit on top — modals, tooltips, dropdowns — so it stays a child logically but escapes the CSS constraint physically."

DoccreatePortal — react.dev

038●●●○Very commonStrictMode

Why does <StrictMode> render components twice in development, and should you worry about it?

In development only, StrictMode intentionally double-invokes component function bodies, and double-invokes effect setup/cleanup, to surface impure behavior that would otherwise stay hidden until it caused a real bug — mutating something during render, or an effect whose cleanup doesn't actually undo the setup. It never double-runs in production.

It's not a bug to work around; it's a signal to fix. If double-invoking an effect breaks something, the effect wasn't idempotent to begin with — usually a missing cleanup function, or side-effectful code that should live in an event handler instead of an effect.

Say it like this

"It's a dev-only diagnostic — React deliberately double-invokes render and effects to catch impurity early instead of letting it surface later as a subtle production bug. If double-running an effect breaks my component, that tells me the effect's cleanup isn't correctly undoing the setup, and I fix that rather than trying to silence StrictMode."

DocStrictMode — react.dev

039●●○○CommonError boundaries

What is an error boundary, and why can't you write one as a function component?

An error boundary is a component that catches JavaScript errors thrown during rendering anywhere in its child tree, logs them, and renders a fallback UI instead of unmounting the entire app. It only catches errors during render, in lifecycle methods, and in constructors of the tree below it — not errors in event handlers, async code, or errors in the boundary itself.

It has to be a class component because the mechanism relies on two lifecycle methods that have no hook equivalent: static getDerivedStateFromError (to render the fallback) and componentDidCatch (to log the error). No hook currently replaces this.

Say it like this

"It catches render-phase errors in its subtree and shows a fallback instead of the whole app crashing white-screen. It has to be a class because it relies on getDerivedStateFromError and componentDidCatch, which don't have a hook equivalent — it's basically the one place classes are still required in modern React, unless I pull in a library wrapper around that same mechanism."

DocError boundaries — react.dev

040●●○○CommonRefs

What's the difference between forwarding a ref and lifting state up?

They solve different problems. Lifting state up moves shared data to a common ancestor so multiple components can read and update the same piece of state — it's about data flow. Forwarding a ref (via forwardRef, or direct ref props as of React 19) lets a parent reach into a child to call an imperative method or access a DOM node — it's an escape hatch around the declarative data flow, not a replacement for it.

The rule of thumb: if the parent needs to *read or drive* something the child renders declaratively, lift state. If the parent needs to *imperatively command* the child — focus an input, scroll a container, trigger a video's play() — that's what refs are for.

Say it like this

"Lifting state up is about shared data flowing declaratively through props. Ref forwarding is an imperative escape hatch for things that don't fit the data-flow model — focusing an input, measuring a DOM node, calling a play method. I default to lifting state, and only forward a ref when the interaction is genuinely imperative by nature."

DocforwardRef — react.dev

041●●○○CommonConditional rendering

Why does {count && <Badge />} sometimes render a stray 0 on the page?

&& returns its left operand if it's falsy. When count is the number 0, JSX doesn't treat 0 as "nothing to render" the way it treats false, null, or undefined — it's a valid renderable value, so React prints the literal digit 0.

The fix is to force a real boolean before the &&, or use a ternary: {count > 0 && <Badge />} or {count ? <Badge /> : null}.

Say it like this

"React skips rendering false, null, and undefined, but zero is a legitimate renderable value, so && leaks it onto the page as text when the left side happens to be 0. I either coerce to a real boolean with a comparison or switch to a ternary — it's a small gotcha but it's the kind of thing that shows up in code review."

DocConditional rendering — react.dev

042●○○○OccasionalChildren

What's the difference between props.children and passing a component explicitly as a named prop, like renderHeader?

children is the implicit "content between the tags" slot — great for the common case of one primary piece of nested content. A named prop that holds a component or a render function gives you multiple, independently named slots, which matters when a component has more than one distinct content region (a header, a footer, a sidebar) that shouldn't just be stacked in document order.

Both are just props under the hood — children has no special mechanism, it's purely a JSX convention for the prop named children.

Say it like this

"children is just a prop with special JSX syntax for the single default slot. Once a component needs more than one distinct content region — a header and a footer that aren't just sequential — I switch to explicitly named props so each slot has an unambiguous place to go."

DocPassing JSX as children — react.dev

043●●○○CommonRules of hooks

Why can't you call a hook inside a condition or a loop?

React doesn't identify hooks by name — it tracks them by call order within a given component render, matching each useState/useEffect/etc. call to a slot in an internal linked list tied to that component instance. If a hook call is skipped on some renders (because it's inside an if) but not others, the order shifts and React attaches the wrong stored state to the wrong hook call.

The fix is always to keep the hook call unconditional and put the condition inside the hook — conditionally skip logic inside a useEffect body, or pass a condition into what the hook does, rather than conditionally calling the hook itself.

Say it like this

"React matches hooks to state by call order, not by name, so a hook that's sometimes skipped shifts every hook after it out of alignment with its stored state. The fix is always to call the hook unconditionally and move the condition inside its body, so the number and order of hook calls stays identical on every render."

DocRules of Hooks — react.dev

044●●○○CommonLists

Is it fine to use Math.random() as a React key?

No — a key has to be stable across re-renders for the same logical item. Math.random() generates a new value every render, so React sees a brand-new key each time and concludes every item was removed and a new one was added, defeating reconciliation entirely: it unmounts and remounts every item on every render, losing local state and re-triggering mount effects and animations.

It's arguably worse than using the index, which is at least stable across re-renders that don't reorder anything.

Say it like this

"A key generated fresh every render is the worst case — React treats every item as new every time, so it tears down and rebuilds the whole list on each render instead of reconciling it. Keys have to be stable per logical item across renders, which is why I pull them from the data's own id, not generate them at render time."

DocRendering lists — react.dev

045●○○○OccasionalServer Components

At a high level, what's the difference between a React Server Component and a regular client component?

A Server Component renders only on the server (or at build time) and never ships its own code to the browser — it can read from a database or filesystem directly, but it can't use state, effects, or browser APIs, because it never runs client-side at all. A regular ("client") component ships JS to the browser, hydrates, and can use hooks and interactivity as normal.

The practical model: Server Components describe structure and fetch data close to the source, reducing bundle size and request waterfalls; you drop down to a Client Component (marked with a "use client" directive in frameworks like Next.js) specifically for the parts of the tree that need interactivity, state, or effects.

Say it like this

"Server Components run only on the server, can fetch data directly, and ship zero JS for themselves to the client — but they can't use hooks or browser APIs, because they never execute in the browser. I keep the interactive leaves of the tree as client components and push everything else — layout, data fetching, static content — as far toward the server as it'll go, mainly for bundle size and fewer request waterfalls."

DocServer Components — react.dev

Block 3

Hooks in depth

Where senior gets separated from mid-level fast. Anyone can call useState; the interview is about dependency arrays, stale closures, and knowing exactly when a hook re-runs. Eighteen questions.

046●●●●Near-certainuseEffect

Walk through exactly when useEffect runs relative to render and paint.

React renders (computes the new tree), commits it to the DOM, lets the browser paint, and only then runs effects — asynchronously, after the paint. This is deliberate: effects are for synchronizing with something outside React (subscriptions, data fetching, DOM measurements that don't need to block paint), so they shouldn't hold up the user seeing the update.

Cleanup runs before the *next* effect execution — either before the component unmounts, or before the effect re-runs because a dependency changed. The order is: unmount previous effect's cleanup (if deps changed) → run new effect.

Say it like this

"Effects run after the browser has painted, not synchronously during render — that's the whole point, so they don't block the user seeing the update. Cleanup for the previous effect always runs before the next one fires, whether that's because a dependency changed or the component is unmounting, which is what makes effects safe to use for subscriptions."

DocSynchronizing with Effects — react.dev

047●●●●Near-certainuseEffect

Why does the ESLint exhaustive-deps rule matter, and when is it actually correct to disable it?

Omitting a dependency doesn't just risk a "wrong warning" — it means the effect's closure has stale values baked in from the render it was created on, so it silently uses outdated props or state instead of throwing an error. The rule exists because this class of bug is invisible until it causes a real problem, often intermittently.

It's legitimately fine to disable it in a narrow set of cases: when you intentionally want an effect to run only once (like a one-time analytics event) and you're knowingly reading a value you don't want to react to, or when you're deliberately reading the latest value via a ref instead of a dependency. The rule of thumb is: disabling it should be a deliberate, commented decision, not a way to silence a warning you didn't understand.

Say it like this

"Missing a dependency means the effect closes over a stale value from whatever render created it, and that bug doesn't announce itself — it just quietly uses old data. I only disable the rule when I've deliberately decided I don't want the effect to react to a particular value, usually because I'm reading the latest version through a ref instead, and I leave a comment explaining why."

DocRemoving Effect dependencies — react.dev

048●●●●Near-certainuseMemo/useCallback

What's the actual difference between useMemo and useCallback, and when do they not help at all?

useMemo(fn, deps) caches the return value of calling fn. useCallback(fn, deps) caches the function reference itself without calling it — useCallback(fn, deps) is exactly equivalent to useMemo(() => fn, deps).

Neither helps if nothing downstream actually depends on referential stability. Memoizing an expensive calculation whose result is only used in JSX text does nothing for render performance if the component wasn't slow to begin with — you're paying the memoization bookkeeping cost for no benefit. They earn their keep specifically when the stable reference or cached value feeds into a dependency array or a memo-wrapped child that would otherwise re-render or re-run unnecessarily.

Say it like this

"useMemo caches a computed value, useCallback caches a function reference — useCallback is really just useMemo returning a function without calling it. Both are useless in isolation; they only pay off when the stable reference actually prevents something downstream, like a memoized child or an effect dependency, from doing unnecessary work. I don't reach for them by default, I reach for them when profiling shows a real problem they'd fix."

DocuseMemo — react.dev

049●●●●Near-certainuseState

Why does calling setState twice in a row in the same handler sometimes not add up the way you'd expect?

Inside the same event handler, state is a fixed value captured from the current render's closure — it doesn't update mid-function. So setCount(count + 1); setCount(count + 1); both read the same stale count and both schedule "set to count+1," which collapses to a single increment, not two.

The fix is the functional updater form: setCount(prev => prev + 1) queues an update that receives the latest pending value at the time it's actually applied, so two calls correctly compound.

// both read the same stale `count` from this render's closure — only +1 total
setCount(count + 1);
setCount(count + 1);

// each functional update sees the latest pending value — +2 total
setCount(prev => prev + 1);
setCount(prev => prev + 1);
Say it like this

"Within one handler, the state variable is a snapshot from that render's closure, so calling setState twice with a value derived from it just schedules the same update twice. The functional form fixes it because each updater receives the latest queued value, not the render's snapshot, so consecutive calls actually compound."

DocQueueing a series of state updates — react.dev

050●●●○Very commonCustom hooks

What actually makes something a "custom hook" versus just a regular utility function?

Purely a naming convention enforced by tooling, not a language feature: a function that calls other hooks internally must be named starting with use so React's linter can apply the Rules of Hooks to it (no conditional calls, top-level only, etc.). Under the hood, a custom hook is just a function — it gets its own independent hook call slots per component that uses it, so state inside a custom hook isn't shared across components using it.

The value of extracting one is the same as extracting any function: pulling out stateful logic (a subscription, a piece of derived state, a fetch pattern) so it can be reused across components without duplicating the useState/useEffect wiring each time.

Say it like this

"It's a naming convention that unlocks tooling — anything named use that calls other hooks gets the Rules of Hooks linting applied to it. Functionally it's just a function; each component that calls it gets its own independent state, nothing is shared globally. I extract one whenever I see the same useState/useEffect combination copy-pasted across components."

DocReusing logic with custom Hooks — react.dev

051●●●○Very commonuseRef

Why doesn't updating ref.current trigger a re-render, and when is that exactly what you want?

useRef returns a plain mutable object that persists across renders, but React never tracks or diffs its .current property — mutating it is invisible to the rendering system by design, unlike state which explicitly schedules a re-render.

That's exactly the right tool for values that need to persist between renders but shouldn't cause a re-render when they change: a DOM node reference, a timer/interval id you need to clear later, a "previous value" you want to compare against without re-rendering just to store it, or a mutable flag like "has this effect already run once."

Say it like this

"A ref is a mutable box that survives across renders without React watching it, so changing it never schedules a re-render — that's the defining difference from state. I reach for it for DOM node access, storing an interval id, or holding a value like 'previous props' that I need to read later but that has no business causing its own render."

DocuseRef — react.dev

052●●●○Very commonuseEffect vs useLayoutEffect

When would you reach for useLayoutEffect instead of useEffect?

useLayoutEffect runs synchronously after the DOM is updated but before the browser paints — it blocks paint until it finishes. useEffect runs asynchronously after paint. That timing difference is the entire reason to pick one over the other.

You need useLayoutEffect specifically when you must measure the DOM (like an element's size or position) and synchronously apply a change based on that measurement before the user sees anything — otherwise there's a visible flash of the wrong layout for one frame. For anything else — data fetching, subscriptions, logging — useEffect is correct and doesn't block the browser from painting.

Say it like this

"useLayoutEffect fires synchronously before paint, useEffect fires asynchronously after paint — that's the only real difference, but it matters a lot for one case: if I need to measure something in the DOM and immediately adjust layout based on it, useEffect would let the user see a flash of the wrong state for a frame. Outside of that measure-then-mutate pattern, I default to useEffect because it doesn't block rendering."

DocuseLayoutEffect — react.dev

053●●●○Very commonuseReducer

When do you reach for useReducer instead of several useState calls?

When state updates involve multiple sub-values that change together in a coordinated way, or the "next state" logic depends on the previous state in a non-trivial way (multiple related fields, a state machine with distinct modes/transitions). Spreading that logic across several useState calls means every event handler has to know how to correctly update all of them in sync, which duplicates and scatters the update logic.

useReducer centralizes the "how state changes" logic into one pure function, so components dispatch intent ("form submitted", "item removed") rather than manually computing the next state inline — which is also easier to test in isolation, since a reducer is a pure function you can call directly with no rendering involved.

Say it like this

"Once several pieces of state change together, or the next state genuinely depends on the current state in a non-trivial way, I move it into a reducer so the transition logic lives in one testable, pure function instead of being duplicated across handlers. Components end up dispatching intent — what happened — rather than computing the next state inline, which also makes the logic trivial to unit test without rendering anything."

DocExtracting state logic into a reducer — react.dev

054●●●○Very commonEffects vs event handlers

You need to show a toast notification after a successful form submission. Do you do that in an effect or in the submit handler, and why?

In the event handler. An effect that watches for "did the submission succeed" and then shows a toast is reacting to a state change as a proxy for an event that already happened — it's indirect, and it re-fires if that success state is set again for any other reason (like a parent re-mounting the component with success already true).

The rule of thumb from the React team: if something needs to happen because of a specific interaction, it belongs in the event handler that caused it. Effects are for synchronizing a component with an external system based on state that's true regardless of *why* it became true — not as a general-purpose "run this after that" mechanism.

Say it like this

"That belongs directly in the submit handler — it's a response to a specific interaction, not a synchronization concern. If I put it in an effect watching for a success flag, it fires any time that flag becomes true for any reason, including ones that have nothing to do with this particular submission, which is a subtle source of duplicate or unexpected toasts."

DocSeparating events from Effects — react.dev

055●●●○Very commonuseEffect cleanup

A fetch inside useEffect can "win" out of order if a dependency changes quickly (like a search query). How do you prevent the stale response from overwriting the fresh one?

Two overlapping requests can resolve in reverse order — the older request's response can arrive after the newer one and overwrite it with stale data. The fix is a cleanup-flag pattern: set a local flag when the effect starts, and in the cleanup function (which runs before the next effect execution) mark that this particular effect's result should be ignored if it resolves later.

useEffect(() => {
  let ignore = false;
  fetchResults(query).then(data => {
    if (!ignore) setResults(data); // only the latest effect's response applies
  });
  return () => { ignore = true; }; // runs before the next effect, marking this one stale
}, [query]);
Say it like this

"I use the ignore-flag cleanup pattern — a local boolean set in the effect, flipped to true in the cleanup, and checked before applying the response. Since cleanup always runs before the next effect starts, any in-flight request from a stale query gets its result silently dropped. If I'm using something like React Query or SWR this is handled automatically, but it's worth knowing the raw pattern."

DocFetching data — react.dev

056●●○○CommonuseState init

What's the difference between useState(expensiveCalc()) and useState(() => expensiveCalc())?

useState(expensiveCalc()) calls expensiveCalc() on every single render, even though the returned value is only actually used on the very first render — the result of every subsequent call is thrown away. Passing a function, useState(() => expensiveCalc()), gives React the initializer lazily: React only calls it once, on mount, and ignores it on every re-render.

Say it like this

"Passing a value directly means it gets computed on every render even though only the first one matters — the lazy initializer function form is called exactly once, on mount. It's an easy one to miss because both versions work correctly, one is just quietly wasting work on every re-render if the calculation is non-trivial."

DocAvoiding recreating the initial state — react.dev

057●●○○CommonuseContext

A component consuming a context re-renders even though the specific field it reads didn't change. Why, and what do you do about it?

useContext subscribes to the whole context value, not individual fields inside it. Every consumer re-renders whenever the provider passes a new value reference, regardless of which field actually changed — because React has no way to know the consumer only "cares about" one property of that object.

Fixes: split one large context into several smaller, more focused contexts so unrelated updates don't cascade; memoize the context value object itself so it doesn't get a new reference on every provider render for no reason; or move to a state management library that supports selector-based subscriptions to a slice of state instead of the whole object.

Say it like this

"Context subscribes to the whole value as one unit — there's no built-in way to subscribe to just one field. My first move is splitting a bloated context into several narrower ones so consumers only subscribe to what they actually use; if the state is genuinely complex and shared widely, I'll reach for something with selector-based subscriptions instead, like Zustand or Redux with reselect."

DocScaling up with reducer and context — react.dev

058●●○○CommonuseImperativeHandle

What does useImperativeHandle do, and why should it be rare in a codebase?

It lets a component customize exactly what a parent receives when it attaches a ref to that component — instead of exposing the raw DOM node, the component can expose a curated object with specific methods, like { focus, scrollToTop }, hiding the rest of its internals.

It should be rare because it's an imperative escape hatch that works against React's declarative data-flow model — every use is a small hidden coupling where a parent directly commands a child rather than passing props and letting the child render accordingly. It's justified for things that are genuinely imperative by nature (focus management, imperative animations, scroll control) but is a smell if it's being used to work around a design that should just be props and state.

Say it like this

"It lets you control what a parent sees when it grabs a ref to your component — a curated set of imperative methods instead of the raw node. I keep it rare because every use is a bit of hidden imperative coupling that bypasses normal prop flow; it's the right tool for genuinely imperative things like focus or scroll, and a smell anywhere else."

DocuseImperativeHandle — react.dev

059●●○○CommonuseSyncExternalStore

What problem does useSyncExternalStore solve that useEffect + useState doesn't?

Subscribing to an external store (browser APIs like window.innerWidth, a third-party state library, a WebSocket connection) with a useEffect that calls setState on change is prone to tearing under React's concurrent rendering — different parts of the tree can briefly read different values of the same external store during a single render, because the effect-based subscription isn't synchronized with React's render phase.

useSyncExternalStore is the official primitive for subscribing to external mutable state safely: React uses it to guarantee every component reading the same store sees a consistent value within one render, and it correctly handles server-rendering too.

Say it like this
