"It's the sanctioned way to subscribe to something outside React's state without risking tearing under concurrent rendering — a useEffect-based subscription can let different parts of the tree see different values of the same external store mid-render. Most of the time I don't call it directly, because libraries like Zustand and React Router already use it internally, but it's the primitive underneath them."

DocuseSyncExternalStore — react.dev

060●●○○CommonuseTransition

What does useTransition actually change about how an update is scheduled?

It lets you mark a state update as non-urgent. React still processes urgent updates (typing in a controlled input, clicking a button) immediately and keeps the UI responsive to them, but it can interrupt, delay, or deprioritize the transition update's rendering work — including throwing away in-progress render work for the transition if something more urgent comes in — instead of blocking the main thread until the expensive update finishes.

The classic use case: typing into a search box that filters a huge list. Without a transition, every keystroke can trigger a heavy re-render that makes the input itself feel laggy. Wrapping the list-filtering state update in startTransition keeps the input snappy while the (now interruptible) list update happens slightly behind, with isPending available to show a subtle loading indicator.

Say it like this

"It tells React that a particular state update can be interrupted and deprioritized in favor of more urgent updates, like the next keystroke. My go-to example is a search box filtering a large list — I wrap the filter update in startTransition so typing stays responsive, and use isPending to show a subtle 'updating' indicator while the heavier work catches up in the background."

DocuseTransition — react.dev

061●○○○OccasionaluseDeferredValue

How is useDeferredValue different from debouncing the same value with a timer?

A debounce with setTimeout waits for a fixed time window of inactivity before updating, regardless of how fast or slow the device actually is — you have to pick a delay in milliseconds by hand. useDeferredValue instead asks React to render an update to the deferred value only when there's spare rendering capacity, adapting automatically to how fast the current device and current render actually are, and it can still be interrupted for a more urgent update just like a transition.

They also compose differently with React's model: since useDeferredValue is a React scheduling primitive rather than a real-world timer, it correctly participates in interruption and doesn't need a manually tuned delay constant that has to be re-tuned across devices.

Say it like this

"A timer-based debounce commits to a fixed delay regardless of the device; useDeferredValue instead defers the expensive render until React actually has spare capacity, so it adapts to how fast the render loop currently is rather than a magic number I picked. It's also interruptible the same way a transition is, which a plain setTimeout debounce isn't."

DocuseDeferredValue — react.dev

062●●○○CommonuseId

Why does React need useId instead of just generating a random id with Math.random() for accessibility attributes?

Two reasons. First, an id generated with Math.random() or an incrementing module-level counter changes on every render (or diverges between renders in Strict Mode's double-invocation), which breaks the stable id/htmlFor pairing that accessibility attributes rely on. Second, with server-side rendering, an id generated independently on the server and the client can mismatch, since each environment computes its own random or incremented value — causing a hydration mismatch.

useId generates a stable, unique-per-component-instance id that is guaranteed to match between server and client render passes, specifically because it's derived from the component's position in the tree rather than a random or mutable counter.

Say it like this

"A randomly generated id isn't stable across renders and, worse, isn't guaranteed to match between server and client output, which causes a hydration mismatch. useId is deterministic based on the component's position in the tree, so it's stable across renders and identical on both server and client — I use it specifically for wiring up label/input id pairs and other aria attributes."

DocuseId — react.dev

063●○○○OccasionalCustom hooks

Sketch a usePrevious hook that returns the value a piece of state held on the last render.

This is the canonical example of using a ref to remember something across renders without causing an extra render: the ref is updated in an effect, which runs after the render has already committed and read the old value, so during render the ref still correctly holds the previous render's value.

function usePrevious(value) {
  const ref = useRef();
  useEffect(() => {
    ref.current = value; // runs after this render commits
  });
  return ref.current; // during render, still holds last render's value
}
Say it like this

"I store the value in a ref and update it inside an effect with no dependency array, so it runs after every render. Because the effect runs after render, during the current render the ref still holds whatever was written last render — which is exactly 'the previous value.' It's a nice small example of using a ref to carry state across renders without triggering one."

DocuseRef — react.dev

Block 4

Component design & patterns

Less about API trivia, more about taste — how you split components, where logic should live, and whether you can justify the structure you chose. Fourteen questions.

064●●●○Very commonCompound components

What is the compound component pattern, and what problem does it solve compared to one component with a dozen boolean props?

Instead of a single component absorbing every variation as a flag (<Tabs showIcons hasCloseButton verticalLayout ... />), compound components split the UI into several components that share implicit state through context, and are composed by the consumer: <Tabs><Tabs.List><Tabs.Tab /></Tabs.List><Tabs.Panels /></Tabs>.

This scales far better than a props explosion because the consumer controls composition and ordering directly in JSX rather than through an ever-growing configuration API, and each sub-component only needs to know about the shared context, not about every sibling's props.

const TabsContext = createContext(null);
function Tabs({ children, defaultTab }) {
  const [active, setActive] = useState(defaultTab);
  return <TabsContext.Provider value={{ active, setActive }}>{children}</TabsContext.Provider>;
}
Tabs.Tab = function({ id, children }) {
  const { active, setActive } = useContext(TabsContext);
  return <button aria-selected={active === id} onClick={() => setActive(id)}>{children}</button>;
};
Say it like this

"It splits a component into pieces that share state through context, composed by the consumer instead of configured through props. It scales better than a growing list of booleans because the JSX structure itself expresses layout and composition, and each sub-component only needs to know the shared context, not every other prop the parent accepts."

DocPassing data deeply with context — react.dev

065●●●○Very commonContainer/presentational

Is the container/presentational split still relevant now that hooks exist?

The pattern (a "smart" container component owning data and logic, wrapping a "dumb" presentational component that only renders based on props) predates hooks and was largely a workaround for class components, where you couldn't easily reuse stateful logic without wrapping components. Hooks solve the actual reuse problem more directly — a custom hook extracts the logic without forcing an extra wrapper component or prop-drilling layer.

The underlying principle is still worth keeping: separating "what fetches/computes the data" from "how it's displayed" makes components more testable and reusable. What's outdated is treating it as a rigid two-component-per-feature rule — today that separation usually lives as a custom hook plus a component, not two nested components.

Say it like this

"The principle behind it is still good — separate data and logic from rendering — but the mechanism has moved. Instead of a container component wrapping a presentational one, I pull the data and logic into a custom hook and keep the component focused on rendering what the hook returns. Same separation of concerns, one fewer wrapper component and no prop-drilling in between."

DocReusing logic with custom Hooks — react.dev

066●●●○Very commonProp drilling

You've got prop drilling five levels deep. What are your actual options, in order of preference, and why not jump straight to a global store?

First option is usually the composition fix, not a new tool: pass the component itself down as a prop (or as children) instead of passing raw data through every intermediate layer that doesn't use it — the intermediate components stop needing to know about the data at all. Second, if the data is genuinely needed by many components at different depths and composition doesn't fit naturally, context is the right scope-appropriate tool.

Jumping straight to a global state library for what's actually local, feature-scoped state adds indirection, makes the data's lifetime harder to reason about (it now outlives the feature that "owns" it), and couples unrelated features to a shared store. The decision should follow the data's actual scope, not default to the most powerful tool available.

Say it like this

"First I check whether the drilling is really a composition problem — passing the component down as children often removes the need to thread the data through layers that don't use it. If several components at different depths genuinely need the same data, that's what context is for. I only reach for a global store when the state's scope is genuinely app-wide, because otherwise I'm giving feature-local data a global lifetime and coupling it to everything else that touches the store."

DocPassing data deeply with context — react.dev

067●●●○Very commonRender props

What is the render-prop pattern, and why has it mostly been replaced by custom hooks?

A render prop is a component that takes a function as a prop (often children) and calls it with internal state, letting the consumer decide how to render that state: <MouseTracker>{pos => <Cursor x={pos.x} y={pos.y} />}</MouseTracker>. It solved logic reuse before hooks existed, but forces an extra component in the tree and nests visually — several render props stacked get hard to read.

A custom hook exposes the same underlying logic (const pos = useMousePosition()) without wrapping anything or changing the component tree's shape, which is why it's now the default for pure logic reuse. Render props haven't disappeared entirely — they're still occasionally useful when a component needs to control not just data but the *timing* of rendering itself, like a virtualization library exposing a render slot per row.

Say it like this

"A render prop hands the consumer a function to call with internal state, so the consumer controls the output. Hooks give you the same reusable logic without an extra wrapper component or the nesting pyramid you get from stacking several render props — that's why hooks won for the general case. Render props still show up in libraries that need to control the rendering timing itself, not just hand back a value."

DocRender Props — React (legacy docs)

068●●○○CommonHigher-order components

What's a higher-order component, and what's a real downside beyond "hooks are nicer"?

A HOC is a function that takes a component and returns a new, enhanced component — withAuth(Dashboard). Beyond being largely superseded by hooks for logic reuse, HOCs have a concrete, still-relevant downside: they add a wrapper layer to the component tree for every HOC applied, which makes debugging in React DevTools noisier (each layer shows up as its own node), and prop names can collide silently if two HOCs happen to inject a prop with the same name.

They also make static typing harder in TypeScript — correctly typing the props a HOC injects versus the props it passes through requires more generic gymnastics than a hook's return type does.

Say it like this

"A HOC wraps a component to inject behavior or props. Beyond hooks being the more direct tool now, a real practical cost is the extra layer in the component tree per HOC — it clutters DevTools and there's a real risk of two HOCs silently colliding on an injected prop name. Typing them correctly in TypeScript is also noticeably more work than typing a hook."

DocHigher-Order Components — React (legacy docs)

069●●○○CommonColocation

What does "colocate state as close as possible to where it's used" mean in practice, and why does it matter for performance?

It means state should live in the lowest component in the tree that actually needs it, not be hoisted up "just in case" a parent might need it someday. State that lives higher than necessary causes every component between the state owner and the actual consumer to re-render when it changes, even components that don't read that state at all.

The practical move: if only one input field's local UI state (like whether a dropdown is open) is being lifted to a page-level component "for consistency," that's an unforced performance cost — moving it back down to the component that owns that specific piece of UI shrinks the re-render blast radius to just that subtree.

Say it like this

"State that lives higher than it needs to forces every component between the owner and the actual consumer to re-render on every change, whether or not they read that state. My default is to push state down to the lowest component that actually needs it, and only lift it when a sibling genuinely needs to read or coordinate with it — not preemptively."

DocSharing state between components — react.dev

070●●○○CommonControlled components

Design an Accordion that works both as a fully-controlled component (parent owns which item is open) and as an uncontrolled one with sensible internal defaults. What's the pattern?

This is the "controlled/uncontrolled with a fallback" pattern most UI libraries use: accept optional value/onChange props for controlled usage, fall back to internal state via useState seeded from an optional defaultValue when they're not provided, and derive which value is actually "live" based on whether value was supplied.

function Accordion({ value, onChange, defaultValue }) {
  const [internalValue, setInternalValue] = useState(defaultValue);
  const isControlled = value !== undefined;
  const activeValue = isControlled ? value : internalValue;

  function handleChange(next) {
    if (!isControlled) setInternalValue(next);
    onChange?.(next); // always notify, whether controlled or not
  }
  // render using activeValue and handleChange
}
Say it like this

"I check whether value was actually passed to decide which state is authoritative — internal state if it wasn't provided, the prop if it was — and I always call onChange regardless, so a controlled parent can respond to the interaction. It's the same pattern most headless UI libraries use, and it means the same component works standalone or fully controlled without a separate implementation for each."

DocControlled and uncontrolled components — react.dev

071●●○○CommonComponent size

What's your actual signal for splitting a component into smaller ones, beyond "it's getting long"?

Line count alone is a weak signal — a long component with one clear responsibility is fine. The real signals are: a piece of the component has its own independent reason to change (a different part of the business logic evolves separately), a section could be reused elsewhere as-is, a chunk of state only affects a sub-section and forces unrelated siblings to re-render, or you find yourself scrolling past unrelated logic to get to the part you need to edit.

Splitting purely by line count often produces components with confusing names and prop lists that just shuffle the same tangled logic across more files, without actually reducing coupling.

Say it like this

"Length by itself isn't the trigger — a genuinely single-purpose component can be long and that's fine. I split when a section has its own reason to change independently, when state local to one part is forcing unrelated siblings to re-render, or when a chunk is reusable elsewhere as-is. Splitting purely on line count tends to just scatter the same coupling across more files."

DocNesting and organizing components — react.dev

072●●○○CommonDerived state

Why is "storing a value in state that's just computed from other state" usually a bug waiting to happen?

If a value can be computed directly from existing props or state during render, storing it in its own useState creates two sources of truth that can drift out of sync — every place that changes the original value now also has to remember to update the derived copy, and it's easy to miss one path.

The fix is almost always to just compute it inline during render (optionally wrapped in useMemo if the computation is expensive), rather than syncing it into state with an effect. An effect that copies one state value into another, derived state variable is one of the clearest "you might not need an Effect" anti-patterns.

// anti-pattern: two sources of truth that can drift
const [fullName, setFullName] = useState("");
useEffect(() => { setFullName(`${first} ${last}`); }, [first, last]);

// correct: derive during render, no separate state, no effect
const fullName = `${first} ${last}`;
Say it like this

"Any value you can compute from existing state should just be computed inline during render, not stored in its own state synced via an effect — the moment it's duplicated, you've created two sources of truth and every update path has to remember to keep them in sync. I only reach for useMemo on top of that if the computation is actually measurably expensive."

DocYou Might Not Need an Effect — react.dev

073●●○○CommonSlots

How would you design a Button component that needs to sometimes render as an <a> tag instead of a <button>, without duplicating all the styling logic?

The common solution is an as prop (sometimes called a polymorphic component pattern): the component accepts which underlying element or component to render as, defaulting to button, while keeping all the shared styling and behavior logic in one place.

function Button({ as: Component = 'button', className, ...props }) {
  return <Component className={cx(styles.button, className)} {...props} />;
}
// usage
<Button as="a" href="/pricing">See pricing</Button>
<Button onClick={submit}>Submit</Button>
Say it like this

"I use a polymorphic 'as' prop that defaults to button but accepts any element or component, so the visual and behavioral logic lives once and just gets rendered through a different tag when needed. In TypeScript that needs some generic work to correctly type the props based on what 'as' resolves to, but the runtime pattern itself is simple."

DocPassing props to a component — react.dev

074●○○○OccasionalHeadless components

What does "headless component" mean, and what's the actual trade-off versus a fully-styled component library?

A headless component (or headless hook) provides behavior, state management, and accessibility wiring (focus trapping, keyboard navigation, ARIA attributes) but renders no styling or markup opinions — you supply the actual JSX and CSS, and the library just gives you the props to attach and the logic to drive it.

The trade-off: you get full visual control and no fighting with an opinionated design system's CSS, but you take on more implementation work up front, and you're trusting the library to have gotten subtle accessibility behavior right, since you're not going to re-derive keyboard navigation for a combobox from scratch yourself.

Say it like this

"Headless means the library owns the behavior and accessibility logic — keyboard nav, focus management, ARIA wiring — but hands you the props to render however you want, with zero styling opinions. The trade-off against a fully-styled kit is more upfront markup work in exchange for full visual control and not fighting someone else's CSS, while still not having to reinvent accessibility from scratch."

DocReusing logic with custom Hooks — react.dev

075●●○○CommonProp types

A component accepts either an items array or a loading boolean, but never both meaningfully at once. How do you model that in TypeScript so invalid combinations aren't representable?

A single props type with both fields optional lets you construct nonsensical states like { loading: true, items: [...] } that the component then has to defensively guard against at runtime. A discriminated union makes the invalid state impossible to construct in the first place — TypeScript narrows based on the shared literal field.

type ListProps =
  | { status: 'loading' }
  | { status: 'error'; message: string }
  | { status: 'ready'; items: Item[] };

function List(props: ListProps) {
  if (props.status === 'loading') return <Spinner />;
  if (props.status === 'error') return <Error message={props.message} />;
  return <ul>{props.items.map(...)}</ul>; // TS knows `items` exists here
}
Say it like this

"I model it as a discriminated union on a status field instead of a bag of optional booleans, so the invalid combination — loading true but items also populated — literally can't be constructed. Inside the component, TypeScript narrows automatically once I check the status field, so I get type-safe access to items only in the branch where it's actually guaranteed to exist."

DocDiscriminated unions — TypeScript Handbook

076●○○○OccasionalFeature folders

"Feature folders" versus "type folders" (all components together, all hooks together) — which do you default to and why?

Type-based folders (components/, hooks/, utils/ at the top level) scale badly once the app grows: working on one feature means jumping between several distant top-level folders, and it's hard to tell what's actually related to what just from the folder structure. Feature-based folders (features/checkout/ containing its own components, hooks, and types) keep everything related to one concern physically close, which matches how people actually work — in one feature at a time.

The caveat: it only pays off once there's an actual concept of separable features. For a small app, the type-based structure is simpler and the split isn't worth the ceremony yet.

Say it like this

"I default to feature folders once the app is big enough to have real features, because it keeps everything you touch while working on one thing physically close together, instead of jumping between distant top-level components and hooks folders. For a small app or a prototype it's not worth the ceremony yet — type-based folders are simpler until the codebase actually earns the split."

DocThinking in React — react.dev

077●●○○CommonChildren as function

What's the difference between React.Children.map and just calling .map directly on props.children?

props.children isn't guaranteed to be an array — it can be a single element, a string, null, or an array, depending entirely on how many children were passed. Calling .map directly on it breaks the moment there's exactly one child, since a single element doesn't have a .map method.

React.Children.map normalizes all of these shapes for you and also automatically handles key assignment for the mapped output, which is easy to get subtly wrong by hand.

Say it like this

"props.children isn't always an array — it depends on whether zero, one, or many children were passed, and calling .map directly on it breaks on the single-child case. React.Children.map normalizes all those shapes and handles key generation correctly, so I use it whenever I'm actually iterating over children rather than just passing them through as-is."

DocChildren — react.dev

Web Components & interop

Less common than the rest of this block, but a real question at companies with a design system that needs to work outside React too, or a legacy app being migrated incrementally.

078●○○○OccasionalUsing a Web Component in React

You need to use a third-party Web Component (a custom element) inside a React app. What's the actual friction point, beyond just rendering the tag?

Rendering the custom element itself works fine — JSX passes through unknown tag names to document.createElement like any HTML element. The real friction is how data gets passed in and events get read back out: React's JSX attributes map to HTML attributes (strings) by default, but many Web Components expect richer JavaScript values — objects, arrays — passed as actual DOM properties, not string attributes, and React doesn't automatically know which of a custom element's props should be set as a property versus an attribute.

Similarly, Web Components typically communicate outward via native CustomEvents dispatched on the element, not React's synthetic event/prop-callback model — so listening to them means manually attaching a native event listener via a ref (useEffect + addEventListener/removeEventListener) rather than passing an onSomething prop the way you would to a normal React component, since React's JSX event prop convention doesn't automatically bridge to custom element events.

function Wrapper() {
  const ref = useRef(null);
  useEffect(() => {
    const el = ref.current;
    if (!el) return;
    el.someComplexProp = { foo: 'bar' }; // set as a real property, not a string attribute
    const handler = e => console.log(e.detail);
    el.addEventListener('custom-event', handler); // native event, not a React prop
    return () => el.removeEventListener('custom-event', handler);
  }, []);
  return <my-custom-element ref={ref} />;
}
Say it like this

"Rendering the tag itself is trivial — the friction is that Web Components often expect rich values set as real DOM properties, not string attributes, and React's JSX attributes default to the attribute model. I usually end up grabbing a ref and imperatively setting properties and wiring up native addEventListener for its CustomEvents in an effect, since React's synthetic event and prop-callback model doesn't automatically bridge to how custom elements actually communicate."

DocCustom HTML Elements — react.dev

079●○○○OccasionalShadow DOM

A Web Component uses Shadow DOM for style encapsulation. Why can't your app's global CSS style anything inside it, and is that ever a problem?

Shadow DOM creates a genuinely separate DOM subtree with its own encapsulated style boundary — CSS rules from the outer document don't cross into a shadow root by design, and rules defined inside the shadow root don't leak out either. This is the actual mechanism (not just a convention) that CSS Modules and CSS-in-JS attempt to approximate at the tooling level — Shadow DOM gives you real, browser-enforced style isolation.

It becomes a real problem specifically when you need to theme or override something inside a third-party Web Component's shadow root from your app — global CSS structurally cannot reach in. The component has to explicitly opt into being customizable, usually via CSS custom properties (which do cross the shadow boundary by design, unlike regular CSS rules) or explicitly exposed ::part selectors that the component author intentionally makes stylable from outside. If the component author didn't expose either mechanism, there's genuinely no way to theme it from outside — that's a real limitation to flag when evaluating a third-party Web Component for use in a themed app.

Say it like this

"Shadow DOM is real, browser-enforced style isolation — CSS from outside genuinely doesn't cross into a shadow root, and it's the actual mechanism CSS Modules and CSS-in-JS are approximating at the tooling level. It becomes a real limitation when I need to theme something inside a third-party component's shadow root — I can only reach in through CSS custom properties or explicit ::part selectors the component author chose to expose, and if they didn't expose either, there's genuinely no way in from outside, which I'd flag before committing to using that component in a themed app."

DocUsing shadow DOM — MDN

080●○○○OccasionalExposing React as a Web Component

Why would a team wrap a React component as a custom element (a Web Component) rather than just exporting the React component directly for other teams to use?

A raw exported React component requires the consumer to also be a React app — using the same version of React, going through the same build pipeline, understanding React's specific integration patterns. A custom element wrapping the same component exposes a framework-agnostic interface: any consumer — a Vue app, a plain HTML page, a different version of React entirely — can use it with nothing more than an HTML tag, no shared build tooling or framework version alignment required.

This matters specifically for a design system or shared component meant to be consumed across genuinely different tech stacks or across teams that can't or won't align on the same React version — the custom element boundary means the internal implementation (React version, internal state management, build tooling) is completely opaque to and independent from whatever's consuming it, at the cost of needing to build and maintain that wrapping layer, plus losing some of React's own idioms (passing a React component as a prop, for instance) at that boundary.

Say it like this

"A raw exported React component forces the consumer to also be React, on a compatible version, in a compatible build setup. Wrapping it as a custom element exposes a framework-agnostic interface — any consumer just uses an HTML tag, no shared tooling or React version alignment required. That's worth the wrapping effort specifically when a component genuinely needs to be consumed across different stacks or teams that can't align on a shared React version — the trade-off is losing some React-specific idioms right at that boundary, like passing a component as a prop."

DoccreateRoot — react.dev

081●○○○OccasionalIncremental migration

A large legacy app built with a different framework (or no framework) needs one specific section rebuilt in React, without a full rewrite. How does React coexist with the existing app in the same page?

React can mount into any specific DOM node via createRoot(domNode).render(...) — it doesn't need to own the entire page, just the specific container element it's told to render into. This means React can genuinely coexist alongside legacy jQuery, vanilla JS, or another framework's code on the same page, each owning its own distinct DOM subtree, with React's render fully scoped to only the elements inside its mount point.

The real complexity isn't the mounting itself — it's the boundary between React's world and the legacy code's world: if the legacy code needs to communicate with the React island (passing data in, reacting to something that happened inside it), that has to go through an explicit bridge — custom events, a shared minimal state object, or exposing specific imperative methods — since there's no automatic two-way binding between a React root and code outside it. This is exactly the incremental strategy that lets a team migrate a large legacy app piece by piece rather than needing the high-risk, long-duration full rewrite covered earlier for modernizing legacy React code, just applied to migrating into React rather than modernizing within it.

Say it like this

DoccreateRoot — react.dev

082●○○○OccasionalAtomic Design

What does Atomic Design's atoms/molecules/organisms hierarchy actually give a team, and where does it tend to break down in practice?

The methodology proposes a strict compositional hierarchy: atoms (a button, an input — the smallest indivisible pieces), molecules (a labeled input combining an atom with a label, a small functional unit), organisms (a search bar combining several molecules into a distinct section), templates, and pages. The genuine value is giving a team a shared vocabulary for component granularity and reinforcing composition as the default way to build UI — a molecule should be built from atoms, not duplicate an atom's styling inline, which naturally pushes toward reuse rather than one-off implementations.

Where it tends to break down: real components frequently don't fit cleanly into exactly one tier — is a card with an image, title, and button an organism or a molecule, and does it actually matter which label it gets — and teams that treat the taxonomy as a rigid classification exercise can spend real time debating labels that don't affect the actual code quality either way. In practice, most teams that get value from it use the vocabulary loosely (as a rough sense of "how composed is this thing") rather than enforcing a strict folder structure with exactly five tiers, since the folder-per-tier version tends to produce more bikeshedding than benefit.

Say it like this

"The real value is a shared vocabulary for component granularity that reinforces composition — a molecule should be built from atoms, not duplicate their styling. Where it breaks down is that real components often don't fit cleanly into exactly one tier, and teams that treat it as a strict classification system end up debating labels that don't actually affect code quality. I've found it works better used loosely as a mental model than enforced as a rigid five-folder structure, which tends to generate more bikeshedding than benefit."

DocAtomic Design — Brad Frost

083●●○○CommonDrag and drop

What's the actual limitation of the native HTML5 Drag and Drop API that leads most React apps to reach for a library like dnd-kit instead of using it directly?

The native API (draggable, dragstart/dragover/drop events) works, but has several real gaps that show up quickly in a production UI: it has essentially no built-in touch/mobile support at all (it's a mouse-era API, so a mobile drag interaction needs a completely separate touch-event implementation layered on top), the built-in drag preview/ghost image is difficult to style and behaves inconsistently across browsers, and it provides no accessibility story out of the box — keyboard-based reordering (a real requirement for users who can't use a mouse) isn't something the native API gives you for free, you'd have to build a whole parallel keyboard interaction model yourself.

Libraries like dnd-kit (or the earlier react-beautiful-dnd) exist specifically to paper over these gaps: they provide a unified interaction model that works across mouse, touch, and keyboard from one API, consistent and stylable drag previews, and built-in accessibility (screen reader announcements during a drag operation, keyboard-based reordering as a first-class supported interaction, not an afterthought). The trade-off is a real dependency and some bundle weight, but for anything beyond the simplest single-mouse-only prototype, the native API's gaps are usually significant enough to justify it.

Say it like this

"The native API has essentially no touch support at all, so a mobile drag interaction needs a completely separate implementation layered on top, and it has no accessibility story out of the box — keyboard-based reordering isn't something you get for free, you'd have to build a whole parallel interaction model yourself. Libraries like dnd-kit paper over exactly those gaps with one unified interaction model across mouse, touch, and keyboard, plus built-in accessibility. For anything beyond a mouse-only prototype, I'd reach for the library rather than fight the native API's real gaps."

Docdnd-kit

084●○○○OccasionalReorderable list state

A user drags an item to reorder a list, but the actual reorder needs to be confirmed by the server, which can fail. How do you handle the UI during and after the drag to avoid a confusing experience?

The interaction has two distinct phases with different state needs. During the drag itself, the reorder should feel instant and purely visual — the list visually reflows to show the new order as the user drags, driven entirely by local UI state, with no network round trip involved in the drag gesture itself, since waiting on a server response mid-drag would make the interaction feel broken and laggy.

Once the drop happens, this becomes exactly the optimistic-update pattern covered earlier: apply the new order to local state immediately (it already visually looks reordered from the drag), fire the request to persist the new order server-side, and if it fails, roll back to the previous order and surface the failure — the same snapshot-before/rollback-on-failure discipline as any other optimistic update, just triggered by a drop event instead of a form submission or a button click. The one added subtlety specific to drag-and-drop: the rollback needs to visually animate back to the original position rather than snapping instantly, or the failure itself becomes a second jarring, confusing UI event on top of the original failed action.

Say it like this

"During the drag itself it should be purely local visual state, no network round trip, since waiting on a server response mid-drag would make it feel laggy and broken. Once it drops, it's the same optimistic-update pattern as anything else — apply the new order locally immediately, fire the persist request, and roll back with a surfaced error if it fails. The one extra detail specific to drag-and-drop is that the rollback should animate back rather than snap instantly, or the failure itself becomes a second jarring UI event on top of the original one."

DocOptimistic Updates — TanStack Query docs

Block 5

State management

Not "which library" trivia — the interview is really about whether you can reason about where state should live and why. Sixteen questions.

085●●●●Near-certainState scope

How do you decide whether a piece of state belongs in local component state, context, or a global store?

The decision follows scope and lifetime, not preference. Local state (useState) is right when only one component and its direct children need it, and it should be reset or discarded naturally when that component unmounts — a form field, a dropdown's open/closed flag. Context is right when several components at different depths in one subtree need to read the same value, but you don't need fine-grained subscriptions or the ability to update it from far outside that subtree efficiently — theme, current user, locale.

A global store (Redux, Zustand) earns its place when state needs to be read and updated from genuinely unrelated parts of the app, survive component unmounts, or benefit from tooling like time-travel debugging or selector-based subscriptions to avoid re-rendering everything on every change. Reaching for a global store by default, before the app actually needs that scope, is the most common overengineering mistake here.

Say it like this

"I follow the actual scope of the data. If only one component subtree cares and it can die with that component, it's local state. If several components at different depths in one area need the same value without heavy update frequency, that's context. A global store is for state that's genuinely shared across unrelated parts of the app or needs to persist independent of any one component's lifecycle — I don't default to it just because it's available."

DocSharing state between components — react.dev

086●●●●Near-certainServer vs client state

What's the difference between "server state" and "client state," and why do libraries like React Query treat them differently from Redux-style state?

Client state is state your app fully owns and controls synchronously — a modal's open flag, form input values, a selected tab. Server state is data your app doesn't own: it lives on a server, is fetched asynchronously, can be stale the moment you receive it, can change from outside your app's actions, and is inherently shared across whoever else is using the API.

Treating server state like client state (dumping fetched data into Redux and manually managing loading/error flags, caching, and refetching) means reimplementing caching, deduplication, background refetching, and staleness logic by hand. Tools like React Query or SWR are purpose-built for exactly that lifecycle — cache keys, stale time, automatic refetch on focus/reconnect — which is a fundamentally different problem than synchronous UI state.

Say it like this

"Client state is synchronous and fully owned by the app — a modal flag, a form value. Server state is async, can go stale, and isn't really 'owned' by any one client. I keep them in separate systems: client state in useState or a lightweight store, server state in something like React Query, because it already solves caching, deduplication, and staleness — problems that have nothing to do with how you'd model a boolean toggle."

DocTanStack Query overview

087●●●○Very commonRedux

Why does Redux insist on immutable updates and pure reducers? What actually breaks if you mutate state directly?

Redux's core mechanisms depend on being able to detect change by reference comparison — the store notifies subscribers (typically react-redux's connected components) by checking whether the returned slice reference changed. Mutating state in place keeps the same reference, so subscribers never get notified and the UI silently doesn't update even though the underlying data changed.

Purity and immutability also enable everything built on top of the pattern: time-travel debugging (replaying past actions requires previous states to be untouched snapshots, not mutated away), predictable testing (a pure reducer is just input → output, no setup needed), and middleware that can safely inspect or replay actions without side effects sneaking in.

Say it like this

"Redux detects changes by comparing references, so a mutation that keeps the same reference means subscribers never get notified and the UI silently goes stale. Immutability and purity are also what make time-travel debugging and predictable testing possible — you can only replay past states reliably if previous states were never mutated out from under you."

DocRedux Fundamentals — Immutability

088●●●○Very commonZustand vs Redux

What's the actual architectural difference between something like Zustand and Redux, beyond "less boilerplate"?

Redux enforces a strict unidirectional pattern: actions are plain serializable objects, dispatched to a single reducer, which is what makes middleware, dev-tools time travel, and action logging possible in a structured way. Zustand is intentionally minimal — a store is just a hook backed by a plain function that returns state and setters, with no requirement for actions or a single root reducer, and components subscribe via selectors so only the parts of state they actually read cause a re-render.

The practical trade-off: Redux's structure pays off in large teams and apps where the discipline of "every state change is a traceable, replayable action" genuinely matters. Zustand's flexibility is faster to start with and has noticeably less ceremony, but you lose some of that enforced structure and traceability unless you deliberately reintroduce conventions around it.

Say it like this

"Redux enforces action-based, single-reducer unidirectional flow, which is what makes time-travel debugging and middleware possible in a structured way. Zustand skips that ceremony — a store is just a hook with selector-based subscriptions, so only the components reading a specific slice re-render. I'd pick Redux where the traceability of every state change genuinely matters at scale, and Zustand when I want less boilerplate and the app doesn't need that level of structure."

DocZustand — Introduction

089●●●○Very commonContext performance

Why is context described as "not a state management tool" in the React docs — what's it actually for, and where does it fall short as one?

Context solves dependency injection — passing a value down through the tree without threading it prop by prop. It was never designed for high-frequency updates: every value change re-renders every consumer of that context, with no built-in equivalent of a selector to subscribe to just part of the value, and no built-in mechanism to batch or deduplicate updates the way a proper store does.

It works fine for low-frequency values — theme, locale, authenticated user — where the update rate is low and most consumers genuinely need the whole value anyway. It falls short for something like frequently-changing form state or a real-time data feed shared across many components, where you'd want selective subscriptions to avoid cascading re-renders.

Say it like this

"Context is dependency injection, not a state manager — it solves 'get this value to a deeply nested component' but has no selector mechanism, so every consumer re-renders on every change to the value, whole object, no matter which field they actually use. It's fine for low-frequency values like theme or the current user; I wouldn't put frequently-changing state in it without also solving that re-render problem some other way."

DocPassing data deeply with context — react.dev

090●●○○CommonNormalization

Why normalize nested API data in your store instead of keeping the nested shape the API returned?

Nested data (a list of posts, each with a full embedded author object) duplicates the same entity in multiple places. Updating that author's name means finding and updating every nested copy, and it's easy to miss one, leaving the UI showing inconsistent data for the same underlying entity in different places.

Normalizing — storing entities in flat lookup tables keyed by id ({ posts: {byId}, authors: {byId} }), with relationships expressed as ids rather than nested objects — means each entity exists exactly once. An update touches one place and every component reading that entity by id sees the change, and it also makes memoized selectors more effective since unrelated entities don't get new references when one entity changes.

Say it like this

"Nested API shapes duplicate entities across the tree, so updating one instance means finding every copy or risking inconsistent UI. Normalizing into flat, id-keyed tables means each entity exists once — one update, every consumer sees it — and it also keeps selectors more stable, since changing one entity doesn't create new references for entities that didn't actually change."

DocNormalizing State Shape — Redux docs

091●●○○CommonSelectors

What problem does a memoized selector (like Reselect) solve that a plain function computing derived data doesn't?

A plain function that derives data from the store (say, filtering and sorting a list) recomputes on every call, even if the underlying inputs haven't changed — and if that derived array or object is used as a prop or dependency, its new reference on every render defeats any memoization downstream, exactly like the reference-equality problem elsewhere in React.

A memoized selector caches its result and only recomputes when its actual input values change, returning the same reference otherwise — so components reading the derived data via the selector don't re-render or re-run effects just because an unrelated part of the store changed.

Say it like this

"A plain derived-data function recomputes and returns a new reference every single call, which cascades into unnecessary re-renders downstream if that's used as a prop or dependency. A memoized selector caches based on its actual inputs and returns the same reference when nothing relevant changed, so it stops that cascade at the source instead of relying on every consumer to individually deep-compare."

DocDeriving Data with Selectors — Redux docs

092●●○○CommonOptimistic updates

What's an optimistic update, and how do you handle it correctly when the server request fails?

An optimistic update applies the expected result to the UI immediately, before the server confirms it, so the interaction feels instant instead of waiting on network round-trip latency — liking a post shows the filled heart right away, not after the request resolves.

Correctness depends on handling the failure path: you need to keep a snapshot of the previous state before applying the optimistic change, and on error, roll back to that snapshot and surface the failure to the user (a toast, an inline error) rather than leaving the UI showing a confident state that never actually happened server-side.

Say it like this

"I apply the expected outcome to the UI immediately so it feels instant, but I keep a snapshot of the prior state before doing so. If the request fails, I roll back to that snapshot and surface the error — the mistake I actively avoid is applying the optimistic change and just leaving it there on failure, which shows the user something that never actually happened."

DocOptimistic Updates — TanStack Query docs

093●●○○CommonURL as state

When should a piece of UI state live in the URL (query params) instead of component state?

Whenever a user reasonably expects the state to survive a page refresh, or expects to be able to share a link that reproduces exactly what they're seeing — filters on a search page, the active tab, pagination, a selected date range. If you'd be annoyed that reloading the page or sending the link to a coworker loses your place, it belongs in the URL.

The trade-off is added complexity: parsing and validating query params, keeping them in sync with component state (or using them directly as the source of truth via router hooks), and deciding what happens with invalid or missing params. Purely ephemeral UI state that nobody needs to bookmark or share — a tooltip's visibility, an in-progress unsaved form draft — has no reason to pollute the URL.

Say it like this

"My test is whether a user would expect a refresh or a shared link to reproduce what they're looking at — filters, the active tab, a page number. If losing that on refresh would be annoying, it goes in the URL as the source of truth, usually through the router's own hooks rather than duplicating it into separate component state. Purely transient stuff, like a tooltip being open, has no reason to live there."

DocuseSearchParams — React Router docs

094●●○○CommonImmer

How does Immer let you write mutation-looking code (state.user.name = 'x') while still producing an immutable update?

Immer gives you a draft object — a Proxy that intercepts every property access and assignment you make. When you "mutate" the draft, Immer records the operations instead of applying them to the real object, and at the end produces a brand-new immutable object with exactly those changes applied via structural sharing — unmodified parts of the tree keep their original references, only the parts you touched get new ones.

This matters most for deeply nested state, where the "correct" immutable update requires spreading every level down to the changed field — Immer removes that boilerplate while keeping the actual output fully immutable and reference-stable for React and Redux's comparison logic.

Say it like this

"Immer hands you a Proxy-backed draft that records what looks like direct mutation, then replays those changes to produce a genuinely new object using structural sharing — parts you didn't touch keep the same reference, only the changed path gets new objects. It's mainly a boilerplate win for deeply nested updates, where hand-written immutable spreading gets unreadable fast, without giving up the reference-stability React and Redux depend on."

DocImmer — Introduction

095●○○○OccasionalRecoil/Jotai

What problem do atomic state libraries like Jotai or Recoil solve that context and Redux don't handle as elegantly?

Both context and a single Redux store centralize state as one (or a few) large blobs, which means dependent-derivation and fine-grained subscription have to be solved on top, manually (selectors, splitting contexts). Atomic libraries flip the model: state is defined as many small, independent atoms, and components subscribe only to the specific atoms they read — no selector layer needed, because granularity is the default, not something bolted on.

Atoms can also derive from other atoms declaratively, forming a dependency graph that automatically recomputes and re-notifies only the affected subscribers, which is closer to how a spreadsheet recalculates than to a single reducer processing every action.

Say it like this

"They flip state from one big object into many small independent atoms, so fine-grained subscription is the default rather than something you build with selectors afterward. Derived atoms form a dependency graph that recomputes and re-notifies automatically, which fits really well for UI with a lot of small, interdependent pieces of state — it's a different mental model from a single store and a single reducer."

DocJotai — Introduction

096●●○○CommonForm state

Why do form libraries like React Hook Form avoid putting every keystroke into React state?

A fully controlled form with each field's value in useState triggers a re-render of the form (or at least that field's subtree) on every keystroke, across every field. For a large form, that's a lot of unnecessary render work for something the user is just typing into.

React Hook Form instead keeps inputs largely uncontrolled, using refs to read values directly from the DOM and subscribing components to updates only when needed (like showing a validation error), which avoids per-keystroke re-renders of the whole form while still giving you validation, submission handling, and error state when you actually need to react to it.

Say it like this

"Fully controlling every field means a re-render on every keystroke across the whole form, which is wasted work for a large form. React Hook Form keeps fields mostly uncontrolled and reads values via refs, subscribing to updates only where something actually needs to react — like a validation message — so typing doesn't cascade into re-rendering fields that have nothing to do with what changed."

DocReact Hook Form — Get Started

097●●○○CommonPersisted state

A user fills out a long multi-step form and accidentally closes the tab. How would you prevent losing their progress?

Persist the form's draft state to localStorage (or sessionStorage, depending on whether it should survive across sessions) on every meaningful change, debounced so you're not writing on every keystroke, and rehydrate from it on mount before the user starts filling anything in. On successful submission, clear the persisted draft so a stale one doesn't resurface later.

Worth calling out as an edge case: this needs a strategy for sensitive fields (never persist a password or payment field to local storage) and for staleness (if the draft is from three weeks ago, you probably want to prompt the user rather than silently restoring it).

Say it like this

"I'd debounce-write the draft to localStorage on change and rehydrate on mount, then clear it on successful submit so it doesn't resurface later. Two things I'd flag proactively: never persist sensitive fields like a password, and for a draft that's old, I'd rather prompt the user to confirm restoring it than silently resurrect something from weeks ago."

DoclocalStorage — MDN

098●○○○OccasionalFinite state machines

A component has booleans like isLoading, isError, and isSuccess that can theoretically all be true at once even though that's nonsensical. What's the fix?

Multiple independent booleans describing what's really one mutually-exclusive status create representable-but-invalid states — nothing stops isLoading and isSuccess from both being true simultaneously, and the component then has to defensively decide which one "wins." Modeling it as a single status enum ('idle' | 'loading' | 'success' | 'error'), or a proper finite state machine for more complex transitions, makes invalid combinations structurally impossible instead of just "shouldn't happen in practice."

Say it like this

"Separate booleans for what's really one status let you construct nonsense states like loading and success both true, and then the component has to guess which one to trust. I collapse them into a single status field — a union type — so only one state can be true at a time by construction. For anything with more complex valid transitions between states, that's exactly the case for a proper state machine library like XState."

DocXState — Introduction

099●○○○OccasionalCross-tab sync

How would you keep a piece of state in sync across multiple open browser tabs of the same app?

The lightweight approach is the storage event: writing to localStorage in one tab fires a storage event in every other open tab (not the tab that made the write), so each tab can listen for it and update its own in-memory state to match. For richer coordination — like knowing exactly how many tabs are open, or passing messages rather than just a stored value — BroadcastChannel gives every tab a shared pub/sub channel without going through localStorage at all.

Say it like this

"For a simple synced value, I write to localStorage and listen for the storage event in other tabs — it fires everywhere except the tab that made the change, so I use it to update local state to match. For anything closer to actual cross-tab messaging rather than just mirroring a stored value, BroadcastChannel is the more direct tool."

Docstorage event — MDN

100●●○○CommonTesting state

Why is a reducer easier to unit test than the equivalent logic spread across several useState setters and handlers?

A reducer is a pure function: given a state and an action, it returns a new state — no rendering, no DOM, no React runtime involved at all. Testing it is just calling the function with inputs and asserting on the output, exactly like testing any other pure function.

Logic scattered across multiple useState setters inside event handlers is entangled with the component's render cycle — testing it properly means rendering the component (via something like React Testing Library), simulating the interaction, and asserting on the resulting DOM, which is a heavier, slower, and more indirect way to verify what's fundamentally just "given this input, what's the next state."

Say it like this

"A reducer is a pure function — call it with a state and an action, assert on the result, no rendering involved. Logic spread across useState setters in handlers is coupled to the component, so testing it properly means rendering the whole thing and simulating interaction through the DOM. Centralizing the transition logic in a reducer turns a rendering test into a plain function test, which is faster and more direct."

DocExtracting state logic into a reducer — react.dev

101●●○○CommonFormik vs React Hook Form

Given React Hook Form's uncontrolled, ref-based approach covered earlier, what does Formik do differently, and why has RHF become the more common default for new projects?

Formik predates React Hook Form and takes the more traditional controlled-inputs approach — form values live in Formik's own managed state, and every field update flows through that state, which is conceptually simpler to reason about but means it inherits the same per-keystroke re-render cost across the form that the earlier controlled-versus-uncontrolled question describes, since Formik's Field components are controlled by design.

React Hook Form's uncontrolled, ref-based design was specifically built to avoid that re-render cost, which is the primary reason it's become the more common default for new projects, especially anything with large or performance-sensitive forms — the trade-off is that RHF's API leans more on refs and imperative registration (register('fieldName')) which can feel less immediately intuitive than Formik's more declarative Field/Formik component model to someone coming from a fully-controlled mental model. Existing Formik codebases don't necessarily need to migrate purely for this reason unless the re-render cost is actually a measured problem — the same "don't rewrite without a real, measured reason" principle from the styling-migration question applies here too.

Say it like this

"Formik takes the traditional controlled approach — values live in its managed state and every update re-renders through that, which is simple to reason about but inherits the same per-keystroke re-render cost controlled forms generally have. React Hook Form's uncontrolled, ref-based design specifically avoids that, which is why it's become the more common default, especially for large forms. I wouldn't necessarily migrate an existing Formik codebase for this alone unless the re-render cost were an actual measured problem, not just a theoretical one."

DocFormik — Overview

102●●○○CommonRTK Query

Given both TanStack Query and RTK Query solve server-state caching, what's the actual reason a team already using Redux might specifically choose RTK Query over TanStack Query?

Both solve the same fundamental problem — caching, deduplication, background refetching for server state — but RTK Query is built as part of Redux Toolkit and integrates directly into the existing Redux store: cached data, loading states, and cache tags all live inside the same store a Redux-based app already has, visible in the same Redux DevTools timeline the team is already using to debug everything else, rather than introducing a second, separate state system alongside Redux for server data specifically.

The practical decision point is whether the app is already committed to Redux for its client state. If it is, RTK Query avoids running two conceptually different caching/state systems side by side and keeps debugging (time-travel, action logging) unified in one place. If the app has little or no existing Redux investment, there's no real advantage to introducing Redux Toolkit purely to get RTK Query — TanStack Query stands entirely on its own with no dependency on any particular state management library, which makes it the more natural default absent an existing Redux codebase.

Say it like this

"They solve the same core problem, but RTK Query lives inside the existing Redux store, so cached data and loading state show up in the same Redux DevTools timeline a Redux-based team is already using, instead of running a second, separate caching system alongside Redux. That's a real advantage specifically if the app is already committed to Redux — it keeps everything in one debugging story. If there's no existing Redux investment, I wouldn't introduce Redux Toolkit just to get RTK Query — TanStack Query stands alone with no dependency on any state library, so it's the more natural default otherwise."

DocRTK Query Overview — Redux Toolkit docs

103●●○○CommonMobX

MobX takes a fundamentally different approach to state than Redux/Zustand's explicit-action model. What's the actual difference in how updates propagate, and what's the trade-off?

Redux and Zustand are explicit: state changes only through dispatched actions or setter calls, and components subscribe to specific slices, with the library explicitly comparing references (or running selectors) to decide what re-renders. MobX takes a reactive, observable approach instead: state is wrapped in observables, and MobX automatically tracks which observables a component actually reads during render (via a Proxy-based mechanism), then automatically re-renders exactly that component when any of the specific observables it read changes — mutating an observable directly (user.name = 'new') is not just allowed but the normal way to update state, a sharp contrast to the strict immutability Redux enforces.

The trade-off: MobX's automatic dependency tracking means less boilerplate and often better out-of-the-box render granularity (it inherently only re-renders components that read the specific piece that changed, without manually-written selectors), but the "just mutate it" model trades away the predictability and debuggability benefits that come from Redux's strict unidirectional, action-logged flow — time-travel debugging and a clear audit trail of "what action caused this change" are much more natural in an explicit-action model than in a reactive-mutation one, where changes can originate from many scattered mutation sites rather than a single dispatch point.

Say it like this

"Redux and Zustand are explicit — state changes through dispatched actions or setters, and the library compares references to decide what re-renders. MobX is reactive — it wraps state in observables, automatically tracks which ones a component actually read during render, and re-renders exactly that component when one of those changes, and you just mutate the observable directly rather than going through an action. The trade-off is less boilerplate and often better automatic render granularity, against losing the predictability and clear audit trail that Redux's strict action-based flow gives you — time-travel debugging fits an explicit-action model much more naturally than scattered direct mutations."

DocMobX — Introduction

Block 6

Data fetching & async UI

Almost every real app is a UI on top of a network call, and almost every interview probes how you handle the parts that go wrong: race conditions, loading states, retries. Fourteen questions.

104●●●●Near-certainReact Query

What does React Query actually give you beyond "a nicer useEffect fetch wrapper"?

A hand-rolled useEffect fetch handles the single-request case, but real apps need: caching so the same query isn't refetched redundantly across components, request deduplication when multiple components mount and request the same data simultaneously, background refetching (on window focus, on reconnect, on an interval) to keep data fresh without a full page reload, and stale-while-revalidate semantics so the UI shows cached data instantly while a fresh copy loads behind it.

It also standardizes the loading/error/success lifecycle and cancellation, so you're not hand-writing the ignore-flag cleanup pattern in every single fetch effect across the app.

Say it like this

"It's not just DX sugar — it solves caching, deduplication across components requesting the same data, and background refetching, all of which you'd otherwise reimplement by hand per feature. The stale-while-revalidate model alone changes the perceived performance of the app, since users see cached data instantly instead of a spinner on every navigation back to a screen they've already visited."

DocTanStack Query overview

105●●●○Very commonRace conditions

Besides the ignore-flag pattern, how does AbortController help with fetch race conditions, and what's the actual benefit over just ignoring the stale response?

The ignore-flag pattern lets the stale request finish and just discards its result — the network request still completes, wasting bandwidth and server work. AbortController actually cancels the in-flight request: calling .abort() on the signal causes the underlying fetch to reject and, more importantly, tells the browser to stop the request at the network level.

The cleanup function creates a fresh controller and aborts the previous one when a dependency changes, which combines correctness (no stale data ever applies) with efficiency (no wasted requests actually complete).

useEffect(() => {
  const controller = new AbortController();
  fetch(`/api/search?q=${query}`, { signal: controller.signal })
    .then(res => res.json())
    .then(setResults)
    .catch(err => { if (err.name !== 'AbortError') setError(err); });
  return () => controller.abort(); // cancels the actual network request
}, [query]);
Say it like this

"The ignore-flag pattern only discards the stale result after it arrives — the request still completes and wastes bandwidth. AbortController actually cancels the request at the network level when a newer one supersedes it, so I get both correctness and efficiency instead of just correctness. It's a small extra step — creating a controller, passing its signal, aborting it in cleanup — for a real improvement."

DocAbortController — MDN

106●●●○Very commonLoading states

What's wrong with a single global isLoading flag for a page that fetches several independent pieces of data?

A single flag ties the entire page's loading state to the slowest request, even if the other four data sources resolved instantly — the user stares at one big spinner for a widget that has nothing to do with the slow one. It also can't express partial success: if one request fails while the others succeed, a single boolean has no room to represent "mostly loaded, one section errored."

The better default is per-section loading and error state, so each independent piece of the UI can render as soon as its own data is ready, and a slow or failed section doesn't hold the rest of the page hostage. If several pieces genuinely need to load together before anything is meaningful to show, then a combined state is correct — but that should be a deliberate choice, not the default because it was easier to write one flag.

Say it like this

"A single global flag couples the whole page to its slowest request and can't represent partial failure. My default is loading and error state scoped per independent section, so each part of the page renders as soon as its own data is ready instead of everything waiting on the slowest one. I'll combine them deliberately only when the sections genuinely have no meaning shown independently."

DocSuspense — react.dev

107●●●○Very commonSuspense

How does <Suspense> for data fetching actually work under the hood — what does a component do to "trigger" the fallback?

A component integrated with Suspense, when its data isn't ready yet, throws a promise during render instead of returning JSX. React catches that thrown promise, shows the nearest ancestor <Suspense> boundary's fallback, and re-attempts rendering the component once the promise resolves. This is why Suspense-compatible data fetching needs library support (React Query's useSuspenseQuery, Relay, a framework's built-in data layer) rather than working with an arbitrary raw fetch — the library has to actually implement the throw-a-promise-and-cache-the-result contract.

The upside over manually checking isLoading in every component is declarative composition: nested Suspense boundaries let you control loading granularity by where you place the boundary in the tree, without threading loading flags through props.

Say it like this

"A Suspense-integrated component throws a promise during render when its data isn't ready; React catches that, shows the nearest fallback, and retries once it resolves. That's why you need a library built for it rather than plain fetch — it has to know how to throw and cache correctly. What I like about it is the declarative composition: where I place the Suspense boundary controls the loading granularity, instead of threading isLoading through every layer."

DocSuspense — react.dev

108●●○○CommonWaterfalls

A page fetches a user, then fetches that user's orders, then fetches details for the first order — each waiting on the previous one to finish. What's this called and how do you fix the parts that don't actually need to be sequential?

It's a request waterfall: each request's start time is gated on the previous one's completion, even when some of them don't have a real data dependency on each other. The total time is the sum of every step's latency instead of the maximum.

The fix is to identify genuine dependencies versus accidental ones. If order details truly need the order id from step two, that dependency is real and can't be removed — but if the page also needs the user's notification preferences, and that doesn't depend on orders at all, it should be requested in parallel from the start with Promise.all, not chained after. In a Suspense/RSC world, this often means fetching independent data at the top of a route in parallel rather than nesting fetches component by component.

Say it like this

"That's a request waterfall — total time becomes the sum of every step instead of the max of the independent ones. My first move is separating genuine data dependencies from accidental sequencing: anything that doesn't actually need a previous response should start immediately in parallel, usually with Promise.all, rather than being chained just because it was written after the previous fetch in the component tree."

DocShowing nested content as it loads — react.dev

109●●○○CommonRetries

If you're implementing automatic retry for a failed request, why should the delay between retries grow, and why add randomness (jitter) to it?

Fixed-interval retries can pile up: if a server is struggling under load and returns errors, every failed client retrying at the exact same fixed interval creates repeated synchronized spikes of traffic hitting the already-struggling server — sometimes called a "thundering herd." Exponential backoff (doubling the delay after each failure) gives the server increasing breathing room to recover instead of hammering it at a constant rate.

Adding jitter — a small random offset to each client's delay — desynchronizes retries from many different clients that failed at roughly the same moment, so they don't all retry in the same instant even with the same backoff schedule, further smoothing out the load instead of trading one synchronized spike pattern for a slightly-less-frequent one.

Say it like this

"Fixed-interval retries from many clients synchronize into repeated traffic spikes against a server that's already struggling. Exponential backoff spaces out each individual client's retries increasingly further apart, and jitter — a bit of randomness on top — desynchronizes different clients from each other, so you don't just trade one spike pattern for a slower one."

DocRetry strategy — Google Cloud docs

110●●○○CommonInfinite scroll

How would you implement infinite scroll without attaching a scroll event listener that fires on every pixel scrolled?

A raw scroll listener fires at a very high frequency and forces you to manually calculate scroll position against document height on every call, which is both wasteful and imprecise. IntersectionObserver instead lets you place an invisible sentinel element near the bottom of the list and get notified — efficiently, off the main thread's synchronous scroll handling — exactly when that element enters the viewport, which is precisely the "user is near the bottom" signal you actually want.

Combined with something like React Query's useInfiniteQuery, the sentinel's intersection callback triggers fetching the next page, and the observer is disconnected once there's no more data or the component unmounts.

Say it like this

"I use IntersectionObserver on a sentinel element near the bottom of the list instead of a raw scroll listener — it fires efficiently only when that element actually enters the viewport, which is exactly the trigger I want, rather than recalculating scroll position on every pixel of scroll. That observer callback triggers the next page fetch, typically through something like useInfiniteQuery to handle the pagination state."

DocIntersection Observer API — MDN

111●●○○CommonCache invalidation

A user creates a new comment, and it should immediately appear in a list fetched by a different query elsewhere on the page. How do you keep them in sync?

Two common approaches with a query caching library. Invalidation: after the mutation succeeds, mark the related query key as stale so it refetches — simple and always correct, but has a brief delay while the refetch happens. Direct cache update: manually write the new comment into the existing cached list for that query key right after the mutation resolves, giving an instant UI update without waiting on a network round-trip, at the cost of you being responsible for keeping the manual update logic correct and in sync with the server's actual behavior (sorting, pagination boundaries, server-computed fields).

The practical default is invalidation for anything where a short delay doesn't matter, and a manual cache update (or combined with an optimistic update) when the interaction specifically needs to feel instant.

Say it like this

"Simplest correct option is invalidating the related query key after the mutation succeeds, so it refetches — a little lag, but always correct. When the interaction needs to feel instant, I'll manually write the new item into the cached list right after the mutation resolves, which is faster but means I own keeping that manual update logic correct against whatever the server would actually return, including sorting and pagination."

DocInvalidations from Mutations — TanStack Query docs

112●○○○OccasionalPolling

When would you choose polling over a WebSocket for "live" data, given that a WebSocket seems strictly more real-time?

Polling is far simpler operationally: it's plain HTTP, works through any proxy or load balancer without special configuration, needs no persistent connection management, reconnection logic, or server-side connection-count concerns, and degrades gracefully — a missed poll just means slightly stale data next interval, not a broken connection state to recover from.

WebSockets earn their complexity when true low-latency, bidirectional communication actually matters — a chat app, a collaborative editor, a live trading dashboard — where the gap between "data changed" and "user sees it" needs to be near-instant. For something like a dashboard that's fine being 30 seconds stale, polling is simpler to build, simpler to debug, and simpler to scale.

Say it like this

"Polling is operationally much simpler — plain HTTP, no persistent connection state to manage, and it fails gracefully as just slightly-stale data. I'd reach for a WebSocket specifically when near-instant bidirectional updates genuinely matter, like chat or a collaborative editor. For something that's fine being a bit stale, like a metrics dashboard, polling is less to build and less to operate."

DocWebSockets API — MDN

113●●○○CommonError handling

Where should a network error be handled: in the fetching hook, the component that calls it, or a global handler — and why not just one place?

It's rarely one place, because different errors need different responses. A global handler is right for cross-cutting concerns that apply everywhere — a 401 should trigger a redirect to login regardless of which request caused it, and a 5xx might trigger a generic toast. But local, per-request handling is needed for errors whose right response is specific to that interaction — a form submission's validation error needs to highlight the specific field, which a global handler has no way to know how to do.

The clean split: a global interceptor (in your HTTP client or query library) for authentication and truly generic failures, and local handling at the call site for errors that need contextual, feature-specific UI.

Say it like this

"I split it: a global interceptor handles cross-cutting cases like a 401 redirecting to login, since that's true regardless of which request triggered it. Anything where the right response is specific to that interaction — a form field that needs to show a particular validation message — has to be handled locally, because a global handler has no context to know what UI that specific failure should produce."

DocQuery Retries — TanStack Query docs

114●○○○OccasionalGraphQL

What real problem does GraphQL solve compared to REST that isn't just "it's a newer technology"?

Two concrete problems.
