Over-fetching and under-fetching: a REST endpoint returns a fixed shape, so a screen needing three fields from a large resource either downloads the whole thing (over-fetching, wasted bandwidth) or the API needs a bespoke endpoint per screen's needs (under-fetching, endpoint sprawl). GraphQL lets the client specify exactly the fields it needs in the query itself.

Second, multiple round trips for related data: fetching a user, then their posts, then each post's comments over REST often means several sequential requests (a waterfall) or a bespoke aggregation endpoint. A single GraphQL query can request that entire nested shape in one round trip, with the server resolving the graph server-side.

The trade-off is real complexity moved to the server (resolver design, the N+1 query problem, caching that isn't as simple as REST's URL-based HTTP caching) — it's not a strict upgrade, it's a different set of trade-offs suited to different problems.

Say it like this

"It directly solves over/under-fetching — the client asks for exactly the fields it needs instead of getting a fixed shape or needing a bespoke endpoint — and it collapses what would be several sequential REST calls for related, nested data into one round trip. The trade-off is real complexity that moves to the server: resolver design, the N+1 problem, and caching that isn't as simple as REST's URL-based model."

DocIntroduction to GraphQL

115●●○○CommonPrefetching

How would you make a "click to view details" navigation feel instant, given the details data isn't loaded yet?

Prefetch the data before the click happens, using a signal that reliably predicts intent: on hover (with a short delay to avoid firing on accidental mouse-overs), on focus for keyboard users, or when the link scrolls into the viewport for a list of cards likely to be visited. With a query caching library, prefetching just means populating the cache for that query key ahead of time, so by the time the actual navigation happens the data is often already sitting in cache and the transition renders instantly instead of showing a spinner.

The trade-off to be deliberate about is wasted requests — prefetching every hover on a page with a hundred list items fires a hundred unnecessary requests for the ones the user never clicks, so the trigger and any debounce/threshold need tuning to the actual UI.

Say it like this

"I'd prefetch on hover or focus, with a short delay so it doesn't fire on every accidental mouseover, and populate the query cache ahead of the actual click. By the time navigation happens the data's often already cached, so the transition feels instant instead of showing a loading state. I'd tune the trigger threshold against how many items are in the list, since naive hover-prefetching on a big list fires a lot of requests nobody asked for."

DocPrefetching & Router Integration — TanStack Query docs

116●○○○OccasionalFile uploads

How do you show upload progress for a large file upload in the browser?

The Fetch API's promise-based interface doesn't expose upload progress events. XMLHttpRequest, despite being older, still exposes upload.onprogress, which fires with loaded/total byte counts you can turn into a percentage — many libraries (like axios) wrap this specifically to give you a progress callback on top of a nicer API.

For very large files, chunked upload with a resumable protocol (splitting the file and uploading pieces, tracking which chunks succeeded) is worth it specifically because it lets an interrupted upload resume from where it left off instead of restarting from zero.

Say it like this

"Plain fetch doesn't expose upload progress, so I use XMLHttpRequest's upload.onprogress directly, or a library like axios that wraps it for the same effect with a nicer API. For genuinely large files I'd look at chunked, resumable uploads, mainly so a dropped connection loses at most one chunk instead of forcing the whole upload to restart."

DocXMLHttpRequest.upload — MDN

117●●○○CommonSkeleton screens

Why do many products prefer skeleton screens over a spinner for loading states?

A spinner conveys almost no information beyond "wait" — it gives no sense of layout, structure, or how much content is coming. A skeleton screen mimics the actual layout that's about to appear (gray blocks where the avatar, title, and text will be), which sets a more accurate expectation and, per UX research, tends to make the same objective wait feel shorter because the brain has something structurally meaningful to anticipate rather than an abstract "loading" symbol.

The practical caveat: a skeleton for content that loads in under roughly 200-300ms is often net negative — it flashes briefly and just adds visual noise, so it's usually worth a short delay before showing it, so fast loads skip the skeleton entirely.

Say it like this

"A skeleton previews the actual layout that's coming, which sets a more accurate expectation than an abstract spinner and tends to make the wait feel shorter. The one thing I'm careful about is not showing it for very fast loads — a skeleton that flashes for 100ms is just visual noise, so I usually delay showing it slightly so quick responses skip it entirely."

DocRAIL performance model — web.dev

GraphQL deep-dive

For a team that's actually chosen GraphQL, the interview goes past "what problem does it solve" into the client-side mechanics that make it different from a REST data layer.

118●●○○CommonFragments

What's a GraphQL fragment for, and why does colocating a fragment with the component that uses it matter?

A fragment is a reusable, named subset of fields on a type — instead of every query that needs a user's basic info re-listing id name avatarUrl, they all reference one UserBasicInfo fragment. The deeper value beyond avoiding repetition is colocation: defining the fragment right next to the component that actually renders those fields means the component's data dependency lives in the same file as the component itself, so a component and the fields it needs never drift apart the way they can when a giant, centrally-maintained query tries to guess what every component down the tree will need.

The parent query composes the fragments its children declared rather than the child receiving arbitrary props from a query it had no say in — this is the GraphQL-specific version of the general principle that a component should own its own data requirements rather than trusting an ancestor to have guessed correctly.

Say it like this

"A fragment is a reusable named set of fields, but the real value is colocation — defining it next to the component that uses it means the component's data needs live in the same file as the component, so they can't silently drift apart the way they do when one giant central query tries to guess what every descendant needs. Parent queries compose the fragments their children declared, so each component genuinely owns its own data requirements instead of trusting an ancestor to have gotten it right."

DocFragments — GraphQL.org

119●●○○CommonNormalized cache

How does a normalized GraphQL cache (Apollo, Relay) know to update a component when a completely unrelated mutation happens to touch the same underlying entity?

Both cache every entity flatly, keyed by a global identifier (typically derived from the type name plus an id field, like User:42) — conceptually the same normalization principle covered earlier for Redux state, but applied automatically by the GraphQL client rather than something you build by hand. Every query result gets decomposed into these normalized entities on the way in, and every component reading a query is really reading a computed view over that shared normalized store.

When a mutation's response includes updated fields for User:42, the client writes that update into the single normalized cache entry for that entity — and because every component whose query touched User:42, anywhere in the app, is subscribed to that same underlying normalized entry, they all recompute and re-render with the fresh data automatically, with no manual cache-invalidation wiring needed between the mutation and the unrelated component, unlike the manual invalidation strategies needed for a plain REST cache.

Say it like this

"The client normalizes every entity into a flat store keyed by something like type-plus-id, so every query result gets decomposed into those shared normalized entries rather than kept as separate nested blobs per query. When a mutation updates that entity, it writes to the one normalized entry, and every component anywhere in the app whose query touched that same entity is subscribed to it and automatically re-renders — no manual invalidation wiring needed between the mutation and an otherwise-unrelated component, which is the real advantage over a REST cache's typically more manual invalidation."

DocApollo Client Cache Overview

120●○○○OccasionalN+1 problem

What's the N+1 query problem in GraphQL, and why is it a server concern rather than something the frontend can fix?

If a query asks for a list of posts and each post's author, a naively-implemented resolver fetches the list of posts (1 query), then independently fetches the author for each post one at a time (N more queries) — for a hundred posts, that's a hundred and one separate database round trips for data that could have been fetched far more efficiently in a couple of batched queries. This is purely a server-side resolver implementation concern: the client just asked for a shape of data, and how the server's resolvers actually fulfill that request — one query at a time versus batched — is entirely the server's responsibility.

The standard server-side fix is a batching/caching layer (the DataLoader pattern is the canonical solution) that collects all the individual "give me the author for this post" requests that happen within a single tick and batches them into one efficient query instead of firing them individually. It's worth knowing this exists and why it matters even as a frontend engineer, because a slow GraphQL API is sometimes actually this problem on the resolver side, not anything about how the query itself was written on the client.

Say it like this

"If a query asks for a list plus a related field per item, a naive resolver fetches the list once and then fetches the related data one at a time per item — N extra round trips for N items. It's purely a server-side resolver concern, not something the client query itself causes or can fix, and the standard fix is a batching layer like DataLoader that collects those per-item requests within a tick and resolves them as one efficient batched query instead. Worth knowing as a frontend engineer mainly because a slow API is sometimes exactly this, not anything about how the client query was written."

DocDataLoader

121●○○○OccasionalOptimistic mutations

How does an optimistic response in Apollo/Relay differ from the general optimistic-update pattern covered earlier for a plain REST mutation?

The underlying principle is identical — show the expected result immediately, roll back on failure — but a GraphQL client's optimistic response integrates directly with the normalized cache rather than needing you to manually patch a specific query's cached list. You provide a shape of what the mutation's response is expected to look like, and the client writes that shape into the normalized cache exactly as if the real response had already arrived — every component reading that entity anywhere in the app updates immediately and consistently, the same automatic propagation covered in the normalized-cache question above, rather than needing to manually find and patch every place that entity happens to be displayed.

On failure, the client automatically rolls the normalized cache back to its prior state for exactly the fields the optimistic response touched, which is more reliable than a hand-rolled snapshot-and-restore for a REST cache, since the GraphQL client already knows precisely which normalized entries the mutation's optimistic shape touched.

Say it like this

"The principle is the same as any optimistic update — show the expected result immediately, roll back on failure — but it plugs directly into the normalized cache, so the optimistic shape gets written into the same cache every component reads from, and every place that entity's displayed updates consistently and automatically, not just one query's cached list I'd have to manually find and patch. Rollback on failure is also more reliable, since the client already knows exactly which normalized entries the optimistic write touched."

DocOptimistic Mutation Results — Apollo Client docs

122●○○○OccasionalPersisted queries

What's a persisted query, and what problem does it solve for a GraphQL API compared to sending the full query string on every request?

A raw GraphQL request typically sends the entire query document as a string in the request body on every call, which for a complex query can be a meaningfully larger payload than the equivalent REST request's URL and params, and — since the client can send an arbitrary query string — gives the server less predictability and control over exactly what shapes of queries it needs to support and optimize for. A persisted query instead registers each unique query string ahead of time (typically at build time) and has the client send only a short hash identifying it, with the server looking up the actual query document server-side by that hash.

This solves two things at once: it meaningfully shrinks request payload size, especially valuable on slow networks, and it lets a server restrict itself to only the finite, known set of persisted queries an allowlist defines, which closes off arbitrary ad-hoc queries from being sent to production — a real security and predictability benefit beyond just the bandwidth savings.

Say it like this

"Instead of sending the full query string on every request, persisted queries register each unique query ahead of time and have the client send just a short hash, with the server looking up the actual query server-side. That shrinks payload size, which matters on slow networks, and it also lets the server restrict itself to an allowlist of known queries, closing off arbitrary ad-hoc queries in production — a real security benefit on top of the bandwidth savings, not just a performance trick."

DocPersisted Queries — Apollo docs

Block 7

Routing & app structure

Less frequent than hooks or state, but a reliable filter question for anyone who's only ever worked on single-page toy apps. Ten questions.

123●●●○Very commonClient-side routing

How does client-side routing avoid a full page reload when the URL changes?

The router intercepts link clicks (calling preventDefault on the browser's default navigation) and instead calls the History API's pushState, which changes the URL shown in the address bar and adds a browser history entry without triggering an actual network request or page reload. The router then reads the new URL and renders the matching component tree client-side.

Back/forward browser buttons are handled by listening for the popstate event, which fires when the user navigates history without the app itself calling pushState, so the router can re-sync its rendered view to match.

Say it like this

"The router intercepts navigation and uses pushState to update the URL and history without an actual page load, then renders the matching route client-side. Back and forward buttons are handled separately by listening for popstate, which fires whenever history changes outside of the app's own pushState calls, so the router can catch up to it."

DocHistory API — MDN

124●●●○Very commonCode splitting

Why is route-based code splitting usually the first optimization to reach for in a growing app, before component-level splitting?

Route boundaries are a natural place to split because a user visiting one route genuinely doesn't need the JavaScript for every other route yet — the split aligns exactly with what the user actually needs at that moment, with no complexity in deciding where the boundary should be. React.lazy combined with the router's route definitions means each route's code (and its own dependencies) only downloads when that route is actually visited, shrinking the initial bundle to just what's needed for the first screen.

Component-level splitting (lazy-loading a heavy modal or chart within a page) is a finer-grained optimization worth doing later, once route splitting alone isn't enough — but it requires more judgment about where the split actually pays off versus just adding loading-state complexity for something small.

Say it like this

"Route boundaries are the obvious first split because they map exactly to what a user actually needs right now — there's no judgment call about where to draw the line. I wire up React.lazy per route so the initial bundle only contains the first screen's code, and only reach for component-level splitting afterward, for something genuinely heavy like a chart library, once route splitting alone isn't enough."

Doclazy — react.dev

125●●○○CommonProtected routes

How would you implement a "protected route" that redirects unauthenticated users to login, and what's the subtlety with the loading state?

The naive version checks an isAuthenticated boolean and redirects if false — but if that boolean starts as false before an async auth check (like validating a token) has resolved, every protected route briefly flashes a redirect to login before flipping back once the check completes, which is a jarring, incorrect UX for users who actually are logged in.

The fix is a three-state model, not a boolean: loading, authenticated, unauthenticated. While loading, render nothing or a spinner — don't redirect yet. Only redirect once the check has actually resolved to unauthenticated.

function ProtectedRoute({ children }) {
  const { status } = useAuth(); // 'loading' | 'authenticated' | 'unauthenticated'
  if (status === 'loading') return <Spinner />;
  if (status === 'unauthenticated') return <Navigate to="/login" />;
  return children;
}
Say it like this

"The mistake I watch for is modeling auth status as a boolean that defaults to false, which flashes a redirect for authenticated users before the async check resolves. I use a three-state status — loading, authenticated, unauthenticated — and only redirect once it's actually resolved to unauthenticated, showing a spinner or nothing meaningful during the loading state instead."

DocNavigate — React Router docs

126●●○○CommonNested routes

What's the benefit of nested routes with an <Outlet /> versus each page independently rendering its own layout?

Without nested routing, every page component has to import and render its own header, sidebar, and footer, duplicating that markup and making a layout change require touching every page. Nested routes let a parent route own the shared layout once and render an <Outlet /> where the matched child route's content goes — the layout persists across navigations between its child routes without re-mounting, so things like a sidebar's scroll position or an open state naturally survive navigation within that section.

It also means loading and error boundaries can be scoped per route level: a nested route's own loader failing doesn't have to blow away the parent layout around it.

Say it like this

"A parent route owns the shared layout once and renders an Outlet for whichever child route matched, instead of every page duplicating header and sidebar markup. Because the parent doesn't remount when you navigate between its children, things like sidebar scroll position or a persistent player naturally survive navigation, which you'd lose if each page rendered the whole layout independently."

DocOutlet — React Router docs

127●●○○CommonData loading

What's the difference between fetching data inside a component with useEffect after it mounts, versus a router-level loader that fetches before the route renders?

The useEffect approach means the component has to mount and render first — usually showing a loading state — before the effect even starts fetching. That's sequential: render, then fetch, then render again with data. A route-level loader (as in React Router's data APIs, or a framework's route loader) starts the fetch before the route's components render at all, often in parallel with downloading the route's code, so the data-fetching and the component-mounting aren't strictly sequential.

The practical benefit is avoiding the "render → fetch → render" waterfall for the initial page load specifically, and it also means the router can hold off rendering the new route until data is ready, avoiding an empty-then-populated flash for straightforward cases.

Say it like this

"useEffect-based fetching is inherently sequential — the component has to render first before the effect even starts. A router loader kicks off the fetch before the route's components render, often overlapping with the route's code loading, which avoids the render-then-fetch-then-render waterfall for the initial navigation. It's a meaningful difference specifically for that first paint of a route, not for updates that happen after the user's already there."

Docloader — React Router docs

128●○○○OccasionalScroll restoration

A user scrolls down a list, clicks into a detail page, then hits the browser back button. What has to happen for scroll position to feel correct, and what's the tricky part?

The expected behavior is: forward navigation to a new route resets scroll to the top, but navigating back should restore the scroll position the list was at before the user left. Most router libraries provide a scroll-restoration mechanism that stores scroll position keyed by history entry and restores it on back/forward navigation.

The tricky part is timing: if the list's content is fetched asynchronously and hasn't rendered yet when the restoration attempt happens, there's nothing tall enough on the page yet to scroll to — restoration has to be deferred until the content that was there before is actually back in the DOM, not just attempted immediately on navigation.

Say it like this

"Forward navigation should reset to top, back navigation should restore where you were, and most routers give you that via a scroll-restoration mechanism keyed to history entries. The part that actually needs care is timing — if the list re-fetches on the way back and hasn't rendered yet, there's no content tall enough to scroll to, so restoration has to wait for that content to actually be back in the DOM."

DocScrollRestoration — React Router docs

129●●○○Common404 handling

What's the difference between a client-side "not found" component and an actual HTTP 404 status?

A purely client-rendered SPA that shows a "Page not found" component still returns HTTP 200 for the initial document, because the server has no idea the client-side router is about to decide the route doesn't exist — the server just served the one static index.html it always serves. That matters for search engines and any tooling that checks HTTP status: a 200 response signals "this page exists and is fine" even though a human sees a not-found message.

Getting a real 404 status code requires server involvement — either server-side rendering that knows the route doesn't match and sets the status explicitly, or a static-hosting rewrite rule that still lets a catch-all route respond with the correct status. Purely client-side routing structurally cannot produce a correct HTTP status, because the status is decided before any JavaScript runs.

Say it like this

"A client-only SPA's initial response is always 200, because the server serves the same index.html regardless of what route the client-side router later decides is unmatched — the 'not found' is purely a client-rendered state, invisible to anything checking the HTTP status. Getting an actual 404 status requires server involvement, which is one of the concrete reasons I'd reach for SSR on a route where SEO or correct status codes matter."

Doc404 Not Found — MDN

130●○○○OccasionalRoute-based state

You navigate from a list page to a detail page and back. Should the list's filters and scroll position reset, and how do you control that?

The answer depends entirely on whether the list component actually unmounts during that navigation, which depends on your route structure. If the detail page is a fully separate route outside the list's tree, the list unmounts and any local state (filters, scroll) is lost by default unless you've explicitly persisted it (URL params, a store, or session storage). If the detail view is instead rendered as a sibling or overlay within the same layout — nested routing, or a modal route — the list component can stay mounted and naturally keeps its state.

This is a design decision, not just an implementation detail: whether the interaction should feel like "leaving the list" or "looking closer at one item while staying on the list" should drive the routing structure, not the other way around.

Say it like this

"It comes down to whether the list actually unmounts on that navigation, which is a routing structure decision, not an afterthought. If I want the filters and scroll to survive, I either keep the list mounted with a nested or modal route so it never unmounts, or I deliberately persist that state to the URL or a store if the detail view genuinely needs to be a separate route."

DocNested routes — React Router docs

131●●○○CommonDeep linking

Why is it a mistake to rely purely on in-memory navigation state (like state passed via a navigate call) to pass data to the next page?

State passed through navigation (like React Router's navigate(path, {"{"}state{"}"})) only exists because the router held onto it in memory during a client-side transition. The moment a user refreshes the page, opens the URL in a new tab, or shares the link with someone else, that in-memory state is simply gone — there's no representation of it in the URL itself, so a "deep link" to that exact page state doesn't actually work.

Anything that needs to survive a refresh or be shareable has to be encoded somewhere durable: query params in the URL, or refetched from an id that is in the URL, rather than passed as transient navigation state.

Say it like this

"Navigation state only exists in memory for that one client-side transition — refresh, a new tab, or sharing the link, and it's gone, because none of it is actually encoded in the URL. I use it only for things that are fine to lose on refresh, like a 'came from' flag for a back button; anything that needs to survive a reload or be shareable goes in the URL as a param or an id the page can refetch by."

DocuseNavigate — React Router docs

132●●○○CommonRoute guards

A user is mid-way through editing a form and clicks a link to navigate away. How would you warn them about losing unsaved changes?

For in-app navigation (clicking a link within the SPA), the router itself can intercept the transition — React Router's blocker APIs let you pause a navigation, show a confirmation dialog, and either proceed or cancel based on the user's choice, since the router controls that transition entirely.

For leaving the site or tab entirely (closing the tab, typing a new URL, refreshing), that's outside the router's control — you need the native beforeunload event, which can prompt the browser's own confirmation dialog. The two mechanisms are separate and both are usually needed for complete coverage, since neither one covers the other's case.

Say it like this

"For navigation within the app, I use the router's own blocking mechanism to intercept the transition and show a custom confirmation before letting it proceed. For leaving the page entirely — closing the tab, refreshing — that's outside the router's reach, so I also wire up beforeunload for the browser's native confirmation. Neither one substitutes for the other, so a real implementation needs both."

Docbeforeunload event — MDN

Block 8

Performance & rendering

The block where "I know the hooks API" and "I actually understand how React renders" separate cleanly. Sixteen questions.

133●●●●Near-certainReact.memo

How does React.memo decide to skip a re-render, and what's the most common way people accidentally defeat it?

React.memo wraps a component and, before re-rendering it when its parent re-renders, shallowly compares each new prop to the previous prop by reference (or by a custom comparator if you supply one). If every prop is reference-equal to last time, React skips calling the component function entirely and reuses the previous render output.

The most common way to defeat it: passing an inline object, array, or arrow function as a prop. <Child onClick={() => doThing()} /> creates a brand-new function reference on every parent render, so the shallow comparison always sees "different," and memo never actually skips anything — the memoization silently does nothing while looking like it should be working.

// defeats memo — new function reference every parent render
<ExpensiveChild onSave={() => save(id)} />

// stable reference — memo can actually skip re-rendering
const handleSave = useCallback(() => save(id), [id]);
<ExpensiveChild onSave={handleSave} />
Say it like this

"memo shallow-compares props by reference and skips the render if everything matches. The classic way to accidentally defeat it is an inline function or object prop — it's a new reference every parent render, so the comparison always fails and memo does nothing while looking like it should be helping. I pair memo with useCallback or useMemo specifically on whatever props are getting passed down, or I just don't bother memoizing that boundary at all."

Docmemo — react.dev

134●●●●Near-certainProfiling

A page feels janky. What's your actual process for finding out why, before writing any optimization code?

Optimizing before measuring is the single most common performance mistake — it's easy to "fix" a component that wasn't the actual bottleneck while leaving the real one untouched. The process starts with the React DevTools Profiler: record an interaction, look at which components rendered, how long each took, and critically, why each one rendered (the Profiler shows the reason — a prop change, a state change, a context update).

From there, distinguish two different problems that need different fixes: a component that renders too often (unnecessary re-renders — the fix is memoization, moving state down, or splitting context) versus a component that renders too slowly even when it should render (an expensive computation during render, a huge list without virtualization — the fix is optimizing that specific work, not preventing the render).

Say it like this

"I always measure before touching anything — the Profiler tells you not just what's slow but why it rendered at all, which matters because 'renders too often' and 'renders too slowly' need completely different fixes. Too often points toward memoization or state placement; too slowly points toward optimizing the actual work happening during render, like virtualizing a huge list. Guessing which one it is without profiling first tends to waste effort on the wrong component."

DocReact DevTools Profiler — react.dev

135●●●●Near-certainVirtualization

Why does rendering a list of 10,000 items get slow, and how does list virtualization actually fix it?

Rendering 10,000 DOM nodes is expensive on multiple fronts: the initial render has to create that many elements, the browser has to lay out and paint all of them, and any subsequent update touching the list has to reconcile against all of them, even though a user's viewport can only physically display maybe twenty at a time — the other 9,980 are rendered work the user never sees at that moment.

Virtualization (via libraries like react-window or @tanstack/react-virtual) only renders the DOM nodes for items currently in (or near) the visible viewport, using absolute positioning and a correctly-sized spacer to make the scrollbar behave as if all 10,000 items were really there. As the user scrolls, items scrolling out get unmounted and new ones scrolling in get mounted — the DOM node count stays roughly constant regardless of total list size.

Say it like this

"The cost scales with total items rendered, not with what's actually visible — a 10,000-item list creates 10,000 DOM nodes even though the viewport shows maybe twenty. Virtualization only mounts the nodes for what's currently near the viewport and uses a correctly-sized spacer to keep scrollbar behavior correct, so the actual DOM node count stays roughly constant no matter how large the underlying list gets."

DocTanStack Virtual — Introduction

136●●●○Very commonBatching

You call setCount and setName in the same event handler. How many re-renders happen, and has this always been true in React?

One re-render — React batches multiple state updates that occur within the same synchronous execution (event handler) into a single re-render pass, rather than re-rendering after each individual set call. This has always been true for updates inside React event handlers, but before React 18, updates inside a setTimeout, a promise callback, or a raw DOM event listener were not batched — each one triggered its own separate render.

React 18's automatic batching extended this to essentially all update sources, so the same "one render" behavior now applies inside timeouts, promises, and native event handlers too, not just React's own synthetic event handlers — a real behavioral change that can surprise code written against older React assumptions.

Say it like this

"Just one — React batches state updates within the same synchronous block into a single render. What changed in React 18 is where batching applies: before, only updates inside React's own event handlers were batched, so the same two calls inside a setTimeout or a promise callback used to trigger two separate renders. Automatic batching in 18 extended the one-render behavior to those cases too."

DocReact 18 — Automatic batching

137●●●○Very commonReconciliation cost

Does React re-render a child component if its parent re-renders but the child's props didn't change?

By default, yes — a component re-rendering causes React to re-render every child in its subtree regardless of whether that child's own props changed at all. This surprises people because it seems wasteful, but it's the correct default: comparing every child's props on every render to decide whether to skip it would itself cost something, and for most components that work is cheap enough that skipping isn't worth the complexity.

"Re-render" here specifically means React calls the child's function and diffs the result — it doesn't necessarily mean the DOM changes, since the diff can still find nothing to update even if the function ran again. React.memo is the explicit opt-in for "actually skip calling this function if props are reference-equal," used specifically where profiling shows that check is worth it.

Say it like this

"Yes, by default — a parent re-rendering cascades to every child regardless of whether that child's specific props changed, and that's intentional, because checking every child's props on every render has its own cost that isn't worth paying by default. Re-render doesn't mean the DOM necessarily changes, just that the function ran again. memo is the opt-in for skipping that function call entirely when props are reference-equal, and I add it where profiling actually shows it matters."

DocRender and commit — react.dev

138●●●○Very commonchildren prop trick

A frequently-updating state lives in a parent, but a heavy sibling component doesn't use that state at all — it still re-renders every time. How do you fix this without adding memo?

This is a case where the composition itself, not memoization, is the real fix. If the heavy component is passed as children (or another prop) from a level above the state, instead of being rendered directly inside the component that owns the fast-changing state, then React sees that children reference as unchanged when only the state-owning wrapper re-renders — because it was created and passed down from an ancestor that didn't itself re-render.

// heavy sibling re-renders every time `open` changes, even though it doesn't use `open`
function Layout() {
  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);
  return (
    <div>
      <Toggle open={open} onToggle={setOpen} />
      <HeavyChart /> {/* re-renders every toggle, unnecessarily */}
    </div>
  );
}

// fix: HeavyChart is passed as children from above the state, so it's untouched
function Layout({ children }) {
  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);
  return <div><Toggle open={open} onToggle={setOpen} />{children}</div>;
}
// caller: <Layout><HeavyChart /></Layout>
Say it like this

"Rather than reaching for memo first, I'd check if this is a composition problem — if the heavy component is passed down as children from a level above the state instead of rendered inside the stateful component, React treats that children reference as unchanged when the state updates, since it wasn't recreated by a re-render. It's a genuinely free optimization that comes from where you put the state, not from memoizing anything."

Docmemo and children — react.dev

139●●○○CommonBundle size

How would you find out why your production bundle is unexpectedly 2MB, and what would you actually look for?

Start with a bundle analyzer (like rollup-plugin-visualizer or webpack-bundle-analyzer) that renders a treemap of what's actually in the output, sized proportionally — this turns "the bundle is big" into "these three specific dependencies are 60% of it," which is what you can actually act on.

Common culprits worth checking specifically: a large library imported for one small utility when a lighter alternative exists (importing all of lodash for one function instead of a targeted import), a dependency that isn't tree-shakeable because it's shipped as CommonJS or has side effects the bundler can't safely eliminate, duplicate versions of the same library pulled in by different dependencies, or genuinely large assets (icon sets, a full date-formatting library with all locales) that could be code-split or lazy-loaded instead of included in the main bundle.

Say it like this

"First step is always a bundle analyzer treemap — it turns a vague 'it's big' into specific offenders. From there I'm looking for the usual suspects: a heavy library imported for one small piece of it, something that isn't tree-shaking correctly, duplicate versions of the same dependency, or large assets that should be code-split rather than sitting in the main bundle. I don't guess at fixes before I've actually seen what's taking up the space."

Docrollup-plugin-visualizer

140●●○○CommonWeb Vitals

What do LCP, INP, and CLS actually measure, and give a concrete cause of a bad score for each.

LCP (Largest Contentful Paint) measures how long it takes the largest visible element — usually a hero image or heading — to render. A bad score often comes from a slow server response, a render-blocking script, or an unoptimized/uncompressed hero image. INP (Interaction to Next Paint) measures the delay between a user interaction and the next visual update — a bad score often comes from a long-running synchronous JavaScript task (a huge state update, an expensive computation) blocking the main thread when the user clicks. CLS (Cumulative Layout Shift) measures unexpected visual movement — a bad score commonly comes from an image or ad without reserved dimensions, so content shifts down once it loads, or a web font swap that changes text size after the initial render.

Say it like this

"LCP is how fast the biggest visible element shows up — usually hurt by a slow server or an unoptimized hero image. INP is the responsiveness of interactions — usually hurt by a long synchronous task blocking the main thread right when the user clicks something. CLS is unexpected layout movement — the classic cause is an image without a reserved width and height pushing content down once it loads."

DocWeb Vitals — web.dev

141●●○○CommonImages

What's the difference between loading="lazy" and loading="eager" on an image, and where should you never use lazy loading?

loading="lazy" defers downloading an off-screen image until it's about to scroll into the viewport, saving bandwidth and initial load time for images the user might never actually scroll to. eager (the browser default) downloads immediately regardless of position.

The image that determines LCP — usually a large hero image at the top of the page — should never be lazy-loaded: lazy loading delays the download until the browser realizes it needs the image, but for the LCP element that delay directly makes your LCP score worse, which is the opposite of the intended effect. That image should load eagerly (and ideally be preloaded) since it's guaranteed to be needed immediately.

Say it like this

"Lazy defers loading until an image nears the viewport, which is a real win for anything below the fold. The one place I'd never use it is the LCP element itself, usually a hero image — lazy loading it directly delays the metric it's most responsible for, so that image should load eagerly and often gets a preload hint instead."

Docimg loading attribute — MDN

142●○○○OccasionalWeb Workers

You have a genuinely expensive synchronous computation (parsing a huge CSV client-side) that freezes the UI while it runs. What's your fix beyond "make it async"?

Wrapping expensive synchronous work in a promise or an async function doesn't actually help by itself — JavaScript is still single-threaded, so the CPU-bound work still blocks the main thread and freezes rendering and input handling for however long it takes; async only helps with I/O-bound waiting, not CPU-bound computation.

The actual fix is a Web Worker: it runs JavaScript on a genuinely separate thread, so the expensive computation doesn't block the main thread that's responsible for rendering and handling user input at all. Communication happens via postMessage, since workers don't share memory or have DOM access — you send the data in, the worker computes, and posts the result back.

Say it like this

"Making it async doesn't actually fix a CPU-bound problem — JS is still single-threaded, so the main thread is blocked for the duration regardless of async syntax. The real fix is a Web Worker, which runs on an actual separate thread, so the heavy computation doesn't freeze rendering or input handling. You lose direct DOM access and shared memory, communicating via postMessage instead, but for something like parsing a huge file client-side, that's the correct tool."

DocWeb Workers API — MDN

143●●○○CommonMemoization cost

Can wrapping everything in useMemo and useCallback "just in case" actually make an app slower?

Yes, in two ways. First, memoization itself isn't free: React has to store the dependency array, compare it against last time on every render, and hold onto the cached value — for a cheap computation, that bookkeeping can cost more than just recomputing the value would have. Second, indiscriminate memoization adds real cognitive overhead and code volume without necessarily preventing any actual re-render, since — per the earlier point about memo — it only helps when the stable reference feeds into something that checks it (a memoized child, a dependency array), and most values don't.

The correct default is to write plain code first and add memoization deliberately where profiling shows an actual problem it solves, not as a blanket habit applied everywhere out of caution.

Say it like this

"Yes — the comparison and caching aren't free, so memoizing something cheap can cost more than just recomputing it would. It also adds code and cognitive load without necessarily preventing anything, since it only pays off when the stable reference actually feeds into something checking it. I write plain code by default and add memoization deliberately once profiling points at a specific problem, not preemptively everywhere."

DocShould you add useMemo everywhere? — react.dev

144●○○○OccasionalReact Compiler

What does the React Compiler actually do, and does it mean you never need to think about memoization again?

The React Compiler is a build-time tool that analyzes your component and hook code and automatically inserts memoization equivalent to what you'd have hand-written with useMemo, useCallback, and memo — without you writing any of it, based on statically understanding which values and JSX depend on which inputs.

It doesn't eliminate the need to understand re-rendering, though — it still requires your components to follow the Rules of Hooks and be free of render-phase side effects (mutating things during render, relying on execution order side effects) to do its analysis safely, and it doesn't solve every performance problem — things like a genuinely too-large list still need virtualization, and choosing where state lives is still a human decision the compiler can't make for you.

Say it like this

"It statically analyzes components and automatically applies the memoization you'd otherwise hand-write with useMemo, useCallback, and memo. It's a real reduction in that specific boilerplate, but it doesn't replace understanding rendering — components still need to be pure and follow the Rules of Hooks for it to work safely, and it doesn't solve structural problems like an unvirtualized huge list or state living in the wrong place."

DocReact Compiler — react.dev

145●●○○CommonCSS-in-JS cost

What's a real runtime performance concern with runtime CSS-in-JS libraries that a build-time CSS approach doesn't have?

A runtime CSS-in-JS library (classic styled-components/emotion usage) generates and injects style tags into the document during rendering, in the browser, which adds actual JavaScript execution cost on every render where styles are computed from dynamic props, plus a one-time parsing/injection cost. A build-time approach (CSS Modules, Tailwind, or zero-runtime CSS-in-JS variants like vanilla-extract or the newer compiled styled-components mode) extracts static CSS at build time, so the browser just loads a plain stylesheet with zero runtime JS cost for styling.

This isn't purely theoretical — it shows up concretely in a component that re-renders frequently with dynamic style props, where the style computation and injection cost repeats on every render, unlike a static class name lookup which costs essentially nothing after the CSS file has loaded once.

Say it like this

"Runtime CSS-in-JS computes and injects styles in the browser during render, which adds real JS cost, especially for components with dynamic, prop-driven styles that re-render often. A build-time approach extracts plain CSS ahead of time, so the browser just applies class names with essentially no runtime styling cost. It's part of why a lot of teams have moved toward zero-runtime CSS-in-JS or utility-CSS approaches for anything performance-sensitive."

Docvanilla-extract — zero-runtime CSS

146●●○○CommonKey change trick

What happens if you change a component's key prop even though its position in the tree doesn't change?

Changing a component's key tells React it's a different element, even at the same position in the tree with the same component type — React unmounts the old instance entirely (discarding all its internal state) and mounts a brand-new instance from scratch. This is a deliberate, useful escape hatch, not just a footgun: it's the standard way to force a full reset of a component's internal state — for example, remounting a form component when switching between "edit item A" and "edit item B" so leftover state from item A's inputs doesn't bleed into item B's form.

// changing the key when `userId` changes forces a full remount,
// resetting all of ProfileForm's internal state instead of reusing stale state
<ProfileForm key={userId} userId={userId} />
Say it like this

"Changing the key forces React to treat it as a different element entirely, so it unmounts the old instance and mounts a fresh one, discarding all its state — even though position and component type stayed the same. I use this deliberately when switching what a form is editing, keying it on the item's id so switching items gives a clean state reset instead of stale input values bleeding across."

DocResetting state with a key — react.dev

147●○○○OccasionalConcurrent rendering

What does "concurrent rendering" actually mean in React 18+, in terms that don't just repeat the marketing description?

Before concurrent features, once React started rendering an update, it ran to completion synchronously and couldn't be interrupted — a large update would block the main thread until it fully finished, even if a more urgent update (like the next keystroke) came in during that time. Concurrent rendering means React can start preparing a render, pause partway through, throw away or set aside that in-progress work if something more urgent arrives, handle the urgent one, and resume or restart the deprioritized work afterward.

Crucially, this is invisible to the DOM — nothing is ever shown to the user until a render fully completes, so "interruptible" describes internal work-in-progress, not flickering half-finished UI. It's the underlying mechanism that makes useTransition, useDeferredValue, and Suspense's non-blocking behavior possible.

Say it like this

"Before concurrent rendering, once React started an update it ran to completion and blocked the main thread the whole time. Concurrent rendering means that work can be paused, interrupted by something more urgent, and resumed or discarded — but that's entirely internal, nothing partially-rendered is ever shown to the user. It's the mechanism underneath useTransition and useDeferredValue, not a separate feature you turn on by itself."

DocReact 18 — What is Concurrent React?

148●●○○CommonFont loading

Why does a custom web font sometimes cause a visible "flash" of different text, and what's the fix?

By default, the browser either waits for the custom font to download before showing text (FOIT — flash of invisible text, blank space then the real font) or shows a fallback font immediately and swaps it once the custom font loads (FOUT — flash of unstyled text, visible layout shift as text re-flows into different widths). Both are controlled by the font-display CSS property.

font-display: swap shows the fallback immediately and swaps in the custom font when ready — good for not blocking text visibility, but causes the visible reflow (and hurts CLS if the fallback and custom font have different metrics). The more complete fix is pairing a fallback font with matched size-adjust metrics (tools like Fontaine or manual size-adjust tuning can compute this) so the fallback occupies the same space as the final font, minimizing the visible shift when the swap happens.

Say it like this

"That's the FOUT/FOIT trade-off, controlled by font-display — swap shows a fallback immediately and swaps in the real font once loaded, which avoids invisible text but can cause a visible reflow if the two fonts have different metrics. The more thorough fix is matching the fallback font's size-adjust to the real font's metrics, so the layout doesn't actually shift when the swap happens, not just accepting the flash as unavoidable."

Docfont-display — MDN

Browser support & progressive enhancement

Less about any specific API and more about whether you have a deliberate strategy versus assuming everyone has the latest Chrome.

149●●○○CommonFeature detection

Why is feature detection generally preferred over user-agent sniffing when deciding whether to use a modern browser API?

User-agent strings are notoriously unreliable — browsers have historically spoofed each other's UA strings for compatibility reasons, the string format itself has become increasingly frozen or generic in modern browsers specifically to discourage UA-based branching, and a UA check that says "Chrome" tells you nothing certain about which specific APIs that particular version actually supports, since support varies release to release in ways a UA string doesn't encode.

Feature detection checks for the actual capability directly — if ('IntersectionObserver' in window), if (CSS.supports('gap', '1px')) — which is accurate by construction: if the check passes, the feature is definitely there, regardless of which browser or version is asking. It also correctly handles cases UA sniffing can't, like a browser that ships a feature ahead of when public UA-version mapping data would suggest, or a user with an extension that alters or spoofs the UA string.

Say it like this

"UA strings are unreliable — they get spoofed, they've become increasingly generic in modern browsers, and even a UA string that correctly identifies the browser doesn't reliably tell you which specific APIs that version supports. Feature detection checks the actual capability directly, so it's accurate by construction regardless of what's asking — if the check passes, the feature is genuinely there. It's the more correct approach essentially across the board, not just a style preference."

DocFeature detection — MDN

150●○○○OccasionalPolyfill strategy

Why is shipping every polyfill unconditionally in the main bundle wasteful, and what's a better strategy?

A polyfill exists to backfill a missing capability in an older browser — but the vast majority of visitors, on modern browsers, don't need it at all, so unconditionally bundling every polyfill ships genuinely dead code to almost everyone, hurting load time for the majority to cover a shrinking minority.

The better strategy is conditional, differential loading: serve a modern bundle with zero polyfills to browsers that support the required features natively, and only serve a polyfilled bundle to the browsers that actually lack them — either via a service like polyfill.io that inspects the requesting browser's UA and serves exactly the needed polyfills (accepting the UA-sniffing caveat from the previous question, since this specific use case is one of the few legitimate reasons to still touch UA at all), or via the `<script type="module">` / `nomodule` pattern, which naturally splits modern-syntax bundles from legacy-transpiled-plus-polyfilled ones based on genuine browser capability rather than a UA guess.

Say it like this

"Unconditionally bundling every polyfill ships dead code to the majority of visitors who are on modern browsers and don't need any of it. The better approach is differential loading — serve a clean modern bundle to browsers with native support and only serve the polyfilled version to browsers that actually lack a feature, either through a service like polyfill.io or the module/nomodule script pattern, which splits based on genuine capability rather than penalizing everyone for the minority still needing the fallback."

DocServe Modern Code to Modern Browsers — web.dev

151●○○○OccasionalProgressive enhancement

What does "progressive enhancement" mean as a design philosophy, and how is it different from "graceful degradation"?

Both aim for the same outcome — the app works reasonably across a range of capabilities — but start from opposite ends. Progressive enhancement starts from a baseline that works with minimal capability (working HTML forms, core content readable with no JS at all) and layers richer behavior on top for browsers that support it — the enhancement is additive, and the baseline is never dependent on the enhancement actually loading. Graceful degradation starts from the full-featured experience and adds fallback handling for when something isn't supported — the subtractive direction, starting from "everything works" and asking "what happens when it doesn't."

Progressive enhancement tends to produce a more robust baseline in practice, because the "no JS, no modern feature" case is the actual starting design target rather than an afterthought bolted on once the full experience already exists — though for a React SPA specifically, the JS-required baseline is often an accepted trade-off rather than something the team is trying to progressively enhance from a true no-JS starting point, which is worth naming honestly rather than pretending every React app follows this philosophy strictly.

Say it like this

"They aim for the same outcome from opposite directions — progressive enhancement starts from a minimal, no-JS-required baseline and layers richer behavior on top; graceful degradation starts from the full experience and adds fallback handling for when something's missing. Progressive enhancement tends to produce a more robust baseline because the minimal case is the actual design target, not an afterthought. I'd also be honest that most React SPAs accept a JS-required baseline as a trade-off rather than genuinely progressively enhancing from true no-JS."

DocProgressive Enhancement — MDN

Perceived performance & micro-interactions

Small interaction details that don't show up in a Lighthouse score but are exactly what separates a polished production app from a functional-but-rough one.

152●●●○Very commonDouble-submit prevention

A user double-clicks a "Submit Order" button, or clicks it once and then again while waiting for a slow response. What actually needs to happen to prevent a duplicate order, and where should that protection live?

The most immediate, cheapest layer is disabling the button (or otherwise making it unclickable) the instant the first click's handler starts, based on a local "is submitting" state flag — this prevents the obvious case of a user clicking rapidly out of impatience while a request is in flight. But this alone is not sufficient as the actual guarantee: it's purely client-side, so it does nothing against a user with two tabs open submitting the same form in both, a request that got fired before the disabled state actually rendered (a real race depending on exact timing), or someone bypassing the UI entirely and hitting the endpoint directly.

The real guarantee has to live server-side: an idempotency key generated client-side (a UUID created once when the form is first rendered, sent with the request) lets the server recognize and safely ignore a duplicate request carrying the same key, returning the original result instead of processing the action twice, regardless of how or why the duplicate request happened — network retry, double-click, two tabs, or anything else. The client-side disabled-button UX is still worth doing for the immediate, cheap feedback and to reduce unnecessary duplicate requests in the first place, but it's a UX nicety layered on top of the actual correctness guarantee, not a substitute for it.

Say it like this

"Disabling the button immediately on the first click is the cheap, immediate layer, and it's worth doing for the UX, but it's not the actual guarantee — it's purely client-side and does nothing against two tabs, a request that fired before the disabled state rendered, or someone hitting the endpoint directly. The real guarantee needs to be server-side, through an idempotency key generated once client-side and sent with the request, so the server can recognize and safely ignore an exact duplicate regardless of what caused it. The disabled button is a UX layer on top of that, not a substitute for it."

DocIdempotent Requests — Stripe API docs

153●●○○CommonPerceived performance

Beyond skeleton screens, what are a few other concrete micro-interaction techniques that make an app feel faster without actually reducing real load time?

A handful of specific, well-established patterns beyond skeletons: giving an interactive element (a button) an immediate visual response on press — before any async result comes back — so the interface never feels unresponsive even during a genuinely slow operation, since the delay is in getting the result, not in acknowledging the interaction happened. Progress indicators that show genuine, granular progress (a percentage, a step count) rather than an indeterminate spinner feel meaningfully faster for the same actual duration, because a spinner gives the user zero information about whether something is almost done or barely started, which reads as more anxious waiting even at an identical clock time.

Staggered or progressive reveal — showing content as each piece becomes ready rather than waiting for everything and showing it all at once (the streaming-SSR pattern from earlier is a server-driven version of this same idea) — makes the wait feel shorter because something is visibly happening continuously, rather than one long pause followed by an abrupt full reveal. And optimistic UI itself, covered extensively earlier, is arguably the most powerful perceived-performance technique of all, since it eliminates the perceived wait for the specific interaction entirely rather than just making the wait feel shorter.

Say it like this

"A few concrete ones beyond skeletons: immediate visual feedback on press before any async result comes back, so the interaction never feels unresponsive even if the actual operation is slow. Genuine granular progress instead of an indeterminate spinner, since a spinner gives zero information about how close something is to done, which reads as more anxious waiting at the same actual duration. Progressive reveal, showing content as each piece becomes ready rather than one abrupt full reveal at the end. And honestly, optimistic UI is probably the most powerful one of all, since it eliminates the perceived wait entirely rather than just making it feel shorter."

DocProgress Indicators Make a Slow System Less Insufferable — Nielsen Norman Group

Block 9

Testing

Interviewers use this block to check whether tests actually protect the app, or just protect the test file from being empty. Fourteen questions.

154●●●●Near-certainTesting Library philosophy

React Testing Library's guiding principle is "test the way your software is used." What does that mean concretely, and what does it push you away from?

It means queries and assertions should mirror what a real user perceives and interacts with — text content, accessible roles, labels — rather than implementation details like component internals, state variable names, or CSS class names. If you can't find an element the way a screen reader user or a person visually scanning the page could, the test is asserting on something the real user experience doesn't actually depend on.

It pushes you specifically away from shallow rendering and reaching into component instance state or props directly (both of which were common with the older Enzyme approach) — because a test coupled to internals breaks on refactors that don't change any actual user-facing behavior, which is the exact opposite of what a good test suite should protect you from.

Say it like this

"It means I query and assert the way a real user would perceive the page — by visible text, by accessible role, by label — not by reaching into component internals or CSS classes. The payoff is that refactoring implementation without changing behavior doesn't break the test, which is the whole point; a test coupled to internals gives false confidence and false failures at the same time."

DocGuiding Principles — Testing Library docs

155●●●●Near-certainQuery priority

In Testing Library, why is getByRole generally preferred over getByTestId, and when is getByTestId actually the right call?

getByRole queries by accessible role and accessible name — the same information assistive technology uses — so a passing test is genuine evidence that the element is actually usable and identifiable in an accessible way, not just present in the DOM. getByTestId queries an attribute (data-testid) that has zero relationship to how a real user or assistive technology finds anything, so a passing test there proves only "an element with this attribute exists," which doesn't validate accessibility or the actual user-facing behavior at all.

getByTestId earns its place as a last resort: for elements with no meaningful role or accessible text to query by (a decorative wrapper, a complex custom widget where role-based querying is genuinely impractical), it's better than not being able to test the element at all — but it should be the fallback after exhausting role, label, and text-based queries, not the default habit.

Say it like this

"getByRole queries the same accessibility information assistive tech relies on, so a passing test is actual evidence the element is usable, not just present. getByTestId proves nothing beyond 'this attribute exists in the DOM' — it doesn't validate anything a real user experiences. I reach for it only as a last resort, for elements that genuinely have no meaningful role or text to query by, not as a default habit."

DocPriority of queries — Testing Library docs

156●●●○Very commonuserEvent vs fireEvent

What's the difference between Testing Library's fireEvent.click and userEvent.click?

fireEvent dispatches exactly one low-level DOM event, directly — a raw click event and nothing else. userEvent simulates the full sequence of events a real browser fires for that interaction: a real click involves pointerdown, mousedown, focus, pointerup, mouseup, and finally click, in order — and userEvent fires all of them, closer to what actually happens when a person clicks.

This matters concretely for components whose behavior depends on intermediate events — a button that visually responds to mousedown before the click completes, or a component relying on focus state, will behave correctly under userEvent and might not under a bare fireEvent.click that skips straight to the final event. Testing Library's own docs now recommend userEvent as the default for exactly this reason.

Say it like this

"fireEvent dispatches one specific DOM event directly, userEvent simulates the whole realistic sequence of events a browser actually fires for that interaction — pointerdown, mousedown, focus, and so on before the click itself. That matters for anything depending on those intermediate events, like focus-dependent behavior, which is why I default to userEvent and only reach for fireEvent for something very low-level that userEvent doesn't cover."

Docuser-event — Testing Library docs

157●●●○Very commonMocking API calls

Why is Mock Service Worker (MSW) generally preferred over mocking fetch or an API client module directly?

Mocking fetch or the API client module means the test exercises your app's code only up to the point right before the network call — anything your actual HTTP client does (headers, retries, error transformation, serialization) is bypassed, so the test can pass while the real integration between your app and its HTTP client is untested. MSW instead intercepts requests at the network level, so your actual fetch/axios code runs exactly as it would in production, and only the network response itself is faked.

The practical benefit is that the same mock handlers can be reused across unit tests, integration tests, and even in the browser during development (MSW can run as a service worker in dev mode), so you're not maintaining separate, potentially-diverging mock setups for different testing layers.

Say it like this

"Mocking fetch directly means the actual HTTP client code — headers, error handling, serialization — never runs in the test, so that integration is untested even if the test passes. MSW intercepts at the network level instead, so my real fetch or axios code executes exactly like production and only the response is faked. It also means the same handlers work across unit tests, integration tests, and even local dev, instead of maintaining separate mocks per layer."

DocMock Service Worker — Introduction

158●●●○Very commonTesting hooks

How do you unit test a custom hook that doesn't belong to any specific component?

Hooks can only run inside a component's render, so you need a way to invoke it in a component context without writing throwaway test-only components by hand for every hook. renderHook from Testing Library does exactly that: it mounts a minimal invisible test component internally, calls your hook inside it, and gives you back the hook's return value plus a way to trigger re-renders and interact with the result — so you can assert on the hook's behavior directly.

This matters because it lets you unit test hook logic (state transitions, memoized values, effect behavior) in isolation, without needing to build and test through an actual UI component that happens to use the hook, which would couple the hook's test to unrelated rendering concerns.

const { result } = renderHook(() => useCounter());
act(() => { result.current.increment(); });
expect(result.current.count).toBe(1);
Say it like this

"I use renderHook from Testing Library — it mounts a hook inside a minimal internal test component and gives you back its return value, plus a way to trigger updates and re-check the result. That lets me test the hook's actual logic in isolation, without wiring up a real component just to have somewhere to call it from, which would couple the test to unrelated rendering behavior."

DocrenderHook — React Testing Library docs

159●●○○Commonact warning

What does the "not wrapped in act(...)" warning actually mean, and what's usually the real cause?

act tells React "flush all pending state updates and effects before the test's next assertion runs" — it's how the testing environment simulates React's normal render-then-commit cycle in a synchronous, deterministic way. The warning means a state update happened outside that controlled window — often because an async operation (a promise resolving, a timer firing) triggered a state update after the test had already moved on without waiting for it.

Testing Library's own utilities (render, fireEvent, userEvent) already wrap themselves in act internally, so the warning most often surfaces when a test doesn't properly await something async — a fetch that resolves later, updating state after the test function has already finished its synchronous body and moved to assertions. The real fix is almost always to correctly await the async operation (with findBy queries or waitFor), not to manually wrap things in act yourself as a band-aid.

Say it like this

"It means a state update happened outside the window React expected to flush and settle updates in — usually because the test didn't wait for something async, like a fetch resolving after the assertion already ran. Testing Library's own render and userEvent already handle act internally, so when I see this warning I look for a missing await or a findBy/waitFor that should be wrapping the async part, rather than manually wrapping things in act as a workaround."

Docact — react.dev

160●●○○CommonSnapshot tests

What's a real downside of snapshot testing for React components, beyond "they can get stale"?

The core issue is that a snapshot doesn't encode intent — it captures whatever the output happened to be at the time it was recorded, with no statement about which parts of that output actually matter. When a snapshot fails, the developer has to manually re-derive "was this change intentional and correct, or did I just break something" by eyeballing a diff, and under time pressure the common failure mode is running --updateSnapshot without actually reviewing the diff carefully — which defeats the entire purpose of the test.

Targeted assertions (asserting a specific piece of text is present, a specific attribute has a specific value) force you to state what actually matters up front, so a failure is meaningful and a passing test is real evidence of something. Snapshots are more defensible for small, stable, low-churn output (a formatted error message, a generated URL) where full-output comparison genuinely reflects the property you care about.

Say it like this

"A snapshot doesn't encode what you actually meant to test — it's just whatever the output was when you recorded it, with no statement of intent. The failure mode I've actually seen is people running update-snapshot under time pressure without really reviewing the diff, which quietly defeats the test's purpose. I prefer targeted assertions that state what actually matters, and reserve snapshots for small, low-churn output where comparing the whole thing genuinely is the property I care about."

DocSnapshot Testing — Jest docs

161●●○○CommonE2E vs integration

Given a fixed amount of testing time, why would you generally write more component/integration tests than end-to-end tests?

This is the classic testing pyramid reasoning applied to a frontend app. End-to-end tests (Cypress, Playwright driving a real browser against a real or realistic backend) give the highest confidence because they exercise the real integrated system, but they're the slowest to run, the most brittle to unrelated changes (a backend deploy, a flaky network condition, timing issues), and the most expensive to maintain and debug when they fail, since a failure could be anywhere in the whole stack.

Component/integration tests (rendering real component trees with Testing Library, mocking only the network boundary) run much faster, are more stable, and pinpoint failures closer to the actual code that broke — which means you can afford far more of them for the same time budget, catching more bugs overall. The right mix keeps a smaller set of E2E tests for the critical user journeys (checkout, signup) where true end-to-end confidence specifically matters, with the bulk of coverage living at the component/integration level.

Say it like this

"E2E tests give the most realistic confidence but are the slowest, most brittle, and most expensive to debug when they fail, since the failure could be anywhere in the stack. Component and integration tests are faster, more stable, and point much closer to the actual broken code, so for the same time budget I get more coverage and faster feedback. I keep a small set of E2E tests for the truly critical flows — checkout, signup — and put the bulk of the coverage at the component level."

DocWrite tests. Not too many. Mostly integration. — Kent C. Dodds

162●○○○OccasionalVisual regression

What kind of bug does visual regression testing catch that a functional test (checking text content, checking that a click handler fired) can't?

Functional tests verify behavior and DOM structure — the right text is present, the right element exists, the click handler ran — but they're blind to pure visual regressions: a CSS change that breaks alignment, an overflow that clips text, a color contrast that becomes unreadable, a component whose layout breaks at a specific viewport width. None of that shows up as a failed assertion in a functional test, because the DOM structure and text content can be entirely correct while the rendered result looks visually broken.

Visual regression tools (Chromatic, Percy) render the component, capture a screenshot, and diff it pixel-by-pixel against an approved baseline, flagging any visual difference for human review — catching exactly the class of bug that "the right elements are present" testing structurally cannot see.

Say it like this

"Functional tests check behavior and structure, but they're blind to pure visual regressions — a CSS change that misaligns something or breaks at a specific viewport can leave every functional assertion passing while the UI looks broken. Visual regression tools screenshot the rendered output and diff it against an approved baseline, which catches exactly that category of bug that structural testing has no way to see."

DocVisual Testing — Chromatic docs

163●●○○CommonTesting async UI

How do you correctly test a component that shows a loading spinner, then the data once a fetch resolves?

The key tool is Testing Library's findBy query variants, which are async and internally poll/wait for an element to appear rather than requiring it to exist synchronously the moment they're called — exactly what's needed since the data appears after an awaited promise resolves. Asserting on the loading state uses the synchronous getBy immediately after render (since the spinner should be there right away), and asserting on the final content uses findBy to wait for it to appear once the mocked fetch resolves.

render(<UserProfile userId="1" />);
expect(screen.getByText(/loading/i)).toBeInTheDocument(); // sync: true immediately
expect(await screen.findByText(/jane doe/i)).toBeInTheDocument(); // async: waits for it
Say it like this

"I assert the loading state synchronously right after render with getBy, since it should be there immediately, and use findBy — which waits and polls — to assert on the content that only appears once the mocked fetch resolves. That combination matches the actual timeline of the component instead of forcing artificial waits or assuming a fixed delay."

DocfindBy queries — Testing Library docs

164●○○○OccasionalCoverage

Why is a high code coverage percentage not the same thing as a well-tested application?

Coverage measures which lines executed during the test run, not whether the test made any meaningful assertion about the correctness of what happened. A test that renders a component and asserts nothing beyond "it didn't throw" can hit 100% line coverage while catching zero real regressions — the line ran, but nothing verified it did the right thing.

Coverage is a useful negative signal (an uncovered line is definitely untested) but a weak positive one (a covered line might still be poorly tested). It's worth tracking to catch obvious gaps, but chasing a specific percentage as a goal in itself tends to produce exactly this kind of hollow test that inflates the number without adding real protection.

Say it like this

"Coverage tells you a line executed, not that anything meaningful was asserted about it — a test that renders something and checks nothing beyond 'it didn't crash' can hit full coverage while catching nothing. It's a decent negative signal for finding obviously untested code, but a weak positive one, and chasing a coverage number as a target tends to produce exactly that kind of hollow test."

DocHow to know what to test — Kent C. Dodds

165●●○○CommonTesting context

A component reads from three different context providers (theme, auth, feature flags). How do you avoid repeating that provider nesting in every single test file?

Wrap Testing Library's render in a custom render function that automatically wraps the component under test with all the app's real (or test-configured) providers, and re-export it in place of the library's default. Every test file imports this custom render instead of the default one, so the provider nesting is written exactly once instead of copy-pasted into every test.

function customRender(ui, options) {
  function Wrapper({ children }) {
    return (
      <ThemeProvider><AuthProvider><FeatureFlagsProvider>
        {children}
      </FeatureFlagsProvider></AuthProvider></ThemeProvider>
    );
  }
  return render(ui, { wrapper: Wrapper, ...options });
}
// test files import { render } from './test-utils', not from the library directly
export * from '@testing-library/react';
export { customRender as render };
Say it like this

"I build one custom render wrapper that includes every provider the app actually needs, and re-export it from a shared test-utils module so every test imports render from there instead of the library directly. That way the provider nesting is written once, and it's also the single place to update if a new global provider gets added to the app."

DocCustom render — React Testing Library docs

166●○○○OccasionalFlaky tests
