A test passes locally but is intermittently flaky in CI. What are the usual culprits specific to React component tests?

The most common cause is a missing await or waitFor/findBy around something async — it passes locally when timing happens to line up (a fast machine, a fast mocked response) and fails in CI where timing is more variable or the environment is slower. Second common culprit: a fixed setTimeout-based wait as a substitute for actually waiting on a condition — "wait 500ms and hope the async thing finished" is inherently timing-dependent and will eventually fail whenever the real operation happens to take longer than that guess.

Less common but real: tests that don't clean up global state between runs (a mocked timer, a module-level variable, a shared MSW handler override) can pass or fail depending on execution order, especially if tests are parallelized differently in CI than locally.

Say it like this

"My first suspect is always a missing await or waitFor around something async — it happens to line up locally on a fast machine and falls apart under CI's timing variance. Second is a fixed setTimeout-based wait standing in for actually waiting on a condition, which is inherently a ticking time bomb. If neither of those is it, I look at whether tests are properly cleaning up shared or global state between runs, since that can behave differently depending on execution order."

DocwaitFor — Testing Library docs

167●●○○CommonWhat not to test

Give an example of something you'd deliberately choose not to write a test for, and explain the reasoning.

A purely presentational component with no logic — it just takes props and renders them into JSX with no conditionals, no computation, nothing that could actually be wrong in a way a test would catch beyond "does React render JSX," which is React's own guarantee, not something worth re-verifying per component. A test here mostly just re-describes the implementation (assert the prop appears in the output) and breaks on any markup refactor without ever having caught a real bug.

The general principle: test where there's actual logic, branching, or a state transition that could plausibly be wrong — a component that's pure prop-to-markup mapping with zero conditionals is closer to a fact about React than a testable behavior of your code.

Say it like this

"A purely presentational component with no branching or computation — it just maps props straight into JSX. A test there tends to just restate the implementation and breaks on markup refactors without ever catching a real bug, because there's no actual logic that could be wrong. I focus testing effort on components with real conditionals, state transitions, or computation — where a bug is actually possible."

DocHow to know what to test — Kent C. Dodds

Block 10

Tooling, build & deployment

The "have you actually shipped something" block — bundlers, environment config, and CI, rather than pure React knowledge. Ten questions.

168●●●○Very commonBundler basics

What does a bundler like Vite or webpack actually do, at a level beyond "it bundles files"?

It resolves a module dependency graph starting from an entry point, transforms non-JS-native syntax (JSX, TypeScript, CSS imports) into something the browser understands via loaders/plugins, and combines everything into a smaller number of optimized output files — while doing tree-shaking (removing exports that are never imported anywhere) and code-splitting (separating code into chunks that can load independently, like per-route).

Vite specifically changes the development-time story: instead of bundling everything upfront before the dev server can respond (webpack's traditional approach), Vite serves source files as native ES modules directly to the browser during development, transforming files on demand as the browser requests them — which is why Vite's dev server starts almost instantly regardless of project size, while a webpack dev server's startup time scales with the project.

Say it like this

"It resolves the dependency graph from an entry point, transforms non-native syntax like JSX and TypeScript, and produces optimized output with tree-shaking and code-splitting. Vite's real innovation is on the dev-server side — it serves native ES modules on demand instead of bundling everything upfront, which is why Vite dev servers start almost instantly regardless of project size, unlike a traditional webpack setup where startup scales with the codebase."

DocWhy Vite — Vite docs

169●●●○Very commonEnvironment variables

Why can a secret API key accidentally end up visible in your production JavaScript bundle even if it's stored in an .env file?

Frontend build tools inline environment variables at build time — anything referenced as import.meta.env.VITE_SOMETHING or process.env.REACT_APP_SOMETHING gets literally substituted with its string value directly into the bundled JavaScript that ships to every browser. Unlike a backend server, where an environment variable stays server-side and is never sent to the client, a frontend build has no server boundary — whatever value the variable held at build time is now permanently embedded in a file anyone can open in browser DevTools and read as plain text.

The rule: only put values in frontend env variables that are genuinely safe to be public (a public API base URL, a publishable Stripe key designed for client exposure). Anything that grants privileged access — a secret key, a database credential — has no business in a frontend build at all; it belongs on a server that the frontend calls, never bundled into client code.

Say it like this

"Frontend env variables get inlined into the bundle at build time, so whatever the value was becomes literal text in a JS file that ships to every browser — there's no server boundary keeping it private the way there is for a backend env variable. So I treat frontend env vars as public by default and never put a genuine secret there; anything privileged has to live behind an actual backend endpoint the frontend calls, not baked into the client bundle."

DocEnv Variables and Modes — Vite docs

170●●○○CommonMonorepos

What real problem does a monorepo tool like Turborepo or Nx solve that plain npm/yarn workspaces don't?

Workspaces solve dependency linking — letting packages in the same repo reference each other without publishing to a registry first. They don't solve build orchestration: knowing which packages actually need to rebuild given what changed, running tasks in the correct dependency order, and — critically — caching task outputs so unchanged packages don't redo work that already ran and produced the same result.

Turborepo/Nx add a task graph that understands package dependencies, so a change to a shared UI library correctly triggers rebuilds of everything downstream that depends on it, while packages untouched by the change reuse cached results (locally and, with remote caching configured, even across different developers' machines and CI runs) instead of rebuilding from scratch.

Say it like this

"Workspaces solve linking packages together locally; they don't solve knowing what actually needs to rebuild or caching that work. Turborepo and Nx add a task graph aware of package dependencies, so a shared library change correctly cascades to what depends on it, while everything else reuses cached output — with remote caching, that cache can even be shared across CI runs and teammates' machines, not just locally."

DocMonorepos — Turborepo docs

171●●○○CommonSemantic versioning

A dependency's version is pinned as ^4.2.1 in package.json. What range of versions can actually get installed, and why does this sometimes cause a "works on my machine" bug?

^4.2.1 allows any version compatible under semantic versioning rules — any 4.x.y where the result is greater than or equal to 4.2.1, but never 5.0.0 or above, since a major version bump signals breaking changes. So 4.3.0 or 4.9.2 could install without anyone changing anything, as long as the maintainer followed semver correctly.

The "works on my machine" scenario happens because two developers (or a developer and CI) installing at different times, without a committed lockfile being respected, can resolve to different actual versions within that allowed range — if a minor version introduced a real behavior change despite semver technically calling it non-breaking (which happens, since semver compliance depends on the maintainer's judgment, not an automatic guarantee), one machine sees the bug and the other doesn't. This is exactly why committing and correctly using a lockfile (package-lock.json, yarn.lock) matters — it pins the exact resolved version so every install is identical regardless of what the range technically allows.

Say it like this

"The caret allows any compatible version within the same major — so 4.2.1 up to but not including 5.0.0. The works-on-my-machine case happens when two installs at different times resolve to different actual versions within that range, and a minor release turns out to have a real behavior change despite being technically non-breaking by semver. That's exactly what a committed, respected lockfile prevents — it pins the exact version so every install is identical."

DocAbout semantic versioning — npm docs

172●●○○CommonCI pipelines

What would you put in a CI pipeline for a React app before it's allowed to merge, and in what order?

The general principle is fail fast on cheap checks first, so a broken PR gets feedback in seconds rather than waiting minutes for an expensive step to discover the same problem. A reasonable order: linting and type-checking first (fast, catches obvious mistakes), unit/component tests next (fast-ish, catches logic regressions), then a production build (confirms the app actually compiles for real, catches issues that only show up in the production build config), and end-to-end tests last (slowest, most valuable for confirming critical flows genuinely work end to end).

Running the slow, expensive E2E suite first would mean a trivial linting typo takes ten minutes to surface instead of five seconds — ordering by cost, not by "importance," gets the fastest possible feedback loop for the most common failure cases.

Say it like this

"I order by cost, cheapest first, so a trivial mistake fails fast instead of waiting behind an expensive step. Lint and type-check first, unit and component tests next, then a real production build to catch anything that only shows up under build config, and E2E last since it's the slowest but the most end-to-end confidence. The goal is the fastest possible feedback for the most common kinds of failures."

DocGitHub Actions documentation

173●○○○OccasionalFeature flags

Why would you deploy a feature behind a flag instead of just merging it when it's ready and shipping directly?

Feature flags decouple deployment (code is on production servers) from release (users can actually see and use it). That separation enables things a direct merge-and-ship can't: gradual rollout to a percentage of users to catch problems before everyone's affected, instant kill-switch rollback by flipping the flag off — no redeploy needed, which is far faster than reverting a deploy — and shipping half-finished work behind a flag that's off, so long-running feature branches don't diverge painfully from main.

The trade-off worth acknowledging: flags accumulate as technical debt if not cleaned up after a feature fully ships, and testing every meaningful combination of flags on/off adds real complexity, so a flag's lifecycle (introduce, roll out, remove) needs to actually be managed, not left in the codebase indefinitely.

Say it like this

"Flags separate deploying code from releasing it to users, which enables gradual rollout and an instant kill switch that's much faster than reverting a deploy. It also lets a long-running feature merge to main early behind a flag instead of living on a diverging branch. The honest trade-off is that flags become debt if nobody removes them once a feature has fully shipped, so I treat a flag's removal as part of the feature's actual definition of done."

DocFeature Toggles — Martin Fowler

174●●○○CommonSource maps

What's a source map for, and why is it a mistake to just always disable them in production for "security"?

Production JavaScript is minified — variable names shortened, whitespace stripped, sometimes bundled from many files into one — which makes a production stack trace essentially unreadable (a.b(c,d) at line 1, column 48213 tells you nothing). A source map is a file mapping positions in the minified output back to the original, readable source, so an error tracking tool (Sentry, Bugsnag) can show you the actual original file, function name, and line where the error really occurred.

Disabling source maps in production doesn't meaningfully hide source code from a determined person — the minified code itself is still fully readable and de-minifiable with effort — it mainly just blinds your own team to what's actually breaking in production, since your error monitoring loses the ability to show real stack traces. The better approach is generating source maps but uploading them privately to your error-tracking service rather than serving them publicly alongside the bundle, which keeps debugging capability without exposing the map file to every visitor.

Say it like this

"Source maps let error tracking show a real stack trace against your actual source instead of an unreadable minified blob. Disabling them entirely doesn't really protect anything — the minified code is still readable with effort — it mainly blinds your own monitoring. What I'd actually do is generate them but upload them privately to the error tracking service instead of serving them publicly, so debugging still works without handing the map to every visitor."

DocSource maps — MDN

175●●○○CommonRollback

A bad deploy is live in production, actively causing errors. What's your immediate action versus what you do afterward?

The immediate priority is stopping user impact as fast as possible, which is almost never "quickly write and ship a fix" under pressure — that risks introducing a second bug while rushed. The fastest safe action is usually rolling back to the last known-good deployment (most modern hosting platforms support instant rollback to a previous build) or flipping off a feature flag if the change was flagged, both of which restore stability in seconds to minutes without touching code at all.

Only after impact is stopped does the actual investigation happen: reproduce the issue, understand root cause, write and properly test a real fix, and redeploy deliberately — not under the same time pressure that caused the original incident, and ideally with a postmortem afterward covering what let it reach production in the first place.

Say it like this

"First priority is stopping user impact, not fixing the bug — that's almost always a rollback to the last known-good deploy, or flipping off a flag if the change was flagged, both of which restore stability in minutes without touching code under pressure. Root-causing and writing the actual fix happens afterward, without that same time pressure, followed by a postmortem on how it got through in the first place."

DocPostmortem Culture — Google SRE Book

176●○○○OccasionalPackage managers

What's the actual mechanism that lets pnpm save significant disk space compared to npm across multiple projects?

npm and yarn (classic) copy or duplicate each package's files into every project's own node_modules that depends on it — the same version of the same package physically exists on disk once per project that uses it. pnpm keeps a single global content-addressable store on disk and uses hard links (or symlinks, depending on platform) from each project's node_modules into that shared store, so a given package version's actual file content is stored exactly once on disk regardless of how many projects depend on it.

This is also why pnpm's node_modules structure is stricter and non-flat by default (unlike npm's historically flattened structure) — the symlink structure it uses to wire up dependencies also has the side effect of preventing a package from accidentally importing another package it didn't explicitly declare as a dependency, which npm's flat structure allowed by accident.

Say it like this

"npm duplicates each package's files into every project's node_modules that needs it. pnpm keeps one global content-addressable store and links into it, so identical package versions are stored on disk exactly once no matter how many projects use them. A nice side effect of how that linking works is it also prevents accidentally importing an undeclared dependency, which npm's older flat structure let slip through."

DocMotivation — pnpm docs

177●●○○CommonSentry / error monitoring

How would you find out that a specific error is happening in production before a user files a support ticket about it?

An error boundary catching render errors handles the UI gracefully but, on its own, doesn't tell anyone it happened — the failure is invisible to the team unless a user happens to report it. Error monitoring (Sentry, Bugsnag, or similar) needs to be wired into that same catch point — both the error boundary's componentDidCatch and a global handler for errors outside React's render (unhandled promise rejections, errors in event handlers) — so every error gets reported with a stack trace, the user's session context, and browser/environment details, and the team gets alerted, ideally with grouping so the same underlying bug affecting a thousand users shows up as one issue, not a thousand.

Say it like this

"An error boundary alone just prevents a white screen — it doesn't notify anyone. I wire error monitoring into both the error boundary's catch point and a global handler for things outside React's render, like unhandled rejections, so every failure gets reported with a stack trace and context automatically, with grouping so the same bug hitting many users shows up as one alert instead of a flood. The goal is finding out before a support ticket does, not after."

DocSentry for React — docs

Linting, formatting & git conventions

Less exciting than architecture questions, but a real filter for whether you've worked on a team versus solo — these are about process discipline, not cleverness.

178●●○○CommonESLint vs Prettier

Why do most React projects run both ESLint and Prettier rather than just one, given they can seem like overlapping tools?

They solve genuinely different problems despite both touching code style. Prettier is purely about formatting — whitespace, line breaks, quote style — and deliberately has almost no configuration, because its entire value proposition is ending bikeshedding by having one opinionated, non-negotiable answer for how code is arranged visually. ESLint is about code quality and correctness — catching a missing dependency in a hook, an unused variable, an accessibility violation in JSX, a genuinely buggy pattern — which has nothing to do with whitespace at all.

Running both requires disabling ESLint's own formatting-related rules (via eslint-config-prettier) so the two don't fight each other by both trying to auto-fix the same whitespace differently — Prettier owns formatting entirely, ESLint owns everything else, and the config exists specifically to keep that division clean rather than having two tools both opinionated about the same thing.

Say it like this

"They solve different problems that happen to both touch code style — Prettier is purely formatting, deliberately unconfigurable, so it ends bikeshedding with one non-negotiable answer for whitespace and line breaks. ESLint is about actual code quality — a missing hook dependency, an accessibility issue in JSX, a genuinely buggy pattern — which has nothing to do with formatting. I run both with eslint-config-prettier disabling ESLint's own formatting rules, so they don't fight over the same thing."

DocPrettier vs. Linters — Prettier docs

179●●○○CommonPre-commit hooks

Why run linting and formatting in a pre-commit hook, given that CI already checks the same things before merge?

CI catching a lint failure means the feedback loop is: push, wait for CI to run, see the failure, fix it, push again — minutes of round-trip time for something that's often a trivial fix. A pre-commit hook (typically via Husky plus lint-staged, running only against the files actually being committed rather than the whole repo for speed) catches the exact same class of issue locally, in seconds, before it's even pushed — the feedback loop shrinks from a CI round-trip to an immediate local check.

It's not a replacement for CI, though — CI remains the actual gate, since a pre-commit hook can be skipped (--no-verify) or simply not run on every contributor's machine consistently, so CI is still the authoritative check; the pre-commit hook is purely an optional speed optimization that catches most issues earlier for people who have it set up, not a substitute for the real gate.

Say it like this

"CI catching it means a full push-wait-see-failure-fix-push round trip, often for something trivial. A pre-commit hook catches the same class of issue locally in seconds before it's even pushed, which is a much tighter feedback loop. It's not a replacement for CI though — it can be skipped or just not set up consistently across every contributor's machine, so CI stays the actual authoritative gate, and the hook is purely a speed optimization on top of it."

DocHusky — Git hooks

180●○○○OccasionalTrunk-based vs feature branches

What's the actual trade-off between trunk-based development and long-lived feature branches for a frontend team shipping frequently?

Trunk-based development — small, frequent merges directly to main, often gated behind feature flags for anything not ready for users — minimizes merge conflict pain (branches diverge for hours, not weeks) and keeps everyone building on genuinely current code, but requires real discipline around feature flags and incremental, always-shippable changes, since an incomplete feature has to be safe to merge and simply hidden rather than kept isolated on a branch.

Long-lived feature branches let a feature stay fully isolated and incomplete for as long as needed without touching main at all, which feels safer for a large, risky change — but the branch diverges from main more and more over time, so the eventual merge gets harder and more conflict-prone the longer it's delayed, and other work on main proceeds without any visibility into what's happening on the branch until it lands. For a team shipping frequently, trunk-based plus feature flags (the same flags covered in Block 10 earlier) tends to scale better specifically because it avoids that growing-divergence problem, at the cost of needing the flag discipline to make incomplete work safe to merge.

Say it like this

"Trunk-based keeps merges small and frequent, minimizing conflict pain and keeping everyone on genuinely current code, but it requires real discipline around feature flags since incomplete work has to be safe to merge and just hidden. Long-lived branches let a feature stay isolated as long as needed, which feels safer for something big and risky, but the branch diverges more over time and the eventual merge gets harder the longer it's delayed. For a team shipping frequently, I'd lean trunk-based plus flags specifically because it avoids that growing-divergence problem."

DocTrunk Based Development

181●○○○OccasionalConventional commits

What's the practical benefit of enforcing a conventional commit message format (feat:, fix:, chore:) rather than free-form commit messages?

Beyond readability, the real payoff is that a consistent, machine-parseable prefix lets tooling automate things a human would otherwise do by hand: automatically generating a changelog grouped by type (features versus fixes versus breaking changes) directly from commit history, automatically determining the correct semantic version bump for a release (a fix: commit implies a patch release, a commit with a documented breaking change implies a major release) without a human manually deciding, and giving reviewers and future developers scanning history a quick, consistent signal of a commit's intent without reading the full diff.

The honest trade-off: it only delivers that value if it's actually enforced consistently (usually via a commit-lint pre-commit hook) — a convention that's optional and inconsistently followed doesn't give the automation anything reliable to parse, so half-enforced conventional commits mostly just add friction without the automation payoff that justifies the friction in the first place.

Say it like this

"The real payoff beyond readability is automation — a consistent, parseable prefix lets tooling auto-generate a changelog grouped by type, and automatically determine the correct semver bump for a release without a human deciding by hand. The honest caveat is it only pays off if it's actually enforced consistently, usually with a commit-lint hook — a convention that's optional and half-followed doesn't give the automation anything reliable to parse, so it ends up being friction without the payoff that justifies it."

DocConventional Commits

182●○○○OccasionalWebpack configuration

In a webpack config, what's the actual distinction between a loader and a plugin, and where does splitChunks fit into that?

A loader transforms an individual file's content as it's being read into the module graph — it operates at the file level, converting something webpack can't natively understand (a .css import, a .svg, JSX/TS syntax) into valid JavaScript webpack can bundle, and loaders run per-file in a defined order (right to left in the array) as each matching file is processed. A plugin operates at a broader scope, hooking into the overall build lifecycle itself — the compilation process as a whole — to do things no single file transform could, like extracting CSS into separate files, generating the HTML file that references the output bundles, or optimizing/analyzing the entire bundle after everything's been assembled.

splitChunks is specifically a plugin-level optimization (part of webpack's built-in optimization config, conceptually a plugin even though it ships built into webpack itself) that decides how to split the final bundle into separate chunks — commonly separating vendor code (node_modules dependencies, which change far less often than app code) into its own chunk so browsers can cache it independently of app code that changes on every deploy, and de-duplicating code that multiple entry points would otherwise each bundle separately. It's a build-output-level decision about how the final artifacts are structured, which is exactly the kind of whole-build-scope concern that fits the plugin model rather than the per-file loader model.

Say it like this

"A loader transforms individual files as they're read into the module graph — turning CSS or JSX into something webpack can bundle, running per-file. A plugin hooks into the overall build lifecycle for things no single file transform could do, like extracting CSS into its own file or generating the HTML entry point. splitChunks fits the plugin model — it's a whole-build decision about how the final output gets split, typically separating rarely-changing vendor code into its own cacheable chunk from app code that changes every deploy, which is a build-output concern, not a per-file transform."

DocSplitChunksPlugin — webpack docs

Cost awareness & dependency lifecycle

The parts of "production-ready" that don't show up in a code review but show up on an invoice or in a deprecation ticket six months later.

183●○○○OccasionalBandwidth cost

At what scale does an engineering choice like image format or CDN egress actually start showing up as a real line-item cost, and how does that change how you'd evaluate a "small" optimization?

At small scale, a slightly larger image format or an unoptimized CDN caching policy is a performance concern, not a financial one — the actual dollar difference is negligible. At real production traffic (millions of page views, video or image-heavy content, a global user base pulling from CDN edges repeatedly), bandwidth and egress are billed per byte transferred, so a seemingly small per-asset saving (WebP/AVIF instead of PNG, a longer cache TTL that reduces origin re-fetches, correctly-sized responsive images instead of serving one large image to every viewport) multiplies across enough requests to become a genuine, trackable cost line rather than just a Lighthouse score improvement.

The practical shift this causes: at meaningful scale, a "nice to have" performance optimization can get reframed and prioritized as a cost-reduction initiative with an actual measurable ROI, which is a different (and often more persuasive, to non-engineering stakeholders) argument than "this makes the page faster." It's worth being able to make that connection explicitly — estimating roughly how much a specific change saves in bytes transferred times request volume — rather than treating cost and performance as always the same conversation.

Say it like this

"At small scale it's purely a performance question — the dollar difference is negligible. At real production traffic, bandwidth is billed per byte, so a small per-asset saving like a better image format or a longer cache TTL multiplies across enough requests to become an actual line-item cost, not just a Lighthouse score. I think it's worth being able to make that connection explicitly and estimate the rough savings, because 'this saves real money at our scale' is often a more persuasive argument to non-engineering stakeholders than 'this makes the page faster.'"

DocServe images in modern formats — web.dev

184●○○○OccasionalThird-party script governance

A marketing team wants to add a new third-party tracking or chat-widget script to the site. Should that go through the same review process as a code change, and why?

A third-party script is, from a technical standpoint, arbitrary third-party code running with significant trust on your page — it can access the DOM, read cookies and local storage (subject to same-origin constraints), make network requests, and directly affect performance metrics the engineering team is accountable for, yet it's often added by a non-engineering team through a tag manager with no code review at all, since the whole point of a tag manager is letting non-engineers add scripts without a deploy.

The reasonable middle ground isn't blocking marketing/analytics teams from being able to add tags at all — that defeats the tool's purpose and creates friction that just gets routed around — but having an actual lightweight governance process: a known inventory of what's currently loaded and why, a basic performance/security review before a new script is approved (even a quick check of its bundle size and whether it's genuinely necessary), and periodic audits to catch scripts that were added for a campaign that ended months ago and never got removed. Treating every single tag addition with full code-review rigor is usually disproportionate; treating it as entirely someone else's problem with zero engineering visibility is a real risk to both performance and security that eventually surfaces as an incident.

Say it like this

"A third-party script is genuinely trusted code running on the page — it can touch the DOM, read storage, make requests, and affect performance metrics engineering is accountable for — but it's often added by a non-engineering team through a tag manager with zero code review. I wouldn't block that entirely, since that defeats the tool's purpose, but I'd want a lightweight governance process — a known inventory, a quick performance and necessity check before approval, and periodic audits to catch scripts nobody removed after a campaign ended. Full code-review rigor per tag is overkill; zero visibility is a real risk."

DocEfficiently load third-party JavaScript — web.dev

185●●○○CommonDeprecation strategy

You need to remove or replace a widely-used internal component or API that several other teams still depend on. How do you actually approach deprecating something safely?

The core discipline is giving consumers a real, visible transition path rather than a surprise breaking change — mark the old thing as deprecated (in code, via a JSDoc @deprecated tag that surfaces in editor tooling, and in any relevant documentation) while it's still fully functional, so it keeps working during a defined transition window rather than breaking immediately the moment the decision is made. Communicate the deprecation with a clear timeline and a concrete migration path — ideally a codemod or at minimum explicit before/after examples — to every team actually consuming it, not just an announcement in a channel most of them won't see in time.

Worth having in place before deprecating anything at scale: some way to actually know who's using it (usage analytics on an internal API, or a genuine audit of consumers, since "nobody should still be using this" is often wrong in a large codebase) and a hard removal date that's actually enforced rather than indefinitely postponed, since deprecated-but-never-removed code accumulates as a specific kind of technical debt that's worse than either fully removing it or fully keeping it — it exists as maintenance burden with none of the benefit of either state.

Say it like this

"The core move is giving consumers a real transition path instead of a surprise break — mark it deprecated in code and docs while it still fully works, and communicate a clear timeline with a concrete migration path, ideally a codemod, directly to the teams actually using it. Before deprecating anything widely used, I'd want a real way to know who's actually consuming it, since 'nobody should still be using this' is often wrong at scale, and I'd want an actual enforced removal date — deprecated-but-never-removed code is worse than either removing it or keeping it, since it's pure maintenance burden with none of the benefit."

Docjscodeshift — codemod toolkit

186●○○○OccasionalUpgrade cadence

Beyond reacting to a CVE from an audit, what does a deliberate, proactive dependency upgrade policy actually look like?

Purely reactive upgrading — only touching a dependency when a security audit flags it — means a team is always at least one incident behind, and it means major version upgrades get put off indefinitely since there's no forcing function, which compounds: the longer a major upgrade is deferred, the further behind the codebase drifts and the larger and riskier the eventual jump becomes, since accumulated breaking changes across several skipped majors are much harder to absorb at once than one at a time.

A proactive policy typically means a regular cadence for reviewing and applying minor/patch updates (often automatable — tools like Renovate or Dependabot can open PRs automatically for routine updates, with CI as the safety net), and a deliberate, planned process for major version upgrades specifically — reading the actual migration guide, budgeting real time for it as planned work rather than squeezing it in reactively, and doing it while the gap from the current version is still small rather than waiting until three majors have piled up. The core principle: treating dependency currency as ongoing maintenance work with a cadence, not a fire drill that only happens when something's already on fire.

Say it like this

"Purely reactive upgrading means always being at least one incident behind, and major versions get deferred indefinitely since there's no forcing function — which compounds, because the longer it's deferred, the bigger and riskier the eventual jump becomes. I'd want a regular cadence for routine minor and patch updates, often automated with something like Renovate or Dependabot with CI as the safety net, and a deliberate planned process for major upgrades specifically — actual budgeted time, done while the version gap is still small rather than after three majors have piled up. It's ongoing maintenance with a cadence, not a fire drill triggered by an audit."

DocRenovate documentation

Block 11

Accessibility & UX quality

Increasingly a real filter question, not a nice-to-have — companies with legal exposure or a genuine quality bar ask this specifically. Nine questions.

187●●●○Very commonSemantic HTML

Why is <div onClick={...}> a real accessibility problem instead of just a style preference for a <button>?

A native <button> comes with a set of behaviors built into the browser for free: it's keyboard-focusable by default (in the natural tab order), it's activated by both Enter and Space, and it's announced by screen readers with the "button" role and its accessible name automatically. A <div> with an onClick has none of that — it's not in the tab order, a screen reader announces it as generic unlabeled content, and pressing Enter or Space on it while focused (if it's even focusable) does nothing unless you've manually reimplemented all of that behavior yourself.

Reimplementing it correctly means adding tabIndex="0", a keyboard event handler for both Enter and Space, role="button", and handling focus styling — which is real, easy-to-get-subtly-wrong work to recreate something the native element gives you automatically and correctly. The rule of thumb: use the native interactive element whenever one exists for what you're building, and only build a custom one from a div when there's a genuine reason the native element can't do the job.

Say it like this

"A native button gets keyboard focus, Enter and Space activation, and correct screen reader announcement for free. A div with onClick has none of that unless you rebuild it by hand — tabIndex, keydown handling for both keys, the right role — and that's real work that's easy to get subtly wrong. My default is always the native interactive element when one exists, and I only reach for a div-based custom control when there's a genuine reason the native one can't do what I need."

Docbutton — MDN

188●●●○Very commonFocus management

You open a modal. What has to happen with keyboard focus for it to be accessible, and what happens when it closes?

On open, focus needs to move into the modal — usually to the first focusable element or the modal container itself — otherwise a keyboard or screen reader user's focus stays wherever it was on the page behind the now-visually-obscured modal, completely disconnected from what's actually on screen. Tab and Shift+Tab need to be trapped within the modal's focusable elements ("focus trap"), so tabbing can't silently leave the modal into content that's visually hidden behind an overlay.

On close, focus needs to return to whatever element originally opened the modal (usually the trigger button), not reset to the top of the page or get lost entirely — otherwise a keyboard user closing the modal loses their place in the page and has to re-navigate from scratch to find where they were.

Say it like this

"On open, focus has to move into the modal, usually to the first focusable element, and tab needs to be trapped inside it so keyboard users can't tab into content hidden behind the overlay. On close, focus has to return to whatever triggered the modal — losing that means a keyboard user has to re-navigate the whole page to find their place again. I usually reach for a maintained library like Radix or Headless UI for this rather than hand-rolling the trap logic, since it's easy to get subtly wrong."

DocDialog (Modal) Pattern — ARIA Authoring Practices

189●●○○CommonARIA live regions

A form validation error appears dynamically below an input, without any page reload. Why might a screen reader user never know it happened?

Screen readers only announce content changes automatically for regions the browser knows to watch — by default, DOM content that appears or changes after the initial page load is silent unless it's explicitly marked as a live region. New text simply appearing in the DOM, even visually right below the field, doesn't trigger any announcement on its own.

aria-live="polite" (or role="alert", which implies aria-live="assertive") on the container marks it as a region the screen reader should watch and announce changes to. "Polite" waits for the user to pause before announcing; "assertive" (used sparingly, for genuinely urgent messages) interrupts whatever's currently being read. For form validation, associating the error directly with the input via aria-describedby in addition is also important, so the error is discoverable when the user focuses the field, not only in the initial moment it appears.

Say it like this

"By default, screen readers only announce content in regions they're told to watch — new DOM content appearing silently isn't automatically announced just because it's visible. I mark the error container with aria-live, polite for most cases, assertive only for genuinely urgent messages, and I also wire the error to the input with aria-describedby so it's discoverable on focus too, not just in the instant it first appears."

DocARIA live regions — MDN

190●●○○CommonColor contrast

What's the actual WCAG contrast ratio requirement for normal body text, and why is "it looks readable to me" not sufficient?

WCAG AA requires a contrast ratio of at least 4.5:1 between normal text and its background (3:1 for large text, defined as 18pt+ or 14pt+ bold). This is a measured mathematical ratio between the two colors' relative luminance, not a subjective judgment — two colors that look "fine" to someone with typical vision, under good lighting, on a high-quality monitor, can fail this ratio and be genuinely difficult or impossible to read for people with low vision, color vision deficiencies, or anyone viewing the screen in bright sunlight or on a lower-quality display.

The practical fix is checking actual contrast ratios with a tool (browser DevTools has a built-in checker, or standalone contrast checkers) during design and development, rather than relying on visual judgment from a designer or developer's own screen and eyesight, which isn't representative of the full range of users and viewing conditions.

Say it like this

"AA requires at least 4.5 to 1 for normal text, 3 to 1 for large text — it's a measured luminance ratio, not a subjective call. Colors that look fine on my monitor in good lighting can genuinely fail that ratio and be unreadable for someone with low vision or in bright sunlight, so I check actual contrast ratios with a tool rather than eyeballing it, since 'looks fine to me' isn't representative of the range of users and conditions out there."

DocUnderstanding Contrast (Minimum) — WCAG 2.1

191●●○○CommonAccessible names

A search input has no visible label, just a placeholder saying "Search...". Why is that not accessible, and what's the fix that doesn't require adding visible text?

Placeholder text is not an accessible name — screen readers may or may not announce it depending on the specific software and browser combination, and it disappears the instant the user starts typing, so anyone who briefly looks away or is using voice control loses the field's identity entirely once it has content. It also fails for anyone who needs to identify the field before deciding to interact with it, since some assistive technology doesn't reliably surface placeholder text as an accessible name at all.

The fix that doesn't require any visible label text is aria-label="Search" directly on the input, which gives screen readers a proper accessible name without changing the visual design at all. A visually-hidden (but DOM-present, screen-reader-visible) <label> using a "sr-only" CSS pattern is the more robust alternative, since it behaves more consistently across assistive technology than aria-label does in some edge cases, while still being invisible on screen.

Say it like this

"A placeholder isn't a reliable accessible name — support for announcing it varies, and it vanishes the moment there's any text in the field. Without changing the visual design, aria-label gives the input a real accessible name, or I'll use a visually-hidden label with an sr-only class, which tends to behave more consistently across different screen readers than aria-label does in some edge cases, while staying invisible on screen either way."

DocFailure of Success Criterion 1.3.1 — WCAG techniques

192●○○○OccasionalReduced motion

How would you respect a user's OS-level "reduce motion" preference in a React app that uses CSS animations?

The prefers-reduced-motion media query reflects the user's OS-level accessibility setting — set by people who experience genuine discomfort, dizziness, or vestibular disorder symptoms triggered by motion, not just a stylistic preference. Wrapping animation-related CSS in @media (prefers-reduced-motion: no-preference) means animations only apply when the user hasn't opted for reduced motion, defaulting to no animation (or a much simpler cross-fade instead of movement) otherwise.

For JS-driven animation decisions rather than pure CSS, the same preference is readable via window.matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)').matches, which can be wrapped in a small hook so components can conditionally skip or simplify programmatic animations too, not just CSS-based ones.

Say it like this

"I scope animation CSS inside a prefers-reduced-motion: no-preference media query, so it only applies when the user hasn't opted out at the OS level — this isn't a style preference, some people get real physical discomfort from motion. For anything driven by JS rather than pure CSS, I read the same preference through matchMedia, usually behind a small hook, so components can skip or simplify animation logic too, not just the CSS side."

Docprefers-reduced-motion — MDN

193●●○○CommonAccessible custom widgets

You're building a custom dropdown/combobox from scratch instead of a native <select>. What ARIA and keyboard behavior do you owe it beyond just visual styling?

A native <select> gets correct keyboard navigation, role, and state announcement automatically. Rebuilding it means you now own all of that: the trigger needs role="combobox" with aria-expanded reflecting open/closed state and aria-controls pointing at the listbox; the options list needs role="listbox" with each option as role="option" and aria-selected correctly reflecting the current selection; and keyboard support needs arrow keys to move through options, Enter/Space to select, Escape to close, and typically typeahead (typing a letter jumps to the matching option), matching what users of the native element expect by muscle memory.

This is exactly the kind of thing where reaching for a maintained headless library (Radix, React Aria, Headless UI) that's already solved this correctly and been tested against real assistive technology is usually the right call over hand-rolling it — the surface area for subtle mistakes here is large.

Say it like this

"I owe it the full combobox pattern — role and aria-expanded on the trigger, listbox and option roles with aria-selected tracked correctly, and keyboard support for arrow keys, Enter, Escape, and ideally typeahead, matching what people expect from the native select by muscle memory. Honestly, this is one of the clearest cases for reaching for a headless library like Radix or React Aria instead of hand-rolling it — the surface area for getting some small piece of this wrong is genuinely large."

DocCombobox Pattern — ARIA Authoring Practices

194●○○○OccasionalAutomated a11y testing

You run an automated tool like axe or Lighthouse and it reports zero accessibility violations. Does that mean the page is actually accessible?

No — automated tools reliably catch a real but limited class of issues: missing alt text, insufficient color contrast, missing form labels, invalid ARIA usage, and similar structurally-detectable problems. Industry estimates commonly cited put automated coverage around 30-40% of actual WCAG success criteria; the rest genuinely require a human or assistive-technology judgment call that a static analysis tool can't make.

What automated tools structurally can't verify: whether the tab order is logical and matches visual flow, whether focus management during dynamic interactions (opening a modal, a route change) is correct, whether alt text that's technically present is actually meaningful and descriptive rather than just non-empty, or whether the experience of navigating the whole page with a screen reader or keyboard-only actually makes sense end to end. A zero-violations report is a useful floor, not a ceiling — real coverage needs actual keyboard-only and screen-reader testing on top of it.

Say it like this

"No — automated tools catch a real but limited slice, structurally detectable things like missing alt text or contrast failures, commonly estimated at somewhere around a third of actual WCAG criteria. They can't judge whether tab order is logical, whether focus is managed correctly during dynamic interactions, or whether alt text is actually meaningful rather than just present. I treat a clean automated report as a floor, and still do actual keyboard-only and screen-reader passes for anything that matters."

Docaxe — Deque accessibility testing

195●●○○CommonSkip links

What's a "skip to main content" link, and why does it matter more than it might seem for keyboard users specifically?

Without one, a keyboard-only user has to tab through every single link in the header and navigation menu, on every single page, before ever reaching the actual page content — a mouse user just clicks wherever they want, but tabbing is strictly sequential through the DOM order, so a large nav becomes a genuine repeated obstacle, not a minor inconvenience, especially on a site visited repeatedly.

A skip link is the first focusable element on the page, visually hidden until it receives keyboard focus (so it doesn't clutter the visual design for mouse/pointer users), and jumps focus directly to the main content region when activated — letting a keyboard user bypass the repeated navigation in one keystroke instead of tabbing through it every time.

Say it like this

"Tabbing is strictly sequential, unlike clicking wherever you want with a mouse, so without a skip link a keyboard user has to tab through the entire nav on every single page before reaching content — that's a real repeated cost, not a minor one. A skip link is the first focusable element, hidden until it gets keyboard focus, and jumps straight to main content when activated, so it doesn't affect the visual design for anyone else while giving keyboard users a real shortcut."

DocSkip Navigation Links — WebAIM

Block 12

Security in the frontend

Fewer questions, but the ones that come up are pointed — interviewers use this block to check you understand the browser is a hostile environment. Seven questions.

196●●●●Near-certainXSS

Does React protect you from XSS automatically? Where exactly does that protection stop?

Yes, by default — when you render a value inside JSX like {userInput}, React escapes it before inserting it into the DOM, so even if the string contains <script> tags or event handler attributes, it's rendered as inert text content, not parsed as HTML. This is the normal, default JSX rendering path and it's genuinely safe for arbitrary user-provided strings.

The protection stops completely at dangerouslySetInnerHTML — as the deliberately alarming name suggests, that API bypasses escaping entirely and injects the string as raw HTML, so if it contains attacker-controlled content (unsanitized user input, or content from an API that itself doesn't sanitize), it's a direct XSS vulnerability. Any use of it with anything other than fully trusted, sanitized content needs an actual sanitization library (like DOMPurify) run on the string first.

// safe by default — React escapes this, XSS payload renders as inert text
<p>{userComment}</p>

// dangerous — raw HTML injection, no escaping happens at all
<div dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: userComment }} />

// correct if you genuinely need to render HTML from an untrusted source
<div dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: DOMPurify.sanitize(userComment) }} />
Say it like this

"Normal JSX rendering escapes values automatically, so interpolating user input directly is safe by default — that's genuinely one of React's real security wins. The protection stops entirely at dangerouslySetInnerHTML, which does exactly what its name says — no escaping at all. Anything going through it that isn't fully trusted needs to be run through a real sanitizer like DOMPurify first, not just trusted because 'React usually handles this.'"

DocdangerouslySetInnerHTML — react.dev

197●●●○Very commonToken storage

Where should you store an auth token — localStorage, a regular cookie, or an httpOnly cookie — and why?

localStorage is directly readable by any JavaScript running on the page — which means it's readable by an XSS payload just as easily as by your own app's code. If an attacker gets any script execution on your page through an XSS vulnerability anywhere, a token in localStorage is trivially stolen. A regular (non-httpOnly) cookie has the exact same problem — document.cookie is also readable from JavaScript.

An httpOnly cookie is set by the server and is invisible to JavaScript entirely — document.cookie simply doesn't include it, so even a successful XSS payload running arbitrary JS on the page can't read or exfiltrate it. This shifts the primary risk from XSS-based token theft to CSRF (since the browser automatically attaches cookies to same-site requests), which is why an httpOnly cookie is typically paired with SameSite cookie attributes and CSRF tokens for state-changing requests, rather than being a complete solution on its own.

Say it like this

"localStorage and non-httpOnly cookies are both readable from JavaScript, which means readable by any XSS payload too — if an attacker gets script execution anywhere on the page, the token is gone. An httpOnly cookie is invisible to JavaScript entirely, so XSS can't read it, but that shifts the risk to CSRF instead, since the browser auto-attaches cookies — which is why I'd pair it with SameSite and CSRF protection, not treat httpOnly alone as the whole solution."

DocHttpOnly — OWASP

198●●●○Very commonCSP

What does a Content Security Policy actually do, and how is it a defense-in-depth layer rather than a replacement for sanitizing input?

A CSP is an HTTP header (or meta tag) that tells the browser exactly which sources are allowed to execute as scripts, load as styles, connect as network requests, and so on — anything not on the allowlist is blocked by the browser itself, regardless of how it ended up in the page's HTML.

It's specifically valuable as a second line of defense: even if an XSS vulnerability somehow lets an attacker inject a malicious <script src="evil.com/steal.js"> tag into the page, a correctly configured CSP that doesn't allowlist that origin causes the browser to refuse to execute it — the attack is blocked even though the injection technically succeeded. It doesn't replace sanitizing input or escaping output (those prevent the injection from happening at all, which is strictly better), but it limits the blast radius of a vulnerability that gets through despite those defenses, which is the whole point of layered ("defense in depth") security.

Say it like this

"CSP tells the browser which sources are allowed to execute as scripts or load as resources — anything not allowlisted gets blocked by the browser regardless of how it got into the page. It's a second layer, not a replacement for sanitizing input: even if an XSS injection technically succeeds, a correctly configured CSP can stop the injected script from actually running because its source isn't allowlisted. It limits the damage from a defense that already failed, which is the point of defense in depth."

DocContent Security Policy — MDN

199●●○○CommonCORS

A fetch to a different domain fails with a CORS error in the browser console. What's actually being blocked, and by whom?

CORS is a browser security mechanism, not a network-level or server-level block — the request itself is typically sent and the server typically does respond (you can often see the request succeed in the network tab), but the browser refuses to let your JavaScript code read the response unless the server's response includes headers (Access-Control-Allow-Origin and related) explicitly permitting that origin to access it. It exists to prevent a malicious site from silently making authenticated requests to another site (like your bank) using the browser's ambient cookies and reading the response.

The fix always has to happen server-side — the server needs to send the correct CORS headers allowing your frontend's origin — because it's fundamentally the server's decision about who's allowed to read its responses cross-origin; there's no client-side workaround that doesn't defeat the security purpose of the mechanism entirely.

Say it like this

"CORS is enforced by the browser, not blocked at the network level — the request often actually reaches the server and gets a response, but the browser refuses to let your JS read that response unless the server explicitly allowed your origin via CORS headers. It exists to stop a malicious site from silently making authenticated requests elsewhere using the browser's ambient cookies. The fix is always server-side — there's no legitimate client-side workaround, because the whole point is that the server decides who can read its responses."

DocCross-Origin Resource Sharing (CORS) — MDN

200●●○○CommonClient-side validation

You've built thorough client-side form validation. Is that sufficient security for the endpoint that form submits to?

No — client-side validation runs entirely in the browser, which is fully under the attacker's control. Anyone can bypass your JS validation logic trivially: open DevTools and edit values in memory, disable JavaScript, or simply send a request directly to your API with a tool like curl or Postman, skipping the browser and your form entirely. Client-side validation is genuinely valuable for UX — instant feedback, no round trip for an obvious mistake — but it provides zero actual security guarantee, because nothing forces a real request to have gone through it.

The server has to independently validate and authorize every request as if the client-side checks don't exist, because from the server's perspective, they effectively don't — the server cannot know or verify that a request actually came from your form's validated JS versus a hand-crafted malicious request.

Say it like this

"No — client-side validation is a UX feature, not a security boundary, because it runs somewhere fully under the attacker's control and is trivial to bypass by hitting the endpoint directly with curl or editing values in DevTools. The server has to validate and authorize every request independently, treating client-side checks as if they don't exist, because there's no way for the server to know a request actually went through them."

DocOWASP Top Ten

201●○○○OccasionalDependency security

You run npm audit and it reports a high-severity vulnerability in a transitive dependency you've never heard of. What's your actual process before just running the auto-fix?

First, actually understand what the vulnerability is and whether it's reachable in your specific usage — a vulnerability in a code path your app never actually calls (a rarely-used function of a large library, or a vulnerability that only matters in a Node.js context when your usage is browser-only) may pose zero real risk despite the audit tool flagging it as high severity, since these tools flag based on the dependency's known issues, not on whether your specific usage triggers them.

Second, check whether the automatic fix is actually safe to apply blindly — npm audit fix --force can bump a transitive dependency to a version with breaking changes that has nothing to do with the vulnerability itself, silently introducing a real regression while "fixing" a theoretical risk. The more careful process is checking if a direct dependency has a newer version that resolves the transitive issue cleanly, reading the actual CVE to judge real exploitability and reachability in your app, and testing thoroughly after any forced version bump rather than trusting the audit tool's fix blindly.

Say it like this

"First I check whether the vulnerability is actually reachable in how we use that dependency — a lot of high-severity flags are in code paths an app never touches. Then I'm cautious about the auto-fix specifically, since force-fixing can bump a transitive dependency into breaking changes unrelated to the actual vulnerability. I'd rather read the real CVE, check if a direct dependency update resolves it cleanly, and test thoroughly after any forced bump than trust the tool's fix blindly."

DocAuditing package dependencies — npm docs

202●●○○CommonOpen redirects

Your app supports a ?redirect= query param that sends users to another page after login. Why is this a security risk, and how do you fix it?

If the value of redirect is used to navigate the user without validation, an attacker can craft a link like yourapp.com/login?redirect=https://evil-lookalike.com — the user sees a legitimate-looking link to your real domain, logs in normally (building trust), and is then silently sent to a phishing site that might, for example, ask them to "re-enter their password" for a plausible-sounding reason. This is called an open redirect, and it's specifically dangerous because it borrows your domain's legitimacy to make the phishing attempt far more convincing than a cold, unrelated phishing link would be.

The fix is to only allow redirects to a relative path within your own app (starting with / and not //, since //evil.com is actually a protocol-relative URL that browsers treat as external), or to validate the target against an explicit allowlist of permitted internal paths — never trust and directly redirect to an arbitrary user-supplied absolute URL.

Say it like this

"That's an open redirect — an attacker crafts a link pointing at your real domain with a redirect param aimed at their phishing site, and because the initial link and login flow are genuinely yours, it's far more convincing than a random phishing link would be. The fix is only ever allowing redirect targets that are relative internal paths, explicitly rejecting anything that looks like an absolute or protocol-relative URL, rather than trusting the param and redirecting to it directly."

DocTesting for Client-Side URL Redirect — OWASP

203●●○○CommonClickjacking

What is clickjacking, and how does an HTTP header prevent it without any change to the page's own code?

Clickjacking is loading your legitimate page inside an invisible or disguised <iframe> on an attacker's site, then tricking the user into clicking something that visually appears to belong to the attacker's page but is actually a click landing on your real page underneath — a "Claim your prize" button on the attacker's page secretly overlays your app's "Delete account" or "Transfer funds" button, so the user's genuine click triggers a real, authenticated action on your site without realizing it.

The fix is entirely server-side and requires no page-level code change: the X-Frame-Options header (or the more flexible modern Content-Security-Policy: frame-ancestors directive) tells the browser your page is not allowed to be embedded in an iframe on another origin at all — the browser refuses to render the page inside a foreign frame, which structurally prevents the entire attack regardless of what the attacker's page tries to do, since there's simply no frame for them to overlay anything onto.

Say it like this

"Clickjacking is embedding your real page in an invisible iframe on an attacker's site and tricking the user into clicking something that looks like it belongs to the attacker's page but actually lands on your authenticated app underneath. The fix is the X-Frame-Options header, or frame-ancestors in a CSP — it tells the browser your page simply can't be framed on another origin at all, which prevents the whole attack structurally, with zero page-level code change needed."

DocX-Frame-Options — MDN

204●○○○OccasionalSubresource integrity

You load a script from a third-party CDN. What does Subresource Integrity (SRI) protect against that HTTPS alone doesn't?

HTTPS guarantees the connection to the CDN wasn't tampered with in transit and that you're actually talking to the CDN you think you are — but it says nothing about whether the CDN itself has been compromised, or whether the specific file at that URL was silently swapped for a malicious one on the CDN's end (a breach, a supply-chain compromise, a malicious insider). HTTPS protects the pipe; it doesn't verify what's actually flowing through it matches what you expected.

SRI closes that gap: the <script> tag includes an integrity attribute with a cryptographic hash of the exact expected file content, computed ahead of time. The browser hashes the actual file it receives and refuses to execute it if the hash doesn't match — so even if the CDN serves something different from what you originally linked to, whether through compromise or accidental mutation, the browser catches the mismatch and blocks it rather than silently running whatever arrived.

<script src="https://cdn.example.com/library.js"
        integrity="sha384-oqVuAfXRKap7fdgcCY5uykM6+R9GqQ8K/uxy9rx7HNQlGYl1kPzQho1wx4JwY8wC"
        crossorigin="anonymous"></script>
Say it like this

"HTTPS verifies the connection to the CDN is genuine and untampered in transit, but it says nothing about whether the file sitting on the CDN itself has been swapped for something malicious, through a breach or a compromised supply chain. SRI closes that gap — the script tag includes a hash of the expected content, and the browser refuses to execute the file if what it actually receives doesn't match that hash, so a compromised or mutated CDN file gets blocked rather than silently executed."

DocSubresource Integrity — MDN

205●●○○CommonSupply-chain attacks

Beyond a known CVE in a dependency, what's a supply-chain attack specific to npm packages, and what practical steps reduce the risk?

A supply-chain attack targets the package itself as the delivery mechanism, rather than exploiting a known flaw in legitimate code. Common forms: typosquatting (publishing a malicious package with a name one character off from a popular legitimate one, hoping for an install typo), a legitimate package's maintainer account being compromised and a malicious version published under the real, trusted name, and malicious postinstall scripts that run arbitrary code automatically the moment a package is installed, before anyone's even imported or reviewed it — several real, widely-publicized incidents have used exactly this vector.

Practical mitigations: a committed lockfile with CI verifying it hasn't drifted (so an unexpected version can't silently slip in), being deliberately cautious about adding new dependencies for something trivial that could be a few lines of your own code instead, considering `npm config set ignore-scripts true` in environments where postinstall scripts genuinely aren't needed (accepting that this can break some legitimate packages that rely on them for native builds), and treating a sudden unexplained version bump or ownership change on a dependency as worth a second look rather than auto-updating everything blindly.

Say it like this

"It targets the package as the delivery mechanism itself — typosquatting a popular package's name, a maintainer's account getting compromised and a malicious version published under the real trusted name, or a postinstall script running arbitrary code the moment it's installed, before anyone's even reviewed it. I'd mitigate with a committed, CI-verified lockfile so nothing unexpected slips in silently, being deliberately cautious about adding a dependency for something trivial I could just write myself, and treating a sudden unexplained version bump or ownership change as worth a second look rather than auto-updating blindly."

Docnpm security best practices

Block 13

Server-side rendering & Next.js

Most senior React roles today are effectively "React inside a meta-framework," so this block checks whether you actually understand what the framework is doing versus just knowing its file conventions. Ten questions.

206●●●●Near-certainSSR basics

What problem does server-side rendering actually solve, and what does it not solve on its own?

A pure client-side rendered app ships a mostly-empty HTML shell and a JS bundle; the browser has to download, parse, and execute that JS before anything meaningful appears — so the user (and any crawler that doesn't execute JS well) sees a blank page or a spinner for that entire time. SSR renders the initial HTML on the server and sends a fully-populated page on the very first response, so there's meaningful content to see (and for a crawler to index) before any client-side JavaScript has even loaded.

What it doesn't solve on its own: the page still isn't interactive until the client-side JS downloads and hydrates — attaching event listeners and React's internal state to the server-rendered markup. A page can look fully loaded but not respond to a click yet, which is a real and sometimes confusing gap between "visually complete" and "actually usable" that SSR by itself doesn't close.

Say it like this

"SSR solves the blank-page problem — there's real, crawlable content on the first response instead of an empty shell waiting on JS. What it doesn't solve by itself is interactivity — the page still needs to download and hydrate before clicks actually do anything, so there's a real gap between looking done and being usable that SSR alone doesn't close."

Docreact-dom/server — react.dev

207●●●●Near-certainHydration

What is hydration, and what causes a hydration mismatch error?

Hydration is the process where React takes server-rendered HTML that's already in the DOM and "attaches" itself to it — reusing the existing markup rather than tearing it down and re-rendering from scratch, while wiring up event listeners and internal state so the page becomes interactive. For this to work correctly, React expects the client-side render output to exactly match what the server actually sent.

A hydration mismatch happens when they don't match — classic causes: rendering something based on Date.now() or Math.random() that naturally produces a different value on server versus client, checking typeof window !== 'undefined' to conditionally render browser-only content (different output server vs. client by design), or a browser extension mutating the DOM before React hydrates. React detects the mismatch and, depending on severity, either patches the specific mismatched node or in worse cases discards the server HTML and re-renders the whole thing client-side, which is both a visible flash and a real performance cost that defeats the point of SSR for that content.

Say it like this

"Hydration reuses the server-rendered DOM instead of throwing it away, attaching event listeners and state to it — but that requires the client's render output to exactly match what the server sent. Mismatches usually come from something inherently different between environments, like Date.now, or from deliberately conditional browser-only rendering. React can patch a small mismatch, but a bigger one means discarding the server HTML and re-rendering client-side, which is exactly the flash and cost SSR was supposed to avoid."

DochydrateRoot — react.dev

208●●●○Very commonRendering strategies

In Next.js terms, what's the actual difference between static generation, server-side rendering per request, and incremental static regeneration — and how do you decide which page uses which?

Static generation (SSG) renders the HTML once at build time; every visitor gets the identical pre-built file served instantly from a CDN — ideal for content that's the same for everyone and doesn't change often (marketing pages, blog posts). Server-side rendering per request (SSR) renders fresh HTML on every single request — necessary when the content is genuinely different per request (personalized to the logged-in user, dependent on request-time data that can't be known at build time), at the cost of a real server round-trip on every visit instead of a cached static file.

Incremental static regeneration (ISR) is the middle ground: pages are statically generated but automatically re-generated in the background after a specified time interval (or on-demand via a webhook), so visitors mostly get fast cached static pages while the content still stays reasonably fresh without needing a full rebuild-and-redeploy for every content change. The decision follows the actual data: identical-for-everyone and rarely-changing goes static, genuinely personalized-per-user goes SSR, and shared-but-periodically-updated content (a product catalog, news content) is the classic ISR case.

Say it like this

"Static generation builds the HTML once and serves the identical file to everyone from a CDN — great for content that doesn't vary per visitor. SSR renders fresh on every request, which you actually need when content is personalized or depends on request-time data. ISR is the middle ground — static pages that regenerate on an interval or on-demand, so most visitors get a fast cached page while content still stays reasonably fresh. I pick based on whether the content varies per user and how often it actually changes, not by default habit."

DocRendering — Next.js docs

209●●●○Very commonServer vs client components

In the Next.js App Router, why can't you use onClick directly on a Server Component, and what's the actual rule for where to put "use client"?

A Server Component never runs in the browser at all — it executes on the server and its output is serialized and sent down; there's no client-side JavaScript instance of it to attach an event listener to, which is fundamentally why interactivity like onClick can't work there, not just a missing feature. "use client" marks a module (and everything it imports) as needing to run in the browser, which is required for anything using state, effects, browser APIs, or event handlers.

The rule of thumb for placement is to push the boundary as deep and narrow as possible — mark only the specific leaf component that actually needs interactivity as a client component, rather than marking a whole page or layout, because everything under a "use client" boundary (and everything it imports) gets bundled and shipped to the browser. A common mistake is marking a large parent client just because one small button inside it needs an onClick, which unnecessarily pulls the entire subtree's code into the client bundle when only a small part of it actually needed to be there.

Say it like this

"A Server Component never runs in the browser, so there's no client-side instance to attach an event listener to at all — it's not a restriction so much as a structural fact about where the code executes. The rule for use client is to push it as deep and narrow as possible, onto the specific leaf that actually needs interactivity, because everything under that boundary gets bundled to the client. Marking a whole page client just for one button's onClick unnecessarily ships the rest of that subtree's code to the browser too."

DocServer and Client Composition Patterns — Next.js docs

210●●○○CommonSEO

Why does SEO come up as a reason to choose SSR over a pure client-side SPA, given that Google's crawler does execute JavaScript?

Google's primary crawler does execute JavaScript, but not instantly and not unconditionally — client-side-rendered pages go through a two-phase process where the initial crawl sees the empty shell, and JS execution/indexing of the rendered content happens later, in a separate rendering queue that can be delayed, sometimes significantly. And Google is only one crawler: many other bots (used by other search engines, social media link-preview generators, some SEO and monitoring tools) either don't execute JavaScript at all or do it much less reliably, so they see only the empty shell regardless.

SSR sidesteps all of this uncertainty entirely: the actual meaningful content is present in the very first HTTP response, so every crawler — sophisticated or not — sees real content immediately, with no dependency on JS execution succeeding, timing out, or being supported at all by whatever's doing the crawling.

Say it like this

"Google does run JS, but on a delayed second pass, not instantly on first crawl, and Google isn't the only crawler that matters — plenty of other bots, including social media link previews, don't reliably execute JS at all. SSR removes that uncertainty completely, since real content is in the very first response regardless of whether whatever's crawling can or will run JavaScript."

DocJavaScript SEO basics — Google Search Central

211●●○○CommonStreaming SSR

What does streaming SSR (Suspense on the server) improve over traditional SSR that has to wait for all data before sending any HTML?

Traditional SSR is all-or-nothing per request: the server has to finish fetching everything the page needs before it can send any HTML at all, so one slow data source (a slow analytics widget, a third-party API) delays the entire page's first byte, even for content that had no dependency on that slow piece.

Streaming SSR, using Suspense boundaries on the server, sends the HTML for parts of the page that are ready immediately, with a placeholder for whatever's still loading, and streams in the actual content for the slow parts as they become ready, patching them into the already-sent page — so a slow widget delays only itself, not the whole page's initial response. This directly improves time-to-first-byte and perceived load time for pages with genuinely independent sections of varying speed.

Say it like this

"Without streaming, one slow data source blocks the entire page's HTML from being sent at all, even for parts that have nothing to do with it. Streaming SSR with Suspense boundaries sends the ready parts of the page immediately and streams in the slow sections as they resolve, patching them into the page that's already showing — so a slow widget only delays itself, not the whole response, which is a real win for time-to-first-byte on pages with independently-speed sections."

DocrenderToPipeableStream — react.dev

212●●○○CommonLayout shift on hydration

A component reads window.innerWidth to decide whether to render a mobile or desktop layout. Why does this specifically cause problems with SSR?

The server has no browser window — window doesn't exist server-side, so this code either crashes during server rendering (if accessed unconditionally) or has to be guarded with a check, which then means the server has to render some default (usually assuming desktop, or a fixed guess) since it genuinely cannot know the real client's viewport width at render time. When the client hydrates and finally reads the real window.innerWidth, if it doesn't match the server's guess — a mobile user whose server-rendered HTML assumed desktop — this produces a hydration mismatch, or at minimum a visible layout flash right after hydration as the "wrong" layout swaps to the correct one.
