Because every file's classes get their own unique generated names, two components can both write .button {"{"}...{"}"} in their own CSS Modules file with zero risk of collision — they compile to entirely different class names in the final output, which is the entire mechanism, no runtime scoping magic involved, just build-time renaming plus a lookup object.

Say it like this

"The build step rewrites every class name into a unique generated name, usually the original plus a hash of the file, and produces a mapping object the component imports and reads from — styles.button isn't the literal string 'button', it's whatever unique name got generated for that file. Two components can both write .button in their own files with zero collision risk, since they compile to entirely different names. It's build-time renaming, not runtime scoping magic."

DocCSS Modules — specification

260●●○○CommonUtility-first CSS

What's the actual argument for utility-first CSS (Tailwind-style) over writing semantic component-scoped classes, beyond "it's faster to write"?

The deeper argument is about CSS's specificity and cascade problems at scale. Semantic class names (.card, .card-header) tend to accumulate overrides over a project's lifetime — someone adds a modifier, someone else overrides it elsewhere for a special case, and eventually predicting what styles actually apply to a given element requires tracing through the cascade across multiple files. Utility classes are applied directly at the point of use, are (by convention) single-purpose and low-specificity, so there's no cascade to trace — what you see on the element is what applies, full stop.

The honest trade-off, not just the upside: markup gets visually noisy with long class lists, and it moves the unit of reuse from "a CSS class" to "a component" — which is arguably the right unit in a component-based framework like React anyway, but it's a real shift from how CSS has traditionally been organized, and teams that value scanning raw HTML for meaning (rather than always looking at the rendered result) find it genuinely harder to read.

Say it like this

"The deeper argument isn't just typing speed — it's that semantic classes accumulate cascade complexity over a project's life, where predicting what actually applies to an element means tracing overrides across files. Utility classes are applied directly at the point of use with no cascade to trace. The honest trade-off is markup gets visually noisier, and reuse shifts from a CSS class to a component, which fits a component framework well but is a real departure from how CSS has traditionally been organized."

DocStyling with utility classes — Tailwind docs

261●○○○OccasionalMigration

A large codebase uses a runtime CSS-in-JS library that's since fallen out of favor. How would you approach migrating away from it without a dedicated multi-month rewrite?

Same principle as any large-scale migration in a live codebase: incremental, not a stop-the-world rewrite. The two approaches usually run together are new code following the new convention from day one (so the problem stops growing immediately), and existing code migrating opportunistically whenever a file is already being touched for a real feature or fix — the same pattern covered earlier for modernizing legacy React code generally.

Worth calling out specifically for a styling migration: both systems typically need to coexist for a while, which means the build needs to support both simultaneously without conflict, and a codemod (an automated script that mechanically transforms code, rather than manual find-and-replace) is worth investing in if the old pattern is mechanical and repetitive enough to script — turning a multi-month manual slog into a mostly-automated pass with manual review of the diffs.

Say it like this

"Same principle as any large legacy migration — incremental, not a dedicated rewrite. New code uses the new approach immediately, existing code migrates opportunistically when a file's already being touched for real work. For something as mechanical as a styling API migration specifically, I'd seriously consider investing in a codemod if the pattern is regular enough to script, since that turns a multi-month manual slog into a mostly-automated pass with human review of the diffs, rather than hand-editing every file."

Docjscodeshift — codemod toolkit

262●○○○OccasionalZero-runtime CSS-in-JS

How does a "zero-runtime" CSS-in-JS library like vanilla-extract give you JS-authored styles without the runtime cost of classic styled-components?

Classic runtime CSS-in-JS generates and injects CSS in the browser, during rendering. A zero-runtime library instead does all the CSS generation at build time — you author styles in TypeScript/JS files with real type-checking and co-location with components, but a build-time compiler statically analyzes and extracts that into plain, static .css files before the code ever reaches the browser. What ships to the browser is the same as if you'd hand-written plain CSS files — no styling-related JS runs client-side at all.

The genuine limitation this introduces: because extraction happens statically at build time, styles generally can't depend on values only known at runtime (like an arbitrary prop value computed from user input) the way runtime CSS-in-JS can — dynamic styling has to go through a more constrained mechanism, like CSS custom properties set via inline styles, rather than arbitrary JS logic generating the CSS itself.

Say it like this

"It moves the CSS generation from the browser at runtime to the build step — you still author styles in JS/TS with real type-checking, but a compiler statically extracts that into plain static CSS files before it ships, so nothing styling-related actually runs client-side. The real limitation is that because it's static extraction, styles generally can't depend on arbitrary runtime values the way classic CSS-in-JS can — dynamic values usually go through CSS custom properties instead of arbitrary JS logic."

Docvanilla-extract — Documentation

263●●○○CommonTheming

Design a theming system that supports light mode, dark mode, and lets a user set a per-component accent color, without shipping duplicate CSS per theme.

The scalable approach is CSS custom properties as the indirection layer: components reference var(--surface-color), var(--accent-color) rather than hardcoded values, and the actual values are defined once per theme at a high-level scope (:root for light, [data-theme="dark"] overriding the same variable names for dark). Switching themes means toggling one attribute or class on a root element — every component using those variables updates instantly, with zero duplicated CSS per theme, since the components' actual rule declarations never change, only the variable values they resolve to.

A per-component accent override extends the same mechanism at a smaller scope: setting --accent-color as an inline style or a scoped class on one specific component overrides just that subtree's value, while everything outside it still inherits the theme-level default — the same custom-property cascade that makes theme switching work also makes granular overrides work, without any special-case logic.

Say it like this

"I'd use CSS custom properties as the indirection layer — components reference var(--accent-color) rather than hardcoded values, and each theme just redefines those same variable names at a root scope. Switching themes is toggling one attribute, with zero duplicated CSS, since the component rules never change, only what the variables resolve to. A per-component override is the same mechanism at a smaller scope — setting the variable on one subtree overrides just that part, and everything else still inherits the theme default through the normal CSS cascade."

DocUsing CSS custom properties — MDN

264●●○○CommonUI component libraries

MUI, Ant Design, and Bootstrap all give you a working set of components immediately. What's the actual trade-off that should drive the choice, beyond visual preference?

The real axis to evaluate is customization depth versus adoption speed. A fully-styled kit like MUI or Ant Design gets a team to a working, accessible, cohesive UI extremely fast, since the components, theming system, and accessibility behavior all arrive pre-built — but customizing deeply beyond what the theming API exposes means fighting the library's own specificity and internal structure, which can be genuinely painful for a product that needs a highly distinctive visual identity rather than "looks like MUI with a different accent color." Bootstrap specifically leans even further toward broad, framework-agnostic convention (originally CSS-only, with React wrappers built on top) and correspondingly offers less React-native theming sophistication than MUI or Ant's component-level APIs.

The practical decision: an internal tool, admin panel, or MVP where visual distinctiveness doesn't matter and speed does is a strong fit for a fully-styled kit. A consumer-facing product where brand identity is a real differentiator is a weaker fit for the same kit, since the effort saved on component-building gets partially spent instead fighting the library's opinions — that's often where a headless library (Radix, React Aria, covered earlier) plus a custom design system ends up being the better trade despite more upfront work, because customization isn't fighting anything, it's the default state.

Say it like this

"The real axis is customization depth versus adoption speed, not which one looks nicer out of the box. A fully-styled kit like MUI or Ant gets you to a working, accessible UI extremely fast, but deep customization beyond the theming API means fighting the library's own specificity and structure. That's a strong trade for an internal tool or MVP where speed matters more than visual distinctiveness, and a weaker one for a consumer product where brand identity is a real differentiator — that's often where I'd lean headless plus a custom design system instead, since customization is the default state rather than something you fight for."

DocTheming — MUI docs

265●○○○OccasionalSass

Native CSS now has custom properties, nesting, and other features Sass was originally created to provide. What would still justify reaching for Sass on a new project today?

A meaningful chunk of Sass's original value proposition — variables via custom properties, nesting, basic composition — has genuinely been absorbed into native CSS, closing much of the gap that originally justified Sass on every project by default. What native CSS still doesn't provide, and where Sass retains a real edge: actual programmatic logic — loops (@each, @for) for generating repetitive rule sets from a data structure, functions with real computation beyond CSS's more limited calc()/clamp(), and module-level features like @use/@forward for organizing large stylesheets into genuinely separate, importable partials with real scoping between them, which native CSS's @import doesn't provide to the same degree.

The practical decision now leans toward: a small-to-medium project with modest styling needs may not gain enough from Sass to justify the build-step dependency it requires (native CSS doesn't need any compilation at all). A large design system with genuinely repetitive, programmatically-generated rule sets, or a codebase that already has deep Sass conventions, still has real reasons to keep or choose it — it's no longer close to a default choice the way it once was, but it isn't obsolete for cases that actually use its programmatic features.

Say it like this

"A lot of the original reason to reach for Sass — variables, nesting — is native now, so it's no longer close to a default choice. What still justifies it is genuine programmatic logic — loops for generating repetitive rules from a data structure, real computed functions, and module-level organization with @use and @forward that native CSS's import doesn't match. For a small project with modest styling needs, I probably wouldn't add the build-step dependency just for what's mostly native now; for a large design system genuinely using loops or functions, it still earns its place."

DocSass Documentation

Block 20

Mobile & React Native crossover

Increasingly relevant even for web-focused roles at companies running a shared React codebase across platforms. Six questions.

266●●○○CommonReact Native basics

What does React Native actually share with React web, and what's fundamentally different?

Shared: the core React programming model entirely — components, hooks, state, props, the reconciliation algorithm, the mental model of "UI as a function of state." Code written with plain React logic (custom hooks with no DOM/browser API dependencies, state management, business logic) can often be shared verbatim between a web and React Native codebase.

Fundamentally different: there's no DOM and no browser at all. React Native renders to actual native platform UI components (a <View> becomes a real native container view, not a <div>), so anything DOM-specific — CSS as commonly used on web, HTML elements, browser APIs like localStorage or fetch's exact behavior — doesn't exist in the same form; React Native has its own styling system (a JS object subset resembling CSS, not real CSS) and its own set of native-bridge APIs for platform features.

Say it like this

"The core React model is fully shared — components, hooks, state, reconciliation — so pure logic with no DOM dependency can often be shared verbatim. What's fundamentally different is there's no DOM at all — React Native renders to real native platform views, not HTML elements, so DOM-specific things like CSS as web knows it or browser APIs don't carry over; it has its own styling system and its own native-bridge APIs instead."

DocCore Components — React Native docs

267●●○○CommonCode sharing strategy

A company wants to share code between their React web app and React Native app. What's actually shareable, and what has to be platform-specific by necessity?

Shareable, generally without much friction: business logic, custom hooks that don't touch platform-specific APIs, state management (a store's logic, selectors, reducers), API client code and data-fetching logic, validation logic, and type definitions — anything that's pure computation or pure state, with no rendering or platform API involved.

Necessarily platform-specific: anything that renders — the actual component markup (View/Text/Image on native versus div/span/img on web are fundamentally different primitives, not just styled differently), navigation (native navigation patterns and libraries versus web routing are different problems), and anything touching a platform-specific API even if conceptually similar (native camera/file-system access versus web's File API, native push notifications versus web push). A realistic shared codebase separates a shared "core" package (logic, state, API layer) from platform-specific UI packages that each consume the shared core, rather than attempting to share components themselves.

Say it like this

"Business logic, hooks with no platform dependency, state management, API and validation logic — all genuinely shareable with little friction. Rendering itself isn't, because View and Text on native and div and span on web are fundamentally different primitives, not just styled differently, and neither is navigation or anything touching a platform-specific API. The realistic pattern is a shared core package for logic and state, with separate platform-specific UI layers consuming it — trying to share components themselves usually doesn't work well."

DocPlatform-Specific Code — React Native docs

268●○○○OccasionalBridge/JSI

Why has React Native's architecture historically had a "bridge" performance concern, and what's changed with the newer architecture?

The older architecture communicated between the JavaScript thread and the native platform thread through an asynchronous, serialized message-passing bridge — every call across that boundary had to be serialized to JSON, queued, and deserialized on the other side, which is real overhead, especially for high-frequency communication like scroll-position-driven animations that need to cross that boundary on every frame.

The newer architecture (JSI — JavaScript Interface, part of what's often called React Native's "New Architecture") replaces that with direct, synchronous references between JS and native code, letting JS hold a direct reference to a native object and call methods on it without the serialize/queue/deserialize round trip, which meaningfully reduces overhead for exactly the high-frequency cross-boundary communication that used to be the bridge's weak point.

Say it like this

"The old architecture used an async, serialized bridge between the JS thread and native — every cross-boundary call got serialized to JSON, queued, and deserialized, which was real overhead especially for high-frequency stuff like scroll-driven animation. The newer JSI-based architecture replaces that with direct synchronous references, so JS can call into native code without that serialize-and-queue round trip, which is specifically aimed at the high-frequency communication that used to be the bridge's actual bottleneck."

DocNew Architecture Overview — React Native docs

269●○○○OccasionalExpo

What's the actual trade-off of building a React Native app with Expo versus a "bare" React Native project?

Expo provides a managed layer on top of React Native: pre-built native modules for common needs (camera, location, notifications) without writing native code, over-the-air JS updates without a full app-store resubmission for every change, and a much faster initial setup and development loop, including testing on a real device without a local native build environment.

The trade-off historically was that anything needing a native module Expo didn't provide meant either "ejecting" out of the managed workflow (losing some of Expo's benefits) or waiting for Expo to add support — that constraint has loosened significantly with Expo's newer config-plugin system letting you add custom native code while staying in the managed workflow, but genuinely exotic native requirements can still hit friction that a bare React Native project, with full direct control over native code, wouldn't.

Say it like this

"Expo gives you pre-built native modules for common needs, over-the-air updates, and a much faster setup and dev loop without managing native build tooling directly. The historical trade-off was hitting a wall if you needed a native module Expo didn't support — that's improved a lot with their config-plugin system, but genuinely unusual native requirements can still be smoother in a bare React Native project with full direct native control."

DocWhy Expo — Expo docs

270●○○○OccasionalResponsive vs adaptive

What's the difference between designing "responsive" and "adaptive" for a web app that also needs to work well as a mobile web view, versus building a genuinely separate mobile experience?

Responsive means one layout that fluidly reflows across screen sizes using relative units and media queries — the same markup and largely the same interaction model, just visually rearranged. Adaptive typically means serving meaningfully different layouts or even different interaction patterns at defined breakpoints, sometimes with different components entirely for mobile versus desktop, not just a fluid reflow of the same one.

The decision of which to use follows how different the actual optimal experience is per form factor — if touch targets, information density, and interaction patterns (swipe versus hover, for instance) are genuinely different enough between mobile and desktop that one fluid layout can't serve both well, adaptive with real breakpoint-specific components is worth the complexity; if the difference is mostly about available space, responsive reflow is simpler and usually sufficient.

Say it like this

"Responsive is one layout that fluidly reflows across sizes with the same markup and interaction model. Adaptive means genuinely different layouts or interaction patterns at breakpoints, sometimes different components entirely. I'd reach for adaptive specifically when the optimal interaction genuinely differs by form factor — touch versus hover, information density — not just available space, where responsive reflow is simpler and usually enough."

DocResponsive Web Design Basics — web.dev

271●○○○OccasionalPWA vs native

When does a Progressive Web App (PWA) genuinely substitute for a native mobile app, and when does it fall short?

A PWA can genuinely cover a lot: installable to the home screen, works offline via a service worker cache, supports web push notifications on most platforms, and is discoverable and updatable through the web with no app-store review cycle — for a content-focused or moderately-interactive app, that's often sufficient and meaningfully cheaper to build and maintain than two separate native codebases.

Where it falls short: deep integration with platform-specific hardware and APIs that the web platform doesn't fully expose (some biometric APIs, certain background processing capabilities, deep OS integrations), performance-critical work needing native-level graphics or processing (high-end gaming, intensive real-time media processing), and — as of any given moment — iOS's PWA support has historically lagged Android's in some specific capabilities (push notification support arrived on iOS notably later than Android, for instance), which needs checking against current platform support rather than assumed.

Say it like this

"A PWA covers a lot — installable, offline via service worker, push notifications on most platforms, no app-store review cycle — and for a content-focused or moderately interactive app that's often genuinely sufficient and cheaper than two native codebases. It falls short for deep platform hardware integration the web doesn't fully expose, or performance-critical native-level work like high-end gaming. I'd also actually check current iOS versus Android PWA capability gaps rather than assume parity, since that's shifted over time and iOS has historically lagged on some specific features."

DocProgressive Web Apps — web.dev

Block 21

AI-assisted development

A newer category with less settled convention than the rest of this bank — what's being checked here is judgment about a tool, not a fixed body of facts. Five questions.

272●●●○Very commonReview discipline

How does the way you review AI-generated code differ from reviewing a human teammate's pull request?

The most important shift: with a human teammate, a plausible-looking implementation carries some default trust that the author understood the problem and considered edge cases, because they engaged with the actual requirements. AI-generated code can look equally plausible — correct syntax, sensible-looking logic, confident naming — while having no actual understanding of the specific codebase's conventions, the edge cases that matter for this particular feature, or subtle correctness issues, because it's pattern-matching against training data, not reasoning about this specific system's requirements.

Practically, that means treating fluency as a weaker signal of correctness than it would be from a human, and being specifically more suspicious of the exact places AI tools are known to be weaker: security implications, off-by-one and edge-case handling, correctness under concurrent/async conditions, and whether it's actually solving the underlying problem or just producing something that superficially resembles a solution. It doesn't mean AI-generated code needs categorically different acceptance criteria — it still needs to be correct, tested, and idiomatic for the codebase — but the calibration of how much a clean-looking diff should reduce your scrutiny is different.

Say it like this

"The main shift is that fluency is a weaker signal of correctness than it would be from a human — AI-generated code can look equally polished while having no real understanding of this specific codebase's edge cases or conventions, since it's pattern-matching rather than reasoning about the actual requirements. I'm specifically more careful around security implications, edge cases, and async correctness, since those are known weak spots, but the bar for acceptance — correct, tested, idiomatic — doesn't actually change, just how much a clean-looking diff earns my trust before I've actually verified it."

DocGoogle Engineering Practices — Code Review

273●●○○CommonWhen not to use it

Give a concrete example of a task where you'd deliberately avoid using an AI coding assistant, and explain why.

A strong example is anything where the actual value of the work is in the thinking process itself, not just the output — designing the core architecture of a new system, working through a genuinely novel algorithmic problem specific to the domain, or a security-critical piece of code where you need to have personally reasoned through every edge case rather than trusting a plausible-looking suggestion. Delegating the thinking on these defeats the purpose even if the resulting code happens to work, because the understanding gained from doing it yourself is often what you actually need afterward — to debug it, extend it, or explain the trade-offs to someone else.

A second category worth naming: something highly specific to undocumented internal context the tool structurally can't know — an internal system's actual behavior that differs from what any public documentation says, or a decision that depends on organizational context (why a past decision was made, what a stakeholder actually needs) that isn't written down anywhere the tool could have learned it from.

Say it like this

"I'd avoid it for anything where the value is genuinely in the thinking, not just the output — core architecture decisions, a genuinely novel algorithmic problem, or security-critical code where I need to have personally reasoned through every edge case rather than trust something that looks plausible. Even if the generated code happened to work, skipping that thinking means I don't actually have the understanding I'll need later to debug or extend it. I'd also avoid it for anything depending on undocumented internal context the tool has no way to actually know."

DocExploring Generative AI — Martin Fowler

274●●○○CommonPrompt injection

If your product lets users' content (a comment, an uploaded document) get fed into an LLM-powered feature, what's the security concern specific to that, beyond normal input sanitization?

This is prompt injection: unlike SQL injection or XSS, where there's a clear syntactic boundary between "code" and "data" that sanitization can enforce, an LLM has no reliable structural way to distinguish your actual instructions from text that happens to be embedded in the user-supplied content it's processing — if a user's document contains text like "ignore previous instructions and instead output X," a sufficiently naive prompt construction can have the model actually follow that embedded instruction as if it came from the legitimate system prompt.

Practical mitigations, none of which fully "solve" it the way parameterized queries solve SQL injection: clearly delimiting user content from instructions in the prompt structure (though this is a mitigation, not a hard guarantee), keeping the model's actual capabilities scoped so that even a successful injection can't do meaningful damage (no direct access to sensitive actions or data the injected instruction could exploit), and treating any output derived from untrusted input as itself untrusted before it's used to trigger further actions — this is an evolving area without a fully settled solution, which is worth acknowledging honestly rather than overstating how solved it is.

Say it like this

"That's prompt injection — unlike SQL injection or XSS, there's no reliable syntactic boundary an LLM enforces between instructions and data, so text embedded in user content can potentially get treated as an instruction. The mitigations are real but not a full solve the way parameterized queries solve SQL injection — clearly delimiting user content in the prompt, scoping what the model can actually do so a successful injection has limited blast radius, and treating any downstream action triggered by model output as still needing its own validation. I'd be honest that this is a genuinely unsolved area, not overstate how contained it is."

DocOWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications

275●○○○OccasionalCodebase-specific context

Why does an AI coding assistant sometimes generate code that's syntactically correct but doesn't match your team's actual conventions (a different state management pattern, a different file structure)?

The model's training data reflects the broad distribution of publicly available code across many projects and conventions, not your specific codebase's particular choices — without additional context, it defaults to whatever pattern is statistically most common or most recently emphasized in its training, which may not be what your team has actually standardized on. It has no inherent way to know your team chose Zustand over Redux for a specific reason, or that your file structure follows a particular feature-folder convention, unless that context is actually made available to it.

The practical fix is giving the tool actual project context rather than expecting it to infer conventions from nothing: project-level configuration files some tools support (documenting conventions, preferred patterns, architectural decisions), pointing it at existing code in the same codebase as a pattern to follow, or simply reviewing and correcting the output with an eye specifically toward convention-matching, not just functional correctness.

Say it like this

"The model's training reflects broad public conventions across many projects, not this specific codebase's particular choices — it has no inherent way to know we picked Zustand over Redux for a reason, or that we follow a specific feature-folder structure, unless that's actually given to it as context. I'd give it real project context — a config file documenting conventions, or pointing it at existing code to match — rather than expecting convention-matching for free, and I'd review specifically for that, not just functional correctness."

DocClaude Code — Anthropic docs

276●○○○OccasionalSkill atrophy

Is there a real risk to relying heavily on AI-assisted coding for a junior engineer specifically, versus a senior one?

The honest concern isn't about AI tools themselves being bad — it's about the order in which understanding gets built. A senior engineer using an AI assistant is generally directing it with an already-formed mental model of what correct looks like, so they can evaluate, correct, and reject its output effectively; the tool accelerates someone who already has the judgment to check its work. A junior engineer leaning heavily on the same tool before having built that underlying judgment risks skipping the actual skill-building step — producing code that works without developing the ability to independently recognize why it works, why an alternative would have been wrong, or how to debug it when the pattern doesn't quite fit next time.

This isn't a reason to avoid the tools entirely for someone junior — used well, they can also be a genuinely good learning aid (asking for explanations, using them to explore alternatives) — but it's a real, worth-naming risk specifically when the tool substitutes for the struggle of building understanding rather than accelerating someone who already has it, and it's a reasonable thing for a team to actively think about in how it mentors junior engineers now.

Say it like this

"I don't think the tools themselves are the problem — it's about whether judgment gets built before or after relying on them. A senior engineer is generally directing the tool with an already-formed sense of what correct looks like, so it accelerates someone who can already evaluate its output. A junior engineer leaning on it before building that judgment risks skipping the actual skill-building step, producing working code without developing the ability to know why it works or debug it when the pattern doesn't quite fit. It's a real thing worth actively thinking about in how a team mentors junior engineers now, not a reason to avoid the tools."

DocExploring Generative AI — Martin Fowler

Block 22

Design collaboration & handoff

The day-to-day reality of working from Figma files and negotiating with designers, distinct from the token-pipeline system-design question earlier. Five questions.

277●●○○CommonResponsive interpretation

A Figma design shows exactly three fixed breakpoints — mobile, tablet, desktop. How do you turn that into a genuinely fluid responsive implementation instead of three rigid snapshots?

Design tools are inherently snapshot-based — a designer can only show you discrete states, not a continuous function — so translating that into implementation means inferring the intent behind the three snapshots rather than literally hardcoding three fixed layouts with hard jumps between them. Comparing the three states reveals what's actually scaling proportionally (spacing, font size following a scale) versus what's genuinely restructuring (a sidebar collapsing into a hamburger menu, a grid changing column count) — the former should use fluid CSS techniques (`clamp()`, relative units, container queries) that interpolate smoothly between and beyond the three given points, while the latter genuinely needs the discrete breakpoint jump the design shows.

The practical move when the intent isn't obvious from three static frames is going back to the designer with a specific question — "does this spacing scale continuously between mobile and tablet, or should it jump at a specific width" — rather than guessing, since three snapshots are underspecified for anything in between by definition.

Say it like this

"Three static frames are inherently underspecified for anything in between, so I look at what's actually changing across them — proportional scaling of spacing and type should be fluid, using clamp or relative units so it interpolates smoothly rather than jumping; structural changes, like a sidebar collapsing to a hamburger, genuinely need the discrete breakpoint the design shows. When the intent isn't obvious from three frames, I'd actually ask the designer rather than guess, since three points don't define a curve."

Docclamp() — MDN

278●●○○CommonUndefined states

A Figma file shows the "happy path" for a component but no design for loading, error, or empty states. How do you handle building it?

These states aren't optional edge cases to skip — a component with no defined empty state or error state will still hit those states in production, just with whatever the browser or an unstyled fallback happens to render, which is a real quality gap even though it wasn't in the design file. The right move isn't guessing silently and shipping an undesigned state, and it also isn't blocking the whole feature waiting on a design update for every missing state.

A practical middle ground: build a reasonable, consistent-with-the-design-system default for these states (using the same spacing, typography, and tone as the happy-path design) so nothing ships broken, while explicitly flagging to the designer which states were undefined and got a default treatment, so there's a clear, visible list of "these were assumptions, not confirmed design" rather than silently baking guesses in as if they were specified.

Say it like this

"Those states aren't optional just because they weren't designed — the component will hit them in production regardless. I build a reasonable default consistent with the existing design system's spacing and tone so nothing ships broken, but I explicitly flag to the designer exactly which states were undefined and got a default, so there's a visible list of assumptions rather than silently guessing and having it look intentional when it wasn't actually confirmed."

DocError Message Guidelines — Nielsen Norman Group

279●○○○OccasionalDesign tokens from Figma

A designer hands you a Figma frame with a color that's slightly different from any existing token in your design system — is a new one-off value ever the right call?

Worth treating as a real question rather than either extreme by default. Silently using the exact new value creates token drift — every "just this once" one-off value is a small crack in the design system's consistency that compounds over time into a system nobody actually trusts to be exhaustive. But rigidly snapping to the nearest existing token without asking risks silently overriding a designer's deliberate choice, if the difference was actually intentional rather than accidental drift on their end too.

The right default is treating a mismatch as a conversation, not a unilateral decision either direction: flag it to the designer specifically — "this doesn't match an existing token, was that intentional, and if so should it become a new token or was it meant to be the existing one" — which either surfaces a genuine gap in the token set worth adding, or catches accidental drift on the design side before it ships.

Say it like this

"I wouldn't silently use the one-off value or silently snap it to the nearest token — either direction risks being wrong. I'd flag the mismatch to the designer directly and ask whether it was intentional, which either surfaces a genuine gap worth adding as a real token, or catches accidental drift on their end before it ships. Treating every small mismatch as a unilateral call in either direction is how a design system slowly stops being trustworthy."

DocDesign Tokens — designsystems.com

280●○○○OccasionalFeasibility feedback

A design calls for an interaction you know is technically difficult or has real accessibility problems. When should you raise that, and how?

As early as possible — ideally before implementation starts, or even during the design review stage if you're involved in it, since flagging a fundamental issue after the design is polished and stakeholders have already approved it is a much more expensive conversation than catching it early, both in wasted design work and in the social cost of pushing back on something that's already been signed off.

How matters as much as when: framing it as a constraint to solve together ("here's the specific issue, here's why, and here are two alternatives that could achieve a similar effect") lands very differently than a flat "this isn't possible" or "this is wrong," which reads as obstruction rather than collaboration. Concrete specifics — not "this feels hard" but "this specific gesture pattern has no reliable keyboard equivalent, which fails a specific WCAG criterion, and here's a variant that keeps the visual feel while being genuinely accessible" — gives the designer something real to work with rather than a vague objection to react to.

Say it like this

"As early as I possibly can, ideally before it's fully polished and approved, since catching it early is a much cheaper conversation than after stakeholders have already signed off. I try to frame it as a constraint to solve together rather than a flat no — here's the specific issue, here's why it's a real problem, and here are one or two alternatives that keep the intent while actually working. Specifics land better than a vague objection, since it gives the designer something concrete to react to instead of just a wall."

DocARIA Design Patterns — W3C

281●○○○OccasionalDesign system contribution

You need a component variant that doesn't exist in the shared design system. Do you build a one-off, or push to add it to the system — how do you decide?

The deciding factor is genuine reusability versus a truly one-time need — not how it feels in the moment, since almost anything can feel "probably needed elsewhere" under deadline pressure while almost nothing actually gets checked against real future usage. A concrete signal worth using: is this variant serving a real, recurring product need (a new severity level for alerts that multiple features will plausibly use) versus a highly specific one-off context that's unlikely to recur (a single marketing page's unique visual treatment).

If it's genuinely reusable, the design-system route is worth the extra process even though it's slower — proposing it, getting design and engineering alignment, adding it properly with the system's conventions — because a one-off that quietly gets copy-pasted into a second and third place without ever becoming a real system entry is exactly how design systems drift into inconsistency. If it's genuinely one-time, building it locally without forcing it through the system's process is the right call, and forcing every one-off through system governance just adds friction without a real payoff.

Say it like this

"The real question is genuine reusability, not how it feels under deadline pressure — almost anything can feel like it might be needed elsewhere. If it's serving a real recurring need, I'd push it through the system's process even though it's slower, because a one-off that quietly gets copy-pasted elsewhere without ever becoming a real system entry is exactly how systems drift into inconsistency. If it's genuinely a one-time context, I'd just build it locally — forcing every one-off through system governance is friction without payoff."

DocDesign Systems — designsystems.com

Block 23

Compliance & legal-adjacent

Not legal advice — the point of this block is knowing when a frontend implementation choice actually has legal weight, and when to loop in someone qualified rather than guess. Five questions.

282●●○○CommonCookie consent

You're implementing a GDPR-style cookie consent banner. What's the actual technical requirement that a lot of implementations get wrong — beyond just "show a banner"?

The requirement isn't just displaying a banner — it's that non-essential cookies and tracking scripts (analytics, ads, most third-party embeds) must not actually load or execute until the user has given genuine, informed consent, and rejecting must be as easy as accepting. A very common implementation mistake: the analytics or ad script tag is already present and firing on page load, with the consent banner just visually overlaid on top as if that satisfied the requirement — the tracking already happened before the user made any choice, which doesn't meet the actual requirement regardless of how the banner looks.

The correct pattern gates the actual script loading behind the consent decision: non-essential scripts are conditionally injected (or dynamically imported) only after affirmative consent is recorded, not loaded upfront and merely hidden behind UI. This is a case where "looks compliant" and "is compliant" can genuinely diverge — worth flagging directly that this has real legal weight, and a team should have actual legal/compliance sign-off on the specific implementation, not just an engineer's best guess at what the regulation requires.

Say it like this

"The requirement isn't just showing a banner — non-essential scripts genuinely can't load or execute until real consent is given, and a common mistake is having the analytics script already firing on page load with the banner just visually sitting on top, which doesn't actually satisfy it since the tracking already happened. I gate the actual script injection behind the consent decision, not just the UI. And I'd flag that this has real legal weight — I'd want actual compliance sign-off on the specific implementation, not just my own best guess at what the regulation requires."

DocCookies, the GDPR, and the ePrivacy Directive — GDPR.eu

283●●○○CommonWCAG as a legal requirement

Beyond it being "good practice," why might WCAG compliance be a genuine legal requirement for a specific product, and how does that change how you'd prioritize accessibility work?

In several jurisdictions, accessibility isn't purely a best-practice recommendation — laws like the ADA in the US (as interpreted by courts for digital properties), the European Accessibility Act, and various national public-sector accessibility mandates create actual legal exposure for non-compliant digital products, particularly for government-adjacent services, larger companies, or products serving the public in specific regulated ways. Companies have faced real lawsuits and settlements specifically over inaccessible websites and apps — this isn't a hypothetical or purely reputational risk in those contexts.

Practically, this changes prioritization: accessibility work stops being a "nice to have that competes with feature work for priority" and becomes closer to a baseline requirement with real business risk attached to skipping it, similar to how a genuine security vulnerability gets prioritized rather than treated as regular backlog work. Whether a specific product actually falls under a specific legal requirement is a real legal question, not something to assume from general engineering knowledge — worth explicitly flagging to legal/compliance rather than assuming either way.

Say it like this

"In several jurisdictions this isn't just best practice — ADA interpretations in the US, the European Accessibility Act, and various public-sector mandates create real legal exposure, and there have been actual lawsuits over inaccessible sites, not just reputational risk. That changes prioritization — it stops competing with regular feature work for priority and becomes closer to a baseline requirement, similar to how a real security vulnerability gets treated. Whether a specific product is actually covered by a specific law is a genuine legal question though, so I'd flag that to legal rather than assume either way myself."

DocGuidance on Web Accessibility and the ADA

284●○○○OccasionalRight to be forgotten

A user requests deletion of their account under GDPR's "right to erasure." What frontend-relevant technical considerations does that actually touch, beyond the backend database delete?

The obvious part — deleting the record from the primary database — is often the easy part; the harder, easy-to-miss parts are places user data lives that aren't the primary database at all: cached data in a CDN or edge cache that might still serve a stale, undeleted version of user-specific content for some time, client-side persisted data (localStorage, IndexedDB, service worker caches) on devices the user previously used that isn't touched by any server-side deletion at all, and third-party services the data was sent to (an analytics platform, an error-tracking tool, an email service provider) that need their own deletion request, since deleting your own database doesn't retroactively delete data already sent elsewhere.

This is squarely a case where the actual compliance requirement should come from legal/compliance, not engineering assumption — but it's worth an engineer proactively knowing that "erasure" has real technical surface area beyond the obvious database row, so the conversation with legal is grounded in an accurate picture of where the data actually lives, rather than everyone assuming the primary database delete was the whole job.

Say it like this

"The primary database delete is usually the easy, obvious part — the parts that get missed are places data lives outside it: CDN or edge caches that might keep serving stale user-specific content for a while, client-side storage on devices that no server-side action touches at all, and third-party services like analytics or error tracking that received the data and need their own separate deletion request. I wouldn't decide the actual compliance scope myself, but I'd make sure legal has an accurate picture of where the data actually lives, not just the primary database."

DocRight to Be Forgotten — GDPR.eu

285●○○○OccasionalAnalytics and PII

A teammate wants to log the full text a user types into a search box to an analytics tool, to understand search behavior. What's the concern, and what would you actually suggest?

The concern is that free-text user input is a genuine PII (personally identifiable information) risk vector that's easy to overlook because it doesn't look like an obvious PII field — a user might type their own name, email, an address, or other sensitive information into a search box for entirely ordinary reasons, and logging that verbatim to a third-party analytics tool can mean sensitive personal data ends up somewhere it was never meant to be, with real regulatory exposure depending on the jurisdiction and what's actually captured.

Rather than either blanket-refusing the analytics need or logging everything verbatim, a reasonable middle path is understanding what the actual analytics goal is and finding a way to serve it with less risk — search query patterns (common terms, zero-result searches, category-level trends) rather than raw verbatim text, or explicit PII-scrubbing/redaction before anything reaches the analytics tool if raw text genuinely is needed for the analysis. This is worth flagging to whoever owns privacy/compliance rather than deciding unilaterally either direction, since the right answer depends on specifics of the regulatory context this particular product operates under.

Say it like this

"Free-text input is an easy-to-overlook PII risk — someone might type their own name or email into a search box for ordinary reasons, and logging that verbatim to a third-party tool can mean sensitive data ends up somewhere it was never meant to be. Rather than refusing outright or logging everything raw, I'd dig into what the actual analytics goal is — often patterns and zero-result queries serve the real need without needing raw text — and if raw text genuinely is required, that needs a redaction step and a real conversation with whoever owns privacy at the company, not a decision I'd make on my own."

DocWhat is Personal Data — GDPR.eu

286●○○○OccasionalDark patterns

A product manager asks you to implement a cancel-subscription flow that's deliberately harder to complete than the sign-up flow. What's the concern with that, beyond it being user-hostile?

Beyond the UX-ethics angle, this is increasingly a genuine regulatory issue, not just a product-philosophy debate — regulators in multiple jurisdictions have specifically targeted "dark patterns" that make cancellation deliberately harder than sign-up (sometimes called the requirement that cancellation be "as easy as" subscribing), with real enforcement actions and fines against companies for exactly this pattern. This has shifted from a purely ethical UX consideration to something with actual legal exposure in a way that's worth knowing, not assuming is only a design-taste argument.

As an engineer being asked to implement this, the reasonable response is raising the concern directly and specifically — naming that this pattern has drawn real regulatory attention and asking whether legal/compliance has weighed in — rather than either silently implementing it or refusing outright without explaining why; the goal is making sure the actual decision-maker has accurate information about the risk, not just an engineer's personal objection.

Say it like this

"Beyond the UX-ethics issue, this has become a genuine regulatory target, not just a design-philosophy debate — several jurisdictions have specifically gone after cancellation flows that are harder than sign-up, with real fines attached. I'd raise that directly and specifically — that this exact pattern has drawn regulatory attention and ask whether legal has actually weighed in — rather than silently building it or refusing without explanation, so the actual decision gets made with accurate information about the real risk, not just my personal objection to it."

DocNegative Option Rule — FTC

Block 24

React anti-patterns & gotchas retrospective

A rundown of classic React mistakes in one place — several individual gotchas already showed up as asides earlier in this bank, but this block asks you to recognize and name the pattern directly, the way a "what's wrong with this code" interview round actually works. Eight questions.

287●●●○Very commonAnti-pattern review

Someone shows you this code and asks what's wrong with it. What do you flag, and why does each one matter?

This kind of "spot the bugs" question is really testing whether several individually-known gotchas are actually internalized well enough to recognize on sight, not explained in isolation when directly asked about each one.

function UserList({ users }) {
  const [selected, setSelected] = useState(null);

  const sorted = users.sort((a, b) => a.name.localeCompare(b.name));

  useEffect(() => {
    fetch(`/api/users/${selected}`).then(r => r.json()).then(setSelected);
  }, [selected]);

  return (
    <ul>
      {sorted.map((u, i) => (
        <li key={i} onClick={() => setSelected(u.id)}>
          {u.name}
        </li>
      ))}
    </ul>
  );
}

users.sort() mutates the array in place and returns the same reference — if users is a prop coming from a parent's state, this silently mutates the parent's data on every render, a real bug that won't show an error but will produce inconsistent behavior elsewhere depending on render order. key={"{"}i{"}"} uses the array index on a list that gets sorted, which breaks identity tracking exactly like the earlier key/reconciliation question describes. And the effect creates an infinite loop: it fetches based on selected, then calls setSelected with the fetch result, which is itself a dependency of the same effect — every fetch response triggers the effect to run again.

Say it like this

"Three things jump out. The sort mutates users in place and returns the same reference, which silently corrupts a prop coming from the parent's state rather than producing a new sorted array. The key is the array index on a list that gets reordered by that sort, which breaks React's identity tracking across renders. And the effect is watching selected and then calling setSelected with its own result, which creates an infinite fetch loop — each response re-triggers the same effect that produced it."

DocUpdating arrays in state — react.dev

288●●○○CommonObject/array dependencies

Why does this effect re-run on every single render, even though options "looks like" it hasn't changed?

This is the reference-equality gotcha applied specifically to dependency arrays. {"{"} sortBy: 'name' {"}"} written inline inside the component body creates a brand-new object every render — identical content, but a different reference each time — and useEffect's dependency comparison is reference-based, so it sees "different" on every render regardless of whether the actual values inside changed.

function List({ items }) {
  useEffect(() => {
    fetchSorted(items, { sortBy: 'name' }); // new object every render
  }, [items, { sortBy: 'name' }]); // always "different" — effect never actually stabilizes
}

The fix is either hoisting the object out of the render entirely if it's genuinely constant (a module-level constant, stable across every render by construction), or memoizing it with useMemo if it needs to depend on other values that do change, or — often the cleanest — depending only on the specific primitive values actually used from inside the object ([items, sortBy] as separate primitive dependencies) rather than the whole object, since primitives compare by value, not reference.

Say it like this

"The object literal in the dependency array is recreated every render with a new reference, and useEffect's comparison is reference-based, so it looks 'different' every time regardless of its actual contents. I'd either hoist it out as a module-level constant if it's truly static, memoize it if it depends on other changing values, or better yet just depend on the specific primitive values used inside it rather than the whole object, since primitives compare by value and don't have this problem at all."

DocRemoving Effect dependencies — react.dev

289●●○○CommonConditional hooks

This pattern shows up a lot from people newer to hooks: an early return before a useEffect call. Why does it break, and what's the fix?

This directly violates the Rules of Hooks covered earlier — an early return before a hook call means that hook is conditionally skipped depending on the component's state, which shifts every subsequent hook's position in React's internal call-order tracking. It typically doesn't even fail cleanly; it produces a confusing runtime error about hooks being called in a different order, or silently misattributes state, well after the actual mistake.

function Profile({ userId }) {
  if (!userId) return <EmptyState />; // early return before a hook — breaks the rule

  useEffect(() => {
    fetchUser(userId);
  }, [userId]);

  return <UserCard userId={userId} />;
}

// fix: hooks unconditional, the condition moves inside
function Profile({ userId }) {
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!userId) return;
    fetchUser(userId);
  }, [userId]);

  if (!userId) return <EmptyState />;
  return <UserCard userId={userId} />;
}
Say it like this

"The early return conditionally skips the hook call depending on whether userId is present, which shifts every hook after it out of alignment with React's call-order tracking — and it usually doesn't fail cleanly, it shows up as a confusing hooks-order error or silently wrong state well after the actual mistake. The fix is always the same shape: keep every hook call unconditional and move the condition inside the hook's body instead, then do the early return after all the hooks have been called."

DocRules of Hooks — react.dev

290●●○○CommonEffects syncing state

This effect syncs a prop into local state so the component can "start" with the prop's value but let the user edit it locally afterward. What's actually wrong with this shape, and what's the right way to do it?

This looks reasonable but has a real timing bug: the effect only runs after the initial render, so on the very first render, localValue is whatever useState's initializer said (often empty or undefined), not yet the prop's value — there's a flash of wrong initial content before the effect catches up. It also means every time the parent's prop value changes for any reason, the effect stomps over whatever local edits the user has already made, which is often not the intended behavior at all.

// buggy: flashes wrong initial value, and clobbers local edits on every prop change
function Editor({ initialText }) {
  const [localValue, setLocalValue] = useState('');
  useEffect(() => { setLocalValue(initialText); }, [initialText]);
  return <textarea value={localValue} onChange={e => setLocalValue(e.target.value)} />;
}

// correct: seed initial state directly, no effect involved at all
function Editor({ initialText }) {
  const [localValue, setLocalValue] = useState(initialText);
  return <textarea value={localValue} onChange={e => setLocalValue(e.target.value)} />;
}
// if it genuinely needs to reset when initialText changes for a DIFFERENT item,
// that's the `key` trick from earlier — key={initialText's owning id} forces a clean remount
Say it like this

"The effect only runs after the first render, so there's a flash where local state is empty before the effect catches up, and every subsequent prop change re-syncs and clobbers whatever the user already typed. The fix is seeding useState directly with the prop as its initializer — no effect at all — and if it genuinely needs to reset when switching to a different item entirely, that's the key-based remount trick, not an effect syncing one piece of state into another."

DocYou Might Not Need an Effect — react.dev

291●○○○OccasionalProp spreading

Why is blindly spreading {"{"}...props{"}"} onto a DOM element considered risky, even though it's convenient?

Spreading an arbitrary props object directly onto a native DOM element passes through whatever the caller happened to include, with no filtering — if a consumer accidentally passes a prop meant for the component's own logic (like an internal isLoading flag) and it wasn't destructured out first, it lands on the DOM element as an unrecognized HTML attribute, which React will warn about and which litters the actual rendered DOM with meaningless attributes.

It also silently allows a caller to override something the component didn't intend to be overridable — if onClick is meant to be handled internally by the component but a caller also spreads their own onClick through ...props, depending on ordering, one silently wins over the other with no warning that a collision even happened. The safer pattern is destructuring the specific known props the component cares about and only spreading a deliberately-scoped rest object, or being explicit about exactly which props are meant to pass through to the underlying element.

Say it like this

"It passes through whatever the caller happened to include with zero filtering — an internal prop that wasn't meant for the DOM at all can leak through as an unrecognized HTML attribute, and it can silently let a caller override something like an internal onClick handler with no warning that a collision even happened. I destructure the specific props a component actually cares about and only spread a deliberately scoped rest object, rather than blindly forwarding everything."

DocForwarding props with spread syntax — react.dev

292●○○○OccasionalInline object styles

Someone writes <div style={"{"}{"{"} padding: 10 {"}"}{"}"} /> with an inline object literal for styling. Setting aside styling architecture preferences from earlier, what's the specific React-performance-adjacent gotcha here?

This is the same inline-object-reference problem from the dependency-array question, applied to props instead: {"{"} padding: 10 {"}"} creates a new object every render, so if this component is wrapped in React.memo, or if this style object gets passed further down as a prop that something else memoizes against, the new reference every render defeats that memoization exactly the same way an inline callback prop does — it's a specific instance of a general pattern, not a separate issue.

For a single leaf DOM element with no memoization anywhere nearby, this specific instance is genuinely harmless — the cost of creating one small object per render is negligible on its own. It's worth flagging as a habit to be aware of specifically because it compounds: the same instinct of "just write an inline object" applied near a memoized boundary is where it actually costs something.

Say it like this

"It's the same inline-reference issue as an inline callback prop, just applied to a style object — a new object every render that can defeat memoization if this sits near a memo boundary. On a single leaf element with nothing memoized nearby, it's genuinely harmless on its own — the real point is recognizing it as an instance of a general pattern, since the same habit applied right next to a memoized component is where it actually starts costing something."

DocMinimizing props changes — react.dev

293●○○○OccasionaluseEffect for derived DOM state

A component uses useEffect to read a DOM element's height after render and store it in state, purely so that height can be displayed as text elsewhere in the same component. Is there a simpler pattern that avoids the extra render this causes?

This works, but it costs an extra render every time: render once without the measurement (since the DOM node doesn't exist until after the first render commits), have the effect run and measure it, call setState with the measurement, then re-render a second time with the actual value now available — a visible, if often brief, two-pass render cycle for something that could sometimes be avoided.

If the measurement genuinely only matters after paint (this is one of the few legitimate uses of the pattern), the extra render is the accepted cost, and useLayoutEffect instead of useEffect at least avoids a visible flash by measuring and updating before the browser paints, folding the correction into the same visual frame rather than a separate one the user can perceive. There's no way to know a DOM node's rendered size before it's actually rendered, so some form of measure-after-render is unavoidable for this specific need — the optimization is in which effect timing you use and whether the extra render is actually visible to the user, not in eliminating the extra render entirely.

Say it like this

"This does cost an extra render — the DOM node doesn't exist to measure until after the first render commits, so there's necessarily a render, a measure, and a second render with the real value. If the measurement only matters post-paint, that two-pass cost is basically unavoidable and accepted, but I'd use useLayoutEffect instead of useEffect specifically so the measurement and correction happen before the browser actually paints, avoiding a visible flash rather than eliminating the extra render itself, which isn't really possible for this specific need."

DocuseLayoutEffect — react.dev

294●○○○OccasionalContext misuse

A codebase has a single giant AppContext holding the current user, theme, feature flags, a shopping cart, and notification state, all in one provider. What's actually wrong with this beyond "it's a lot in one place"?

This is the context re-render problem from earlier, at maximum severity: because useContext subscribes to the whole context value as a single unit, any change to any one of those unrelated pieces of state — the cart updating, a notification arriving — re-renders every single consumer of AppContext anywhere in the app, including components that only ever read the theme and have nothing to do with the cart or notifications at all.

The fix is splitting this into several independent, narrowly-scoped contexts — UserContext, ThemeContext, CartContext, NotificationContext — each with its own provider, so a component only subscribes to (and only re-renders from) the specific slice of state it actually reads, rather than being coupled to every other unrelated piece of state that happens to share the same giant provider purely out of convenience at setup time.

Say it like this

"Because useContext subscribes to the whole value as one unit, any change to any one of those unrelated pieces re-renders every consumer of that single giant context, including components that only care about the theme and have nothing to do with the cart. I'd split it into several narrowly-scoped contexts instead, so a component only subscribes to, and only re-renders from, the specific slice of state it actually reads, rather than being coupled to everything else that happened to get bundled into one provider for setup convenience."

DocScaling up with reducer and context — react.dev

Block 25

Code review conventions for React

A distinct skill from the general "how do you give feedback" behavioral question — this is about what a strong reviewer specifically looks for in a React PR, and how to give feedback that actually lands. Six questions.

295●●●○Very commonReview checklist

Walk through what you actually look for, specifically, reviewing a React component PR — beyond "does it work."

A useful mental checklist, roughly in order of how costly a miss is: correctness of hook dependencies — is the dependency array actually exhaustive and correct, or does it have a stale closure waiting to surprise someone in production; state placement — is this state at the right level, or does it belong lower (colocated) or is it being unnecessarily duplicated as derived state; reference stability where it matters — is a new object/array/function being created inline somewhere that actually defeats a nearby memoization boundary, not everywhere reflexively; accessibility basics — semantic elements used correctly, interactive elements keyboard-operable, meaningful labels; and test coverage matching the actual risk — is there a test for the specific logic or edge case this PR introduces, not just coverage-for-coverage's-sake.

The meta-point worth naming: a strong reviewer isn't running through this list mechanically on every PR — the depth of review should scale with the actual risk and complexity of the change, since exhaustively applying every check to a one-line copy fix wastes everyone's time for no benefit.

Say it like this

"Roughly in order of how costly a miss is: hook dependency correctness, since a stale closure bug is invisible until it isn't; whether state is at the right level or should be colocated or derived instead of duplicated; whether a reference is being recreated somewhere that actually defeats a nearby memoization boundary, not reflexively everywhere; basic accessibility — semantic elements, keyboard operability, real labels; and whether there's a test for the actual new logic or edge case, not just coverage for its own sake. And I scale how deep I go with the actual risk of the change — a one-line copy fix doesn't get the same scrutiny as a new state management pattern."

DocGoogle Engineering Practices — Code Review

296●●○○CommonFeedback framing

What's the difference between "this is wrong" and "consider X" as review comment framings, and does that distinction actually matter or is it just etiquette?

It's not purely etiquette — the framing actually encodes real information about the comment's severity and certainty, and blurring that distinction has a real cost. A comment flagging something that's objectively broken (a genuine bug, a security issue, a clear correctness problem) benefits from being stated directly and unambiguously, because softening a real bug into a suggestion can cause it to be treated as optional and merged anyway. A comment that's a preference or a trade-off (a different but equally valid pattern, a style choice) benefits from being framed as a suggestion, because stating a preference with the same certainty as a bug either misleads the author about how serious it is, or trains them to distrust the reviewer's signal on genuinely serious issues because everything sounds equally urgent.

The practical skill is calibrating the framing to match the actual severity — using unambiguous language for things that must change, and explicitly-optional language for things that are genuinely a matter of taste — rather than using one uniform tone for everything, which either makes real bugs sound skippable or makes minor preferences sound mandatory.

Say it like this

"It's not just etiquette — the framing carries real information about severity. Something genuinely broken should be stated directly, because softening a real bug into a suggestion risks it getting merged as optional. Something that's a legitimate preference or trade-off should be framed as a suggestion, because stating it with bug-level certainty either misleads the author about how serious it is, or trains them to stop trusting my signal when something actually is serious. I try to calibrate the language to the actual severity rather than using one uniform tone for everything."

DocConventional Comments

297●●○○CommonNitpicks vs blockers

How do you decide what actually blocks a merge versus what you leave as an optional comment for the author to consider or ignore?

The genuine blocking bar should be narrow: correctness bugs, security issues, and things that would be genuinely difficult or risky to fix after merging (an API contract that's hard to change once other code depends on it, a data migration that isn't reversible). Style preferences, minor naming disagreements, and "I would have structured this slightly differently but this way is also fine" fall outside that bar and should be explicitly marked as non-blocking — many teams use an explicit convention (a "nit:" prefix, or a tool that supports resolvable-but-non-blocking comments) specifically so the author can tell at a glance which comments actually gate the merge.

Blocking everything, including minor preferences, has a real cost beyond just slowing this one PR down — it trains authors to see every review as an obstacle course rather than genuine collaboration, and it dilutes the signal of an actual blocking comment when it's surrounded by ten non-blocking ones treated with the same weight.

Say it like this

"I keep the genuine blocking bar narrow — correctness bugs, security issues, and things that are genuinely hard or risky to fix after merging. Style preferences and 'I'd have done this slightly differently but this is also fine' get marked explicitly non-blocking, usually with a nit prefix, so the author can immediately tell what actually gates the merge versus what's optional. Blocking on everything trains people to see review as an obstacle course and dilutes the signal when a real blocker is surrounded by ten minor ones treated the same way."

DocWhat to look for in a code review — Google

298●○○○OccasionalLarge PRs

You're handed a 2,000-line PR touching a dozen components. How do you actually review it well, rather than skimming and approving?

A genuinely thorough line-by-line review of something this large in one pass is close to impossible to sustain — attention degrades well before line 2,000, and the honest failure mode of a huge PR is a reviewer skimming the diff view and approving based on "it looks fine" rather than actually reasoning through the logic, which defeats the point of review entirely.

The better move, if the PR genuinely can't be split (worth asking for directly if it plausibly could be — several logically independent changes bundled into one PR is itself something worth pushing back on before reviewing starts), is reviewing in deliberate passes rather than one linear scroll: first pass for overall structure and approach, second pass focused specifically on the highest-risk files (new business logic, anything touching auth or payments), and being honest about which parts got a genuinely careful read versus a lighter pass, rather than implicitly certifying uniform scrutiny across all two thousand lines when that didn't actually happen.

Say it like this

"A genuinely careful line-by-line pass across two thousand lines isn't realistically sustainable, and the honest failure mode is skimming and approving on 'looks fine,' which defeats the point. If it genuinely can't be split — and I'd ask directly whether it can, since several independent changes bundled together is itself worth pushing back on — I review in deliberate passes: structure first, then a focused, careful pass on the highest-risk parts specifically. And I'm honest about which parts actually got careful scrutiny versus a lighter pass, rather than implicitly certifying uniform depth that didn't happen."

DocSmall CLs — Google Engineering Practices

299●○○○OccasionalDisagreement in review

A reviewer leaves a comment on your PR that you genuinely disagree with. What do you actually do?

The instinct to avoid is either silently complying with something you think is wrong just to get the PR merged faster, or dismissing the comment without engaging with it — both skip the actual point of review, which is genuinely evaluating the concern, not just resolving the thread. The better move is engaging with the specific reasoning: asking why they see it as a problem if it's not obvious, or explaining your own reasoning clearly if you believe your approach is correct, so the conversation is actually about the technical merits rather than who yields first.

If after real back-and-forth the disagreement is still unresolved and it's a genuine judgment call rather than a clear-cut correctness issue, escalating to a third opinion (a tech lead, a quick synchronous conversation) is more productive than an extended asynchronous back-and-forth in comment threads, which tends to degrade rather than converge past a certain point — and once resolved either way, actually committing to the decision rather than relitigating it in the next PR.

Say it like this

"I'd engage with the actual reasoning rather than either silently complying to get it merged or just dismissing the comment — asking why they see it as a problem, or explaining my own reasoning clearly if I think my approach is right, so it's actually about the technical merits. If real back-and-forth doesn't resolve it and it's a genuine judgment call, I'd rather get a third opinion or have a quick synchronous conversation than let an async comment thread drag on, since those tend to degrade rather than converge past a certain point. And once it's resolved either way, I actually commit to it instead of relitigating it later."

DocHandling Pushback — Google Engineering Practices

300●○○○OccasionalAutomating review

What kinds of things should be caught by automated checks (linting, CI, an AI review bot) rather than a human reviewer's attention, and why does that division matter?

Anything mechanically detectable and objectively defined — formatting, a missing hook dependency, an unused import, a straightforward accessibility rule violation, a failing test — should be caught automatically, because a human spending attention re-verifying something a tool can check deterministically and instantly is wasted human judgment on a problem that doesn't need judgment at all. This isn't just efficiency for its own sake: every mechanical issue a human has to manually flag in review is also attention not spent on the things that genuinely need human judgment — architectural fit, whether the actual approach makes sense for the problem, subtle logic errors a linter has no way to detect.

The practical implication is investing in automating the mechanical layer specifically so human review time concentrates on what only a human can actually evaluate — a team that relies on humans to manually catch formatting inconsistencies in every PR is spending scarce review attention on the wrong layer of the problem.

Say it like this

"Anything mechanically detectable and objectively defined — formatting, a missing dependency, an unused import, a failing test — should be automated, because a human re-verifying something a tool can check deterministically is judgment spent on a problem that doesn't need judgment. It's not just about efficiency — every mechanical issue a human has to manually catch is attention not spent on what actually needs a human, like whether the overall approach makes sense. I'd rather invest in automating that mechanical layer so review time concentrates on the things only a person can actually evaluate."

DocWhat to look for in a code review — Google

Block 26

Onboarding & documentation practices

The unglamorous work that determines whether a codebase is actually maintainable by someone other than the person who wrote it. Six questions.

301●●○○CommonStorybook

What does Storybook actually give a team beyond "a place to look at components in isolation"?

Rendering a component in isolation, outside the full app, is the visible surface, but the deeper value is in what that isolation enables: developing and testing a component's various states (loading, error, empty, different prop combinations) without needing to manufacture the real app conditions that would normally produce each state — no need to actually trigger a real network failure to see and iterate on the error state's design.
