Then it waits — a new version doesn't take over while an old one is still controlling any open tab, deliberately, since a service worker intercepts requests and swapping versions mid-session could cause inconsistent behavior within one page load. Activate only happens once nothing's using the old version anymore. That waiting phase is exactly why 'I deployed a fix but still see the old version' is such a common confusion, and skipWaiting is the explicit opt-out that forces immediate activation, trading a small risk of mid-session inconsistency for users getting the update without closing the tab."

DocThe Service Worker Lifecycle — web.dev

337●●●○Very commonCache strategies

What's the difference between "cache-first" and "stale-while-revalidate" as service worker caching strategies, and when would you pick one over the other for a given type of resource?

Cache-first checks the cache before ever hitting the network; if a cached response exists, it's returned immediately with no network request at all, and the network is only consulted if the cache is empty for that resource. This is ideal for resources that genuinely never change once deployed — a versioned/hashed JS or CSS bundle filename, where a cache hit is always correct by construction, since a content change would produce a different filename entirely.

Stale-while-revalidate returns the cached response immediately (fast, same as cache-first for the response the user sees right now), but simultaneously fires a network request in the background to fetch a fresh version and updates the cache with it for next time — the user gets an instant response now, potentially slightly stale, while the cache silently catches up for the following request. This fits resources that change occasionally but where showing slightly-stale content briefly is an acceptable trade-off for speed — an API response for content that updates periodically, or an HTML page itself. The decision between them essentially comes down to whether staleness is ever acceptable for that specific resource: truly immutable assets get cache-first, anything that can go stale but benefits from feeling instant gets stale-while-revalidate, and anything that must always be fresh (a genuinely real-time value) shouldn't be cached this way at all.

Say it like this

"Cache-first returns the cached response with no network call if one exists, only hitting the network on a cache miss — ideal for something genuinely immutable, like a content-hashed JS bundle, where a cache hit is always correct by construction. Stale-while-revalidate returns the cached response immediately too, but fires a background request to refresh the cache for next time, so the user gets an instant but potentially slightly stale response now, and the cache catches up after. I'd pick cache-first for truly immutable assets and stale-while-revalidate for anything that changes occasionally but where a brief staleness is an acceptable trade for feeling instant."

DocStale-while-revalidate — web.dev

338●○○○OccasionalService worker scope

What determines which pages a given service worker actually controls, and why does the file's location in the URL path matter?

A service worker's scope — the set of URLs it's allowed to control — defaults to the directory the service worker script itself is served from, and everything below it in the URL path. A service worker registered at /app/sw.js can, by default, only control pages under /app/, not the entire origin including /other-section/, unless the scope is explicitly widened via the Service-Worker-Allowed response header, and even then it can never exceed the origin itself — a service worker can never control a different domain, only paths within the same origin it was served from.

This is exactly why a service worker meant to control an entire site typically needs to be served from the root (/sw.js), not nested in a subdirectory — a service worker placed at /static/js/sw.js would, by default scope rules, only ever be able to control pages under /static/js/, which is almost certainly not what was intended, and is a genuinely common configuration mistake when a service worker file ends up bundled and served from wherever the build tool happens to output static assets rather than deliberately placed at the root.

Say it like this

"Scope defaults to the directory the service worker script is served from and everything below it — a worker at /app/sw.js can only control pages under /app/ by default, not the whole origin, unless the scope is explicitly widened with a response header, and it can never cross origins regardless. That's exactly why a site-wide service worker needs to be served from the root rather than nested in a build tool's asset subdirectory — if it ends up at something like /static/js/sw.js by default bundler output, it'll only ever control that narrow path, which is a genuinely common misconfiguration."

DocServiceWorkerRegistration.scope — MDN

339●○○○OccasionalBackground sync

A user submits a form while offline. What does the Background Sync API let a service worker do that the app couldn't otherwise, and what are its actual limitations?

Without Background Sync, a failed request while offline is just gone unless the app itself has some retry mechanism running — and that mechanism only works while the tab is actually open, since regular JavaScript execution stops when a tab is closed or the browser is shut down. Background Sync lets a service worker register a sync event that the browser will fire later, once connectivity is actually restored, even if the tab that originally triggered the request has since been closed — the service worker itself can keep running briefly in the background specifically to retry that queued action, independent of any page being open at all.

The real limitation worth knowing: browser support has historically been inconsistent — it's well-supported in Chromium-based browsers but was, for a long time, notably absent in Safari, which meaningfully limits how much a team can rely on it as the sole mechanism for offline reliability across all users rather than as an enhancement layered on top of the local-first storage and manual sync-queue pattern covered in the earlier offline-support system design question. In practice, most robust offline implementations use Background Sync as a nice-to-have acceleration where supported, with the actual reliability guarantee still coming from the local-first persistence and retry-on-next-app-open pattern that works everywhere regardless of this specific API's support.

Say it like this

"Without it, a failed offline request is just gone unless the app has its own retry running, and that only works while the tab's actually open. Background Sync lets the service worker register a sync event the browser fires once connectivity returns, even if the tab that made the original request is already closed — the service worker can run briefly in the background just to retry it. The real caveat is browser support has historically been inconsistent, notably missing in Safari for a long time, so I wouldn't rely on it as the sole offline mechanism — it's a nice acceleration layered on top of the local-first storage and manual sync-queue pattern that actually works everywhere regardless of this specific API's support."

DocBackground Synchronization API — MDN

340●○○○OccasionalService worker security

Why does the browser require HTTPS to register a service worker (with an exception for localhost), and what's the actual risk it's protecting against?

A service worker is granted significant power over an origin: it can intercept, modify, or completely fabricate responses to every network request the pages it controls make, persist across browser sessions once registered, and run even when no page from that origin is open. If a service worker could be registered over plain HTTP, a network-level attacker performing a man-in-the-middle attack could inject a malicious service worker into the response — and unlike a single compromised page load, an injected malicious service worker would persist and keep intercepting requests indefinitely, long after the specific MITM attack window closed, giving an attacker a much more durable foothold than any other single-request injection could.

The HTTPS requirement closes that specific attack vector: it guarantees the service worker script itself was delivered over an encrypted, tamper-resistant connection, so a network attacker can't inject or modify it in transit, given how much lasting power the browser is handing that script. The localhost exception exists purely for local development convenience, where the threat model of a network-level MITM attack on your own machine's loopback interface is meaningfully different from a real network in production.

Say it like this

"A service worker has real power — it can intercept and modify every request the pages it controls make, and it persists across sessions even when no page is open. If it could be registered over plain HTTP, a network attacker doing a man-in-the-middle attack could inject a malicious service worker that keeps running and intercepting requests indefinitely, long after that specific attack window closed — a much more durable foothold than a single injected response. HTTPS guarantees the worker script itself was delivered over an encrypted, tamper-resistant connection, which closes that vector. The localhost exception is just for local dev, where that specific network threat model doesn't really apply."

DocService Worker Registration — web.dev

Block 31

Browser storage

Beyond the localStorage-vs-cookie question already covered for auth tokens — the fuller picture of what the browser actually offers for persisting data, and which tool fits which job. Six questions.

341●●●○Very commonsessionStorage vs localStorage

What's the actual difference in lifetime and scope between sessionStorage and localStorage, beyond "one is temporary"?

localStorage persists indefinitely — until explicitly cleared by the app or the user, with no automatic expiry at all — and is shared across every tab and window for the same origin, so a value written in one tab is immediately visible to another tab of the same site. sessionStorage is scoped to a single tab, not just a "session" in the vague sense: it's cleared when that specific tab closes, and critically, it's not shared even between two tabs of the same site open at the same time — opening the same URL in a new tab starts with empty sessionStorage, not a copy of the other tab's data.

The practical decision: use localStorage for anything that should genuinely persist and be consistent across the user's tabs (a saved draft, a user preference), and sessionStorage specifically for data that should be scoped to one particular browsing session/tab and shouldn't leak into or be shared with a separate tab — a multi-step wizard's in-progress state where having two tabs of the same wizard silently share state would actually be confusing, or a value that's deliberately meant to reset if the user opens the flow in a fresh tab.

Say it like this

"localStorage persists indefinitely with no automatic expiry and is shared across every tab of the same origin. sessionStorage is scoped to one specific tab, not just a vague session — it clears when that tab closes, and it's genuinely not shared even with another tab of the same site open at the same time. I'd use localStorage for anything that should be consistent across tabs, like a saved preference, and sessionStorage specifically when I want state scoped to one tab and don't want two tabs silently sharing it — a multi-step flow where that sharing would actually be confusing."

DocWindow.sessionStorage — MDN

342●●○○CommonIndexedDB

Why would you reach for IndexedDB instead of localStorage for a data-heavy client-side feature, given that both persist across sessions?

localStorage has real, practical limits that make it unsuitable beyond simple key-value data: its API is fully synchronous, meaning a read or write blocks the main thread while it happens, which becomes a genuine performance problem as stored data grows; it only stores strings, so anything structured needs manual JSON.stringify/parse on every access; it has no querying capability at all beyond looking up an exact key; and it has a small storage quota (typically around 5-10MB depending on the browser) that a data-heavy feature can hit quickly.

IndexedDB is a genuine transactional database built into the browser: it's fully asynchronous so it never blocks the main thread, stores structured JavaScript objects natively (no manual serialization needed for common types), supports indexes and actual querying beyond exact-key lookup, and has a dramatically larger storage quota (often hundreds of MB or more, varying by browser and available disk space). The trade-off is a genuinely more complex, more verbose API — it's low-level enough that most teams reach for a wrapper library (Dexie.js, idb) rather than using the raw IndexedDB API directly, but for anything beyond simple key-value persistence — an offline-capable app's local data store, a large cached dataset, the note-taking offline example from earlier — it's the right tool where localStorage genuinely isn't.

Say it like this

"localStorage is synchronous, so reads and writes block the main thread as data grows, it only stores strings so everything structured needs manual serialization, it has no real querying, and the quota is small, typically around 5 to 10MB. IndexedDB is a real async transactional database — it never blocks the main thread, stores structured objects natively, supports actual querying, and has a much larger quota. The API's genuinely more complex, so I'd usually reach for a wrapper like Dexie rather than raw IndexedDB, but for anything beyond simple key-value data — an offline-capable app's real local store — it's the right tool where localStorage just isn't built for the job."

DocIndexedDB API — MDN

343●●○○CommonCache API

How is the Cache API (used by service workers) conceptually different from IndexedDB or localStorage, given all three "store data in the browser"?

The Cache API is purpose-built specifically for storing Request/Response pairs — it's not a general-purpose data store for arbitrary JavaScript values, it's explicitly modeled around HTTP: you store a Request (effectively a URL plus method/headers) as the key and a full Response object (status, headers, body) as the value, which maps directly onto its actual purpose of intercepting a network request and serving a previously-cached response instead, exactly the mechanism behind the cache strategies from the service worker question earlier.

IndexedDB and localStorage store arbitrary application data — user preferences, structured records, anything the app defines the shape of. The Cache API specifically stores HTTP exchanges — it's the tool for "when this URL is requested, here's the response to serve," not for "remember this user's settings." A production offline-capable app typically uses both together for their genuinely different purposes: the Cache API for caching network responses (assets, API responses) that a service worker intercepts and serves, and IndexedDB for the app's actual structured data model that needs real querying and persistence beyond just "the last response for this URL."

Say it like this

"The Cache API is specifically for Request and Response pairs — it's modeled directly around HTTP, storing a request as the key and a full response as the value, which is exactly what a service worker needs to intercept a request and serve a cached response instead. IndexedDB and localStorage store arbitrary app data with a shape the app defines. A real offline app typically uses both for their different purposes — Cache API for caching actual network responses that a service worker serves, IndexedDB for the app's real structured data that needs querying beyond just the last response for a URL."

DocCache — MDN

344●○○○OccasionalStorage quotas & eviction

A user's browser is running low on disk space. Can the browser silently delete data your app stored in IndexedDB, and what does that mean for how you should design around it?

Yes, under real storage pressure — browsers implement storage eviction policies where, if the device is genuinely low on disk space, the browser can clear an origin's stored data (IndexedDB, Cache API storage, and in some cases localStorage) without the app being notified in advance or the user necessarily realizing it happened, typically evicting the least-recently-used origins first under a "best-effort" storage model that's the default unless an app has requested (and been granted) persistent storage.

The practical implication: client-side storage should generally be treated as a cache or a convenience layer, not as the sole, guaranteed source of truth for anything the user would be genuinely upset to lose — critical data should still be synced to a server whenever connectivity allows, with local storage as an offline/performance optimization on top of that, not a replacement for it. For genuinely important cases (an offline-first app where local data really is authoritative for a period), the navigator.storage.persist() API lets an app request persistent storage that's exempt from this best-effort eviction, though the browser can still deny the request or apply its own criteria for granting it, so even that isn't an absolute guarantee.

Say it like this

"Yes — under real disk pressure, browsers can evict an origin's storage without warning, typically least-recently-used first, under a best-effort model that's the default. So I'd treat client-side storage as a cache or convenience layer, not the sole source of truth for anything the user would be upset to lose — critical data should still sync to a server whenever possible, with local storage as an offline or performance layer on top, not a replacement. For cases where local data genuinely needs to be more durable, there's a persistent storage API you can request, though even that isn't an absolute guarantee, since the browser can still apply its own criteria for granting it."

DocPersistent storage — web.dev

345●○○○OccasionalStorage security

Is data stored in localStorage or IndexedDB encrypted at rest, and does that matter for what you'd feel comfortable storing there?

No — by default, data in localStorage, sessionStorage, and IndexedDB is stored as plain, unencrypted data on disk (the browser's own storage files), readable by anything with file-system access to that user's device and browser profile — it's not exposed to other websites (same-origin policy still applies at the JavaScript API level), but it's not protected against something with direct device or file-system access, like malware already running on the machine, or someone with physical access to an unlocked, unencrypted device.

This matters for what's reasonable to store: it reinforces the same principle from the token-storage discussion — anything genuinely sensitive shouldn't be sitting in client-side storage in plaintext regardless of which specific storage mechanism, since none of them provide encryption at rest as a built-in guarantee. If a feature genuinely needs to store something sensitive client-side (rare, and worth question first, but it happens — an offline-capable app caching some user data locally), the app itself would need to handle encryption before writing to storage, using the Web Crypto API, rather than relying on any assumption that the storage mechanism itself protects the data.

Say it like this

"No, none of them encrypt at rest by default — it's plain data in the browser's own storage files, not exposed to other websites through the same-origin policy, but not protected against something with actual file-system or device access, like malware already on the machine. That reinforces the same principle as token storage — nothing genuinely sensitive should sit in client-side storage in plaintext regardless of which mechanism, since none of them guarantee encryption at rest. If something sensitive genuinely needs local storage, the app has to handle encryption itself with the Web Crypto API before writing it, not assume the storage layer protects it."

DocWeb Crypto API — MDN

346●○○○OccasionalChoosing a storage layer

Given a specific need — a small user preference, a shopping cart, an offline note-taking data store, and cached API responses for a service worker — which storage mechanism fits each, and why?

A quick decision map, following the properties covered in this block: a small user preference (theme choice, sidebar collapsed state) fits localStorage — small, simple, synchronous access is fine at that size, persists across tabs and sessions as expected for a preference. A shopping cart that should genuinely be consistent across the user's tabs but doesn't need complex querying also fits localStorage reasonably well for a simple cart, though a more complex cart with many items and richer querying needs might push toward IndexedDB.

An offline note-taking app's actual data — potentially many records, needing real querying (search, filter by date), needing to not block the main thread as it grows — fits IndexedDB, which is exactly the local-first storage layer from the earlier offline-support system design question. Cached API responses and assets that a service worker intercepts and serves fit the Cache API specifically, since that's the tool actually designed around Request/Response pairs rather than arbitrary data. The general pattern: reach for the simplest tool that actually fits the data's shape and access pattern, rather than defaulting to one storage mechanism for everything regardless of fit.

Say it like this

"A small preference like theme choice fits localStorage — simple, synchronous is fine at that size, persists across tabs as expected. A simple shopping cart also fits localStorage reasonably well; a more complex one with real querying needs might push toward IndexedDB. An offline note-taking app's actual data — potentially many records needing real search and filtering without blocking the main thread — is IndexedDB territory, the same local-first layer from the offline-support design question earlier. Cached network responses for a service worker are specifically Cache API, since that's built around Request and Response pairs rather than arbitrary data. Generally I'd reach for the simplest tool that actually fits the data's shape, not default to one mechanism for everything."

DocStorage for the web — web.dev
